- **Question:** An invitation is organizations'; the grants it offers (officer hats, existing allowances, a new allowance) are one Board proposal. Which concept owns the operation's flow and the proposal of the grants?
- **Chosen:** Budgets: the flow creates the invitation through organizations' rpc, then budgets' rpc plans every grant (officer hat mints from the deployment's hat ids; an allowance's hat, and for a new one `Hats.createHat` and `Budgets.createChildBudget` with its budgets row), opens one proposal (`board.propose`) and binds it to the invitation (`organizations.bind-invitation-grants`). Budgets also proposes taking the hats back when an offer ends with them on chain.
- **Why:** Revised in the 8 November adversarial review. The frozen text offers 'existing spending allowances, a new spending allowance or Manage allowances with the invitation', and the designed call is one Board batch with 'the allowance's `Hats.createHat`, `Budgets.createChildBudget` and `Hats.mintHat`'. Planning an allowance (its parent, its hat, CHILDREN_EXCEED_PARENT, its budgets row) is budgets' alone, and board sits below budgets, so board's flow could not compose it; the budgets designer had to narrow the frozen offer to existing allowances (budgets.invitation-allowance-offers), which P10 rung 1 does not allow. Budgets is the lowest concept that depends on organizations, board and its own allowances, and it already opens Board proposals for allowances (`org allowance assign`). Officer hat mints are two Hats calls built from hat ids organizations already holds. Board keeps `org member grant` and `org member revoke`; an invitation's grants, and taking them back, have one owner. The request key makes a repeat return the same invitation and the same proposal.

### operations.owners: Each frozen operation has one owning concept; an area spreads over several

- **Question:** Which concept designs each of the 215 frozen operations?
- **Chosen:** Each area maps to a concept; an operation whose behaviour belongs to another concept goes there (OPERATION_OWNERS).
- **Why:** Areas are how people find commands, not concepts (the operation record says so). 134 operations sit in their area's primary concept (AREA_CONCEPTS) and 81 move with their behaviour: agents, session and local commands out of accounts; `doctor` and keys to applications; verification and `contact bank add` to banking; invitations and members to organizations, `invite accept` to banking, allowances and `org member invite` to budgets and votes to equity out of governance; requests, services, the inbox, contacts, documents, `address` and `balance` out of payments, with `contact get` to banking; `activity annotate` to books; `bank match` to requests; `org department pay` to payments; `dev fund` to accounts, `dev key` to applications. The map is typed against every frozen id, so none can be missed, and every concept but media, parties, indexing, approvals and notices owns at least one operation. The rule behind each exception: an operation's flow sits in a concept that depends on every concept whose rpcs it composes (operations.sign-in-in-accounts, operations.member-invite-in-budgets, operations.invite-accept-in-banking, operations.contact-get-in-banking); every devnet-only operation is devnet's (operations.dev-mail-notices-in-devnet).

### operations.sign-in-in-accounts: `auth login` and `auth verify` are accounts' flows over access' rpcs

- **Question:** Both commands end by reporting whether the account is set up, which is accounts'. Which concept owns their flows?
- **Chosen:** Accounts: the flow sends and checks the code through access' rpcs, then reads the Safe's setup state.
- **Why:** 'Like auth login, it returns the setup link if your account is not ready yet' is frozen surface (P10 rung 1). A flow may compose only rpcs of concepts its own concept depends on, and access depends on nothing, so the flow sits in accounts, which is the person's way in. Sessions, codes, the send cooldown and an agent's key challenge stay access' rpcs.

### procedures.accounts-admit: One authority procedure: an invitation admits its invitee to the beta

- **Question:** `org member invite` 'admits the invitee to the beta with no second welcome email'. Admission is accounts', the invitation organizations', and accounts sits below organizations. How is the admission written?
- **Chosen:** Organizations' invitation rpc invokes `accounts.admit` in its own transaction.
- **Why:** P4 sends a lagging change to the outbox only when the consumer can depend on the producer; here it cannot, and a flow step cannot do it either, because accounts could not check that the caller really invited that email. An invitation and the admission it carries must never disagree at a commit: an invitee who got the invitation but is still waiting cannot take the Seat. So it is one fact, of authority rather than money, and the one procedure that is not a money fact; its `about` begins `Authority fact:`. Admission stays in accounts with referrals, because a spent invite and the friend it admits are one write there. The beta gate is checked only above accounts (setting up the Safe, the faucet, `org create`, `referral invite`); the old hold at profile onboarding (old profiles, now parties, below accounts) moves to the Safe's setup, which already refuses a waiting person. Aaron may rule this against P4's narrow reading.

### responsibilities.destinations: Receiving destinations stay in parties; the Solana cleanup is dropped

- **Question:** Old parties' destinations included internal report and remove operations for unused Solana wallets. Keep them?
- **Chosen:** Destinations stay with the Party; the Solana report and removal are dropped.
- **Why:** The two internal operations clean up old rows on a network the target does not serve; nothing is migrated (P9) and no frozen operation calls them (P2).

### responsibilities.keepers: 'Manage books' is the Books officer hat

- **Question:** Old books listed its keepers (who holds Manage books). What is that on v4?
- **Chosen:** The wearers of the Books hat: board grants and revokes it, books reads its wearers from indexing.
- **Why:** On v4 every officer permission is a hat granted by a Board batch (CAP-1101), and Capxul reads it only from Hats; a table would copy the chain.

### responsibilities.landings: Every old concept area lands in a target concept, or is dropped by a decision

- **Question:** How is it shown that every responsibility of the 17 old concepts lands somewhere?
- **Chosen:** OLD_AREAS in shared/landings.ts: each of the old concepts' area folders, the target concepts it lands in, and the decision that put it there.
- **Why:** A concept's `about` says what it absorbs, but nothing checks that the old list is covered. The map lists all 91 area folders under packages/backend/src/<concept>/, plus the root files of documents and parties, and every entry names a decision record, so a dropped responsibility is a judgment a reviewer can find. The map is not a record: the build follows its decisions' `affects`.

### responsibilities.operator-proof-tools: Old operator's proof identities, fixtures and receipt hold are dropped

- **Question:** Old operator peeked at sign-in codes and Safes, seeded identities and fixtures, purged accounts and held receipts for acceptance runs. Port them?
- **Chosen:** Drop them: eval worlds are built by running operations as the devnet's fixed identities and saved as snapshots; codes are read from the mail sink; a held receipt is the bundler fake's fault switch.
- **Why:** They wrote other concepts' rows from outside (the as-is book's write-boundary findings), which no target rule allows. A world made by real operations is the black-box baseline spec 30 asks for, and the supervisor restores it in seconds. No frozen operation reaches them.

### responsibilities.overlays: Old activity's overlays are dropped

- **Question:** Old activity added Convex overlays to indexed chain rows. Keep them?
- **Chosen:** Drop: the feed reads Ponder's views and payments' rows in one Postgres, so there is nothing to overlay.
- **Why:** The overlay joined two stores (Convex rows on top of the indexer's answer). With one Postgres the join is a query.

### responsibilities.repairs: Old applications' repairs are dropped

- **Question:** Old applications had operator-only repair mutations run by hand with `convex run`. Port them?
- **Chosen:** Drop: no frozen operation reaches them (P2); a data repair is a migration, not an rpc.
- **Why:** They mended rows the old shapes let go wrong (disposable proofs). Nothing is migrated from old staging (P9), and the target's generated migrations and tenancy evals cover what they guarded.

### responsibilities.roles: Old organizations' invitable roles list is dropped

- **Question:** Old organizations listed 'the roles a member can be invited into'. What is that on v4?
- **Chosen:** Drop: on v4 an invitation offers the three officer hats and the Organization's allowances, which budgets' invite flow reads and `--preview` shows.
- **Why:** The roles were pre-v4 Zodiac Roles shapes (P9). No frozen operation calls the read.

### responsibilities.service-lanes: Old accounts' service-key lanes become the service signer

- **Question:** Old accounts kept the Companies House key's sender lane (one send in flight). Where does that go?
- **Chosen:** Into the service-signer integration: one send in flight per key, signed bytes recorded before broadcast.
- **Why:** Every service key (claim, tier, Budgets executor, escrow relayer, faucet) needs the same rule, and only one of them is accounts'.

### responsibilities.summaries: Old accounts' summaries: deposit instructions to activity, the rest by surface

- **Question:** Old `accounts.summaries` served `home`, `me`, the 'needs you' list and deposit instructions. Where do they go?
- **Chosen:** Deposit instructions become activity's `address`; `home`, `me` and 'needs you' come back only as flows for a named surface (P2).
- **Why:** `address` is the only frozen operation they back (`client.account.profile.depositInstructions`), and it reads for whoever you act as, which is activity's. `home` composed treasuries, payroll runs and activity items in one action; no frozen operation calls it, so P2 deletes it until the web app names it, and then it is a react-surface flow over activity's, approvals' and requests' reads.

### rules.admissions-and-wait-keys: Row admissions and wait keys are fields, not prose

- **Question:** The build session's generators (B1) make each table's row-level policy and the runtime's map from a waking event to the instance waiting for it. Both lived only in prose: a table's scope or about said that a payee, an addressed payer, a holder by their Safe or anyone also reads it (payments, requests, documents, media, agents, banking, equity, payroll and every chain view), and a wait's about named its key (rules.process-wakeups). Give them fields, and how does a missing one show?
- **Chosen:** TableSpec gains `admits`: one entry per policy clause, with how the session is matched (`tenant`; `safe`, through parties' directory; `own-safe`; `email`; `anyone`), the column or a one-hop join (`via` a table and its column), the rows (`where`), and whether it reads and writes. A wait gains `key`: one entry per way it is woken, its carriers (a product event, a chain event, a table's row, a signal rpc or the instance's own call) and the properties they carry (`reaches` for Ponder's progress passing a block). Both optional. Rules `admission` and `wait-key` check them wherever present; in a target book `chain-admits` and `wait-without-key` require them; the gap `admission-in-prose` shows a target table whose prose admits someone with no `admits`. Each clause and each key yields an eval: `tbl:<table>/admits/<n>`, `wfl:<workflow>/wake/<wait>`.
- **Why:** A policy the generator cannot see refuses a legitimate reader, or, written by hand, admits too much; a route written by hand is the reactor rules.process-wakeups rejected. As fields both are checked on every build and each becomes an eval, so B2 proves every clause and B0 the routing. A wait without a key is found by structure alone and leaves the generator nothing to emit, so in a target book it is a problem; a timer is the instance's own and takes none. Admission prose is found only by reading words (ADMISSION_PROSE in packages/book/src/build.ts), which can be wrong either way, so it is a gap: visible, never why the book fails to build. A chain table's scope always is its policy, so its admissions are a rule. The matchers are the book's own: `safe` is P8's chain-read scope (indexing.scope-by-parties), which requests.owner-tenancy, payroll.payee-reads and equity.holder-reads name; `own-safe` is documents.tenancy's principal's own Safe or a Seat's company Safe, and an agent's own row (agents.who-acts). `writes` is what each concept's decision grants and no more: requests' pay and decline and equity's holder writes are their rpcs' work, not a policy's. A key reaches several instances only where the design means it: every approval on one nonce, every Payment of one command, every instance whose block Ponder's progress passed. A cross-tenant index stays one named query, not a clause (organizations' offers, accounts' referee). Review tightened both rules: a clause must read or write, `anyone` never writes, and a `where` value must be one its column can hold, or the clause admits no row; a signal routes by its instance's own columns only when it reads or writes the instance's table, the row it resolves them from. Each clause's eval allows for the tenant and the other clauses.

### rules.counterparty-reads: A counterparty reads company rows through admissions, never elevated

- **Question:** Some rpcs serve a counterparty who holds no Seat: an invited investor (equity.investments.*), a shareholder voting (equity.votes.get), a grantee, a payee. They read company rows beyond their own: fundraise-rounds, votes, equity-installs and the organization's public fields. Row-level security (P8) admits only the company's tenant. How do these reads pass it?
- **Chosen:** Each such table gets a read-only admission joined from the counterparty's own row (the investment, the grant, the share holding, the agreement) to the company rows it may see, so the database enforces it. The build session adds these clauses at B1, book first, before generating the policies; the admission-in-prose gap and the tenancy evals show any it misses.
- **Why:** P8 puts tenancy in the database so that no handler's mistake can leak another tenant's rows; an elevated read moves that guarantee back into each handler, exactly where the old system leaked. A clause joined from the counterparty's own row admits only what that relationship already entitles them to, and each clause is an eval (tbl:<t>/admits/<n>). Found by the adversarial review of the admissions, which listed fundraise-rounds, votes, equity-installs and organizations; the review did not finish the list, so B1 starts from the rpcs whose `who` is a counterparty.

### rules.durable-processes: Which objects are long-lived and get one durable process each

- **Question:** A3 gives each long-lived business object one durable process. Which objects are they?
- **Chosen:** An approval, a Payment (one process for its command and its date: payments.one-process-per-payment), a Board proposal, an Organization's setup, a personal Safe's setup, an agreement, a request, an invitation, a bank payout, a verification, an agent change and a round (equity). Added by their designers: an assistant grant (access.assistant-grant-process), an identity document (media.document-process), a budget change (budgets.one-process-per-change), and an equity install, grant, sale and vote (equity.processes).
- **Why:** Each waits on something outside a request (a signature, the chain, a date, a person, Bridge) and must survive a crash: P3's test holds for each, and A3 says the process owns its machine. Short-lived rows (a contact, a book account, an annotation) change only inside rpcs and have no process. The concept designer may add objects, never drop one of these without a decision. The list was brought up to date with the designs on 8 November 2026: payments folded the payment command's and the dated payment's processes into one per Payment, with its own decision, and four designers added objects that wait on a person, the chain or a date.

### rules.frozen-flags-boolean-forms: The old CLI's boolean spellings are recorded once in flags.ts

- **Question:** Effect 4.0.0, which the old CLI pinned, parses any boolean flag in four spellings: `--x`, `--x false`, `--x=no` and `--no-x`, where a following word counts only when it is true, yes, on, 1, y, false, no, off, 0 or n. No CLI source declares them, and the printed help shows none. The old CLI's tests use them (`--json false`, `--json=no`, `--no-json`), and runtime/format.ts reads `--no-json` itself. Does books/frozen/flags.ts record them, and where?
- **Chosen:** Recorded once as `booleanForms` in books/frozen/flags.ts beside the global flags. The generator stops if the old test that uses them is gone. Each boolean flag keeps `takesValue: false`: it needs no value.
- **Why:** flags.ts is the reference the target designs its flags from, not surface to keep (system.frozen-behaviour). As reference it must describe the old grammar truly, and without this a boolean flag reads as taking no value, though the old CLI's own tests pass it one. The forms are the same for every boolean flag, so once is exact. The target decides its own spellings; Effect 4.1.2 gives the same four by default. One edge for its designer: a word after a boolean flag that is one of those words is taken as its value, so the old `org director threshold --confirm 1` reads 1 as --confirm's value, not as <n>.

### rules.frozen-flags-built-ins: Effect's built-in global flags are recorded as the old CLI printed them

- **Question:** --help (-h), --version (-v), --wizard, --completions and --log-level come from the Effect library, not from the old CLI's source. Are they frozen, and in whose words?
- **Chosen:** Recorded, as the newest recorded `capxul schema … --json` result printed them (apps/docs/factory/proofs): name, alias, printed type, required and help text.
- **Why:** A person types them and the old docs list them among the global flags, so the reference must hold them; under system.frozen-behaviour they are reference for the target CLI's own global flags, not surface. This repository has Effect 4.1.2, whose --completions also offers powershell and pwsh; the old CLI pinned 4.0.0 and printed <bash|zsh|fish|sh>, so the library source is not the old words. The 38 recorded results all agree. The old CLI drops --wizard outside an interactive terminal (runtime/entry.ts); that is behaviour, not grammar.

### rules.frozen-flags-designed-attribution: A designed flag belongs to the command its synopsis part or table row names

- **Question:** One frozen page's designed (v4) block often describes its whole command family. `org round create`'s synopsis is `capxul org round preview|create --class … · invite <round> --investor --amount · show <round>`; `org vote list`'s flags table lists `--until` "For `wait`"; `org agreement change`'s lists `--last-day` "For `end`". `org vote create`'s table keys each row by a matter and puts that matter's flags (`--name --converts-to …`) in the meaning cell. On which frozen command does flags.ts list each designed flag?
- **Chosen:** On the frozen command its part or row names: a part names its command in full after `capxul` (or an operator script), or by its last words under the page's family, and `a|b` names both; a row naming "For `x`" is for x, and also limits the page's synopsis for that flag to x. When no part names the page, the first part is the page's own v4 spelling. A row keyed by an argument's value lists the flags in its meaning cell for the page. flags.ts names the other page in `designedNotInCliOnPage`.
- **Why:** flags.ts is the reference the target designs its flags from (system.frozen-behaviour), so it has to say which command each designed flag was for. The page form put flags on the wrong commands and lost others. It gave `org vote list` the `--until` that the old CLI declares on `org vote wait`, gave `org agreement change` the `--last-day` designed for `end` (whose CLI flag is `--on`), gave `org round create` the invite's `--investor --amount`, and left `org round preview`, `org round invite` and `org safe invite`, which the old CLI never had, with no flags at all, though their only grammar is in these synopses. It also missed the 15 matter flags of `org vote create` beyond `--class --authorised`. Named, the designed text lists 58 flags for 25 commands that the old CLI does not declare. One part names no frozen command (`invites void --referee` on `waitlist invites grant`, an operator script); the generator reports it and keeps it nowhere.

### rules.frozen-flags-kept: The old flags are reference for the target's flags, not surface to keep

- **Question:** In 66 of the 75 designed operations the old CLI has, the designed (v4) text leaves out 189 flags the CLI declares, most often --email, --timeout-seconds and --no-wait. The designed text also lists 58 flags for 25 commands that the old CLI never declared (each on the command its synopsis part or table row names: rules.frozen-flags-designed-attribution), and 23 frozen commands the old CLI never had at all. What do books/frozen/flags.ts and the designed flags bind?
- **Chosen:** Nothing: they are reference. flags.ts records both, the CLI's own and the designed ones the CLI lacks (`designedNotInCli`, with `inCli: false` on the 23 commands), without inventing a type or help text. B1 designs each operation's flags in the book as one consistent system and may keep, rename, merge or drop any old flag; a dropped flag needs no decision, but an acceptance claim that depends on a flag's behaviour keeps that behaviour.
- **Why:** First recorded as union under P10 rung 1, which made flags surface. Aaron's system.frozen-behaviour (8 November 2026) frees the spelling: the 215 operations and what their acceptance claims require are kept, and the flags are the target's to design. The extraction still earns its keep: it is the one complete list of what the old commands took, so B1's design sees every input an old caller could pass, and the designed-only flags show what v4 meant to add.

### rules.frozen-flags-source: The frozen flags are read from the old CLI's own grammar

- **Question:** Where does books/frozen/flags.ts take each frozen command's arguments and flags from, so that it keeps the old CLI's words and invents nothing?
- **Chosen:** tools/freeze-flags.ts parses apps/cli/src with the TypeScript compiler API and evaluates only the Effect CLI grammar (Command.make, Flag.*, Argument.*, GlobalFlag.Setting), following constants and flag helpers across imports into the old packages. It never runs a handler, and it stops on any name, help text, choice or default it cannot work out.
- **Why:** `capxul schema` is the CLI's own manifest, but running it needs the old workspace installed (Effect 4.0.0, @capxul/sdk and their dependencies) and runs the old code; the checkout has no node_modules. The docs pages lose what the frozen surface needs: they capitalise each help text and end it with a period, escape Markdown, and drop types, defaults and repeats. Read from source, the grammar agrees with the docs pages on all 192 commands the old CLI has (names, order, required, and help text once the docs formatting is undone), and the six global flags it declares agree with its recorded `capxul schema` output. Two runs write the same file byte for byte.

### rules.frozen-flags-unlisted: The old CLI's 21 unlisted commands are not frozen

- **Question:** The old CLI has 21 command paths that no frozen page names, all hidden from --help: bare `capxul` (home), `invite get|review|wait`, `org invite get|wait|retry`, `org allowance command get|wait`, the ten `org payroll` paths and `org proposal create`. Does flags.ts freeze them?
- **Chosen:** No. They are recovery and unfinished commands the old book never made operations. flags.ts covers the 215 frozen commands only, and the target adds such a command only through an operation record.
- **Why:** The frozen surface is the 215 operations (P10), and P2 deletes what nothing reaches. No frozen page or acceptance line needs one of them: `schema` #4 uses `schema org.payroll.run` as its example, but it still holds when neither spelling names a command, since both are refused alike with CLI_USAGE; its eval should use a frozen command. No frozen operation describes bare `capxul`, so what it prints is the target CLI's own design.

### rules.global-tenancy: A global table has no tenant and is written only through its own concept

- **Question:** CONVENTIONS said a `global` table is written only by the system (prices, flags). Parties' directory is `global` but written through parties' procedures and rpcs at a person's or an Organization's request (parties.directory-tenancy). Which holds?
- **Chosen:** `global`: no tenant column; every row may be read by every tenant and holds nothing private; written only by the owning concept's own procedures, rpcs and workflows, never directly by a tenant. Prices, flags and the directory of Parties.
- **Why:** Row-level security keeps a tenant's rows from other tenants; a global row belongs to none, so what matters is who writes it and that nothing private is in it. Every target write goes through its concept's records anyway (P4), so 'only the system' and 'only the owning concept' differ only in whether a caller's request may cause the write. The directory's tenancy stays flagged for Aaron's data-model review (W1) in parties.directory-tenancy; this record only stops the conventions contradicting it.

### rules.outbox: The outbox and the telemetry relay are runtime, not booked records

- **Question:** Are the outbox table and the relay that sends events to PostHog concept records?
- **Chosen:** The runtime writes each transition's event to an outbox row in its transaction; one generic relay delivers them; a reaction is a workflow triggered by the transition.
- **Why:** P3 and P7 make telemetry the runtime's job ('telemetry is never a workflow') and every reaction a workflow whose trigger is the transition. Booking the outbox in a concept would make every concept depend on it, and the generic relay has no domain behaviour to specify.

### rules.procedures: A procedure only for one money fact, read narrowly

- **Question:** P4 allows a cross-concept write in one transaction only for the same money fact. What counts?
- **Chosen:** A fact about money moved, reserved, routed or owned: a Payment and its book fact or document; a chain write and the approval or proposal that carries it; a name and the Safe it routes money to; a deposit and the request it paid; a Payment command another concept births. Everything else goes through the outbox.
- **Why:** The test is P4's: if the two writes disagreed at a commit, could money move, or be owned, that Capxul's records do not account for? An approval opened without its intent would let a person sign a money movement with no Payment; a proposal without its intent would execute a budget nobody recorded; a handle bound to the wrong Safe misroutes payments. A notice, a projection or telemetry can lag and be rebuilt, so it never qualifies. Each procedure's `about` names its fact, and the build's `notice-in-transaction` rule, when it lands, refuses one that is only a notice. One authority fact is admitted beside them, `accounts.admit` (procedures.accounts-admit), because neither a procedure for money nor the outbox can carry it.

### rules.process-wakeups: The runtime wakes a waiting process by the key its event carries

- **Question:** Approvals waits for Ponder's UserOperationEvent at (chain, sender, nonce) and for its opener's withdrawal; payments, budgets, payroll and equity wait on approval_transitioned and proposal_transitioned. What delivers an event to the one instance waiting for it?
- **Chosen:** The runtime. A transition's outbox event wakes each instance whose wait names it and whose key its props carry (the opener and subject on approval_transitioned and proposal_transitioned); an indexed chain event wakes one by the key Ponder's row carries (chain, sender, nonce; a payment id). The book names the wait and, in its `key`, what wakes it and by which properties.
- **Why:** A3 makes each long-lived object one process that owns its machine, and a wait is its own span, never system time. A reactor per event would add a workflow for every wait, and a poll spends system time and lags by its interval. Effect Workflow resumes an instance through a durable deferred it can address, so the runtime keeps the map from an event's key to that address, fed by the outbox relay and by Ponder's progress (integration indexer). The A2 spike must prove both, including a transition a procedure writes in another concept's transaction (approvals.withdraw). The key is now a field of the wait (rules.admissions-and-wait-keys): each entry names its carriers (an event, a chain event, Ponder's row, a signal, or the instance's own call) and the properties they carry, the build checks each property against the carrier, and `wfl:<workflow>/wake/<wait>` is the eval the runtime must pass.

### rules.surfaces-and-wait-events: Flows name their surfaces; waits name the chain events that end them

- **Question:** Three groups asked for the schema to say what the book could only say in prose: twelve flows serve the approval page, checkout, the web app or an agent's SDK host and read as `unused-flow`, and a process that waits on a chain event cannot link it, so sixty chain events read as `unconsumed-chain-event` (A3 makes a process wait on its events, not be triggered by them). Change packages/book, or leave the gaps?
- **Chosen:** FlowSpec gains `surfaces` (the system's surfaces: cli, sdk, react, mcp, http); a flow whose surfaces leave out cli is not an unused-flow, and a target flow that implements an operation must keep cli (rule flow-surface). A workflow wait gains `events` (chain events), a link the gap counts. Both are optional, so the as-is and frozen books are unchanged.
- **Why:** A gap should mean something is missing; these were the schema's blind spots, and 72 real gaps were hidden among 130. A workflow per event would split an object's one writer (A3, rules.durable-processes). `surfaces` reuses the system's vocabulary, and flow-surface keeps it from hiding a CLI flow. A wait's `events` are the logs the process reads at finality, so the remaining unconsumed events are ones only indexing's views read or nothing uses. The routing key was left to the wait's `about` (rules.process-wakeups); rules.admissions-and-wait-keys later made it the wait's `key`. Tests in packages/book/test/build.test.ts cover both.

### rules.transaction-width: sys:tx-width counts the procedures an rpc invokes itself

- **Question:** P4's sys:tx-width allows at most 3 procedures from at most 2 other concepts in one transaction. Does a procedure a door invokes inside itself (board.propose opening approvals.open) count against the rpc that invoked the door?
- **Chosen:** Count what one run of the rpc or workflow invokes itself; where its branches invoke different doors (approvals.open for a person, board.propose for an Organization), its widest branch. A procedure's own invocations count against that procedure, held to the same limit. A wider transaction needs the invoking concept's decision record.
- **Why:** The rule bounds what one concept couples into its transaction, and an invoker cannot change what a door does inside: board.propose always opens its first approval. Counting that against every proposer (budgets, payroll, payments, agents, equity) would push each to split one money fact over two transactions, which P4 exists to prevent. The door is bounded on its own (board.propose: one procedure of one concept). An rpc's `invokes` lists every branch's doors, so agents' create and sweep list three concepts but run two (agents.transaction-width). P4 allows a wider one with a decision record: payments.birth-width is that record for payments' prepare and propose (parties, documents, and approvals or board). Equity's Board-matter prepares are within the rule.

### system.actors: Actors as v4 has them

- **Question:** Which actors does the target system list?
- **Chosen:** The old actors, with ai-seat folded into agent, system-job renamed system, and office-holder covering every hat wearer.
- **Why:** The designed text replaces `--ai` with `--agent` ('The Seat is held by an agent'), so an AI Seat is an agent with a Seat. A workflow acts for no caller, whatever started it, so 'system' covers crons, timers, chain events and transitions.

### system.build-order: Concepts are built in dependency layers; priority orders the work within each

- **Question:** Board is low priority, yet budgets, payments, payroll, agents, equity and notices depend on it. In what order are the concepts built, so that no lane waits on a concept below it?
- **Chosen:** By the concept map's layers, each concept's door (its tables, machines and procedures) before any concept that depends on it: access; media; parties; indexing; approvals; accounts; organizations; then board's proposals and door, books, documents, contacts and applications; budgets and agents; payments and payroll; banking and activity; requests; notices; devnet's own dev commands alongside the harness. Within a concept, critical operations first. Board's director and vote operations and all of equity are built last, and a lane may build against their events before they exist.
- **Why:** Built in layers, every procedure a lane invokes already exists and is proven, so no lane builds against a stub that can drift from the real door. Board shows why priority alone fails: its proposal machine and board.propose carry every Board payment, budget and grant, so they are on the critical path even though its director and voting commands are not. Equity stays last because only notices depends on it, and only for its events.

### system.frozen-behaviour: The freeze keeps behaviour, not spelling

- **Question:** The old book's 215 operations were frozen word for word: commands, flags, `--json` shapes, refusal codes and text, with P10 making the frozen text win wherever it disagreed with the code. Does the rebuild keep that spelling, or only what it does?
- **Chosen:** Keep the behaviour, free the spelling. The 215 operations stay as the list of capabilities, and what each acceptance claim requires to happen stays as its evals: that is the hill. The exact flags, `--json` shapes, refusal codes and wording are no longer frozen; the target book designs them as one consistent system. A refusal is a behaviour (a signed-out caller is refused; a payment over the balance is refused), checked by its code only where the target's own error vocabulary keeps it. A renamed command, a rewritten claim or a retired one carries a decision.
- **Why:** Nothing outside depends on the exact CLI surface, and the old staging data is being dropped (environments.staging), so the strict freeze only made the rebuild copy old inconsistencies: restoring old --json shapes and refusal codes cost design work and bought nobody anything. What must survive is what the system does, and the acceptance claims say that. This supersedes the strict reading of the freeze and P10's 'the frozen text wins' for spelling; P10's rungs still decide behaviour, money and internals. books/frozen stays as the list of capabilities and the source of each claim, and books/frozen/flags.ts is reference for designing the new flags, not surface to preserve. tools/check-frozen.ts checks that every operation is booked once and that every acceptance claim and refusal behaviour is kept in intent, or rewritten or retired with a decision.

### system.layout: Product code mirrors the book: one package per component, one folder per concept

- **Question:** The book says what to build but not where the code goes. Where do the product packages, the generated files, the B0 spike and each concept's code live, how is generated code told apart from hand-written code, and which branch integrates the lanes?
- **Chosen:** npm workspaces in this repository. One package per component: packages/runtime (the Effect services: auth, SQL, chain, integrations, the workflow engine, tracing), packages/backend, packages/sdk (the Core SDK), packages/cli, packages/sdk-react, packages/mcp, packages/indexer (Ponder), packages/devnet, plus packages/generate (the generators) and packages/evals (the harness). In the backend and the SDK, src/<concept>/ mirrors books/target/<concept>/, and another concept's code is imported only from its src/<concept>/door.ts (sys:imports). Generated files live in each package's src/generated/, each headed 'Generated from books/target by packages/generate. Do not edit.', and line counts leave that folder out. The B0 spike is spikes/durable-execution/, deleted once its decision is recorded. Lanes are branches lane/<concept> merged by pull request into integration/build; integration/build merges into main at each milestone's exit.
- **Why:** A record and its code then share a name and a place, so an agent finds one from the other without a map, and the evals that need a layout have one: sys:imports reads src/<concept>/ imports, sys:loc counts each concept's folder, system.size counts everything outside src/generated/, and sys:generated regenerates src/generated/ and diffs it. By layer spreads a concept over four places, which is what made the old system hard to change; the old layout carries its pre-v4 packages (protocol, aa-gateway, scenarios) that P9 removes. One integration branch keeps main green while lanes land.

### system.size: The rebuild's hand-written code is at most a quarter of the old system's

- **Question:** The rebuild is meant to need much less code, and lines of code are on the scoreboard. How much hand-written product code may the target have, and when does each concept get its own line budget (sys:loc/<concept>)?
- **Chosen:** At most 100,000 hand-written, non-test, non-generated lines across the backend, Core SDK, CLI, React SDK, MCP server, indexer and devnet: a quarter of the old system's 400,000 or so. The build session sets each concept's lines (the concept record's budget, checked by sys:loc) after the pilots (B3), from the lines per operation they measure, and records it here.
- **Why:** Measured on integration/factory (8 November 2026), the old product code is about 400,000 non-test lines: backend 184,000, Core SDK 59,000, protocol 55,000, CLI 48,000, devnet 22,000, React SDK 9,600, observability 9,400, indexer 4,600, MCP 4,400, AA gateway 2,900. Much of it goes: pre-v4 paths (P9), schemas, routers, clients, CLI wiring and telemetry that are now generated from the records, and duplicate event and status plumbing (P7). What is left to write by hand is the body of each rpc, flow step and workflow step, about 215 operations at 150 to 300 lines, plus a runtime spine of some 15,000 and a devnet of some 10,000: 60,000 to 100,000. A per-concept number set now would be a guess from the old areas, which are what is being cut; the pilots measure the real ratio first. With no budget, size is not hill-climbable.

### vocabulary.classes: Use ADR-0039's auth and permission classes; one class per code

- **Question:** The old code had no auth or permission class and four codes classed by mode. What do target codes carry?
- **Chosen:** NOT_AUTHENTICATED is auth; ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED and BELOW_THRESHOLD are permission; by-mode codes get one class and carry the mode in details.
- **Why:** The book's error record has one class, and ADR-0039's classes include auth and permission, which say who acts better than `input` and `state` did. A failure mode (timeout, refused, revert) is a detail of one occurrence, not of the code, so it travels in `details.mode` and in the span's attributes (P6).

### vocabulary.declined-preview-cancelled: Declining a terminal preview ends CANCELLED in every command

- **Question:** The frozen pages say a declined preview writes nothing, not what it exits with. The old CLI answered CLI_USAGE (exit 2); contacts answers CANCELLED (exit 130, contacts.decline-is-cancelled). One rule for every command?
- **Chosen:** A 'no' to a terminal preview ends CANCELLED (exit 130) and writes nothing, in every command; a script without `--confirm` stays CLI_USAGE, as the frozen mistakes say.
- **Why:** The frozen book is silent (P10 rung 5: the code is evidence), and one CLI must answer one way. A 'no' is the person's choice, not a usage mistake, and CANCELLED already says the command stopped before it finished and nothing was sent, as a declined approval ends (vocabulary.drop-signer-rejected). Scripts pass `--confirm` and never see the question, so no script's exit changes. A command whose approval page is its confirmation refuses `--confirm` and has no terminal question to decline.

### vocabulary.doctor-deadline-exit: `doctor`'s exit 124 is the command's, not NETWORK_ERROR's

- **Question:** The frozen `doctor` exits 124 with NETWORK_ERROR when its online check passes its deadline. One code has one exit (5). Which wins?
- **Chosen:** NETWORK_ERROR keeps exit 5; `doctor` alone exits 124 on its own deadline, as its frozen notes and acceptance say.
- **Why:** The 124 is the timeout convention of one command (`timeout(1)`), written into `doctor`'s notes and an acceptance line, so it is frozen surface (P10 rung 1); every other NETWORK_ERROR exits 5 by the old exit table. Moving the code's exit would change every other command's. The applications designer books the override on the operation.

### vocabulary.drop-account-not-found: Drop ACCOUNT_NOT_FOUND; an unknown id is INVALID_INPUT

- **Question:** Keep ACCOUNT_NOT_FOUND?
- **Chosen:** Merge into INVALID_INPUT, which the frozen refusals already use for an id that names nothing.
- **Why:** No frozen text names it; the frozen refusals answer an unknown or unreadable id with INVALID_INPUT throughout. The old E5 problem (not found and not yours look alike) is a privacy property worth keeping: a caller learns nothing about another tenant's ids.

### vocabulary.drop-actor-stopped: Drop ACTOR_STOPPED; a frozen agent's refusal is WRONG_STATE

- **Question:** Keep ACTOR_STOPPED for a stopped account or Organization?
- **Chosen:** Merge into WRONG_STATE: a frozen agent or a stopped Organization is a state that does not allow the request.
- **Why:** No frozen text names it, and on v4 a frozen agent is refused by AgentOwner on chain, which surfaces as a state refusal.

### vocabulary.drop-account-claimed-by-other-signer: Drop ACCOUNT_CLAIMED_BY_OTHER_SIGNER; it is WRONG_STATE

- **Question:** Keep ACCOUNT_CLAIMED_BY_OTHER_SIGNER?
- **Chosen:** Merge into WRONG_STATE, as the designed `account retry` refusal does.
- **Why:** The designed text answers 'The Safe is already claimed by another signer' with WRONG_STATE (CompaniesHouseClaimer's SwapRefused).

### vocabulary.drop-email-delivery-failed: Drop EMAIL_DELIVERY_FAILED; a mail failure is PROVIDER_ERROR

- **Question:** Keep EMAIL_DELIVERY_FAILED?
- **Chosen:** Drop it: notices send after the commit and retry durably; the sign-in code's failure is the mail provider's, PROVIDER_ERROR.
- **Why:** No frozen text names it. In the target no request waits on an email except the sign-in code, whose failure is a provider failure like any other; every other email is a notices workflow, whose failures are its delivery records.

### vocabulary.drop-env-missing: Drop ENV_MISSING; configuration is checked at boot

- **Question:** Keep ENV_MISSING for a request that meets a missing setting?
- **Chosen:** Drop it: every layer reads its configuration when the service starts, and a missing setting stops the boot.
- **Why:** With Effect layers, configuration is read when the layer is built, so a missing setting is a failed boot (`env:<env>/boot`) and never reaches a caller. The old code met it at run time, where it was misread as an outage (old R3).

### vocabulary.drop-player-not-found: Drop PLAYER_NOT_FOUND

- **Question:** Keep PLAYER_NOT_FOUND, Openfort's missing player?
- **Chosen:** Drop it: Openfort's player is not a Capxul concept; a missing person is NOT_AUTHENTICATED or PROFILE_NOT_FOUND.
- **Why:** No frozen text names it, and it leaks a vendor's word into the product's vocabulary.

### vocabulary.drop-signer-rejected: Drop SIGNER_REJECTED; a declined approval is CANCELLED

- **Question:** Keep SIGNER_REJECTED for a person who declines to sign?
- **Chosen:** Merge into CANCELLED, as the frozen fixes say ('a rejection with CANCELLED').
- **Why:** Three frozen fixes say a rejected approval ends with CANCELLED; nothing names SIGNER_REJECTED.

### vocabulary.drop-stale-epoch: Drop STALE_EPOCH

- **Question:** Keep STALE_EPOCH for a request based on stale data?
- **Chosen:** Drop it: the frozen text answers a stale revision with INVALID_INPUT and a changed state with WRONG_STATE.
- **Why:** `service revise` refuses a changed revision with INVALID_INPUT; no frozen text names STALE_EPOCH.

### vocabulary.exit-codes: Exit codes: the designed text where it names one, else the old CLI

- **Question:** One code has one exit. Where the designed refusals and the old CLI disagree, which wins?
- **Chosen:** The designed text's exit where it names the code; the old `exitClass` otherwise; a tie goes to the class rule (input refusals exit 2).
- **Why:** P10 rung 1 makes exit codes surface, with the designed v4 text first; it names exits for 22 codes and moves five from 6 to 2 (INSUFFICIENT_BALANCE, VERIFICATION_REQUIRED, PAYROLL_BUDGET_EXCEEDED, INVALID_RECIPIENT, TOKEN_NOT_SUPPORTED). Two outliers are settled: INVALID_INPUT is 2 (27 designed refusals) not 1 (one, waitlist.invites.grant), and TOKEN_NOT_SUPPORTED is 2 (one 2, one 6; it is an input refusal). Recomputing every exit would change codes no text asked to change.

### vocabulary.keep-unnamed: Keep five unnamed codes the runtime needs

- **Question:** UNKNOWN, TRANSACTION_FAILED, PROVIDER_RESPONSE_INVALID, NOT_IMPLEMENTED and CLI_DEFECT are named by no frozen text. Keep them?
- **Chosen:** Keep all five, each for one case nothing else covers.
- **Why:** UNKNOWN is the one defect code every unexpected failure maps to. TRANSACTION_FAILED is a revert on chain, which no refusal names but every chain write can meet. PROVIDER_RESPONSE_INVALID marks an answer that will not change on retry, which PROVIDER_ERROR (retryable) would misstate. NOT_IMPLEMENTED is what a frozen command answers until it is built (the low-priority equity area will be last), and it leaves when the last one is built. CLI_DEFECT is exit 1 in the CLI's frozen exit table.

### vocabulary.kept: Keep every code the frozen book names

- **Question:** Which old error codes does the target keep without question?
- **Chosen:** Every code named in a frozen refusal (top-level or designed), a mistake's fix or an acceptance line.
- **Why:** P10 rung 1: refusal codes are surface, and the book wins. A code named in a mistake's fix (PROFILE_NOT_FOUND, OTP_EXPIRED, WORK_DIED, ENVIRONMENT_NOT_CHOSEN, CANCELLED) is what a person is told to expect, so it is surface too. That keeps 32 old codes named in refusals and these 5; with the 5 new equity codes and the 5 kept unnamed ones the target has 47 codes, from the old 50.

### vocabulary.new-equity-codes: Add the five codes the frozen equity refusals name

- **Question:** The frozen book names CONSENT_MISSING, INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS, NOT_VESTED, RESERVED_MATTER and ROUND_CLOSED, which the old code never defined. Add them?
- **Chosen:** Add all five with the designed text's exit 2, class state.
- **Why:** They are refusal codes in the frozen surface (P10 rung 1), from designed v4 text that was never built (equity was NOT BUILT on the old devnet). Merging them would change what the frozen commands answer. INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS stays apart from INSUFFICIENT_BALANCE because `shares exercise` names it.

### vocabulary.shared-only: All error codes are shared; no concept defines its own

- **Question:** May a concept define an error code in its own folder, as old organizations, payroll and permissions did?
- **Chosen:** Every code lives in shared/vocabulary.ts; a new one needs a shared decision.
- **Why:** The CLI's exit table, the SDK's error union and the refusal evals all enumerate one list, and an old concept's codes (PAYROLL_BUDGET_EXCEEDED) are now raised by another (budgets). One file makes a duplicate code impossible.

### vocabulary.provider-error: PROVIDER_ERROR keeps both meanings the frozen book gives it

- **Question:** The frozen book names PROVIDER_ERROR for 'cannot be reached' and for 'refuses the call'. Split it?
- **Chosen:** One code, class provider-unavailable, retryable; `details.mode: refused` marks a refusal.
- **Why:** `verify start`, `verify status` and `bank settle` name PROVIDER_ERROR for both, and refusal codes are surface (P10). The common case is unavailability, so that is its class. A malformed answer keeps its own code (PROVIDER_RESPONSE_INVALID).

### journeys.onto-target: Each old journey is a journey of target operations, proved by machine events

- **Question:** The old book's 13 journeys are a key, an ordered list of frozen operations and chapters; their funnels were separate contracts over old events. A target journey step names an operation, a system actor and what proves it. How does each old journey map onto the target, and what proves each step?
- **Chosen:** One journey per old key, the key kept as its id. Each step is the frozen operation's target record, in the old order, taken by the system actor its `who` (or, where the frozen page has none, its rpc's) names in the journey's story, and proved by the event of the machine whose state that operation changes: the transition its rpc drives, or, for a chain write its flow waits on, the one the concept's process makes when the write lands. A step that changes no machine (a read, a local or devnet step, a change kept in a plain column) is proved by `success`, the operation's own outcome.
- **Why:** P7 (accepted) re-points each journey step to a machine event, or to the operation's outcome where no state changes. The first event a flow sends is mostly mechanism many steps share (an approval opened, a recipient's Party named, a Board proposal), so it would not say which step happened; success alone loses what a process does after the call, such as a chain write landing. The old contracts grouped operations into outcomes and kept drop reasons, which P6 and P7 move to the wide event's error code, while a target journey is one list that is both the jrn: scenario and the fun: funnel. Keys stay as ids (the id pattern takes underscores), so the old catalog's and PostHog's names hold. Steps that share an event are told apart by its properties: its `transition`, or the kind of change it carries (nine run_company steps are budget_change_transitioned, told apart by `kind`). Three pairs of them share a `kind` too: `org allowance change` and `org department budget set` (set_amount), `org allowance assign` and `org department holder add` (add_holder), `org allowance revoke` and `org department holder remove` (remove_holder). The event carries no budget kind, so within each pair only its `budget_ref` (the allowance's or the department's) tells them apart; a funnel that needs them apart needs that kind on the event, which is budgets' change. Alternative endings the old lists keep (a cancel, a decline, a retry) stay steps: the scenario takes each on a second object its world holds, so every step's event is still sent once, in order.

### journeys.steps: Optional operations leave the journeys; the refused `org director` writes go

- **Question:** Four old journeys list reads and changes 'a person makes along the way', and run_company lists the books as optional ('so no payroll step needs them'): 44 operations. And four old steps, `org director add`, `remove`, `replace` and `threshold`, always refuse on v4. Which old operations stay steps?
- **Chosen:** A journey's steps are its main list. The 44 optional operations are left out. Of the refused writes, govern_company drops `remove`, `replace` and `threshold`, whose `org board director remove` and `org board threshold` are its steps already, and run_company's `org director add` becomes `org board director add`, taken by the founder in the same place.
- **Why:** A funnel step measures who got through, so an optional step would count a person who never renames a books account as dropped before the payroll run that ends run_company. The old book marks these as no steps, its funnels never counted them, and each keeps every L1 eval of its own (acc, ref, evt, spn, bud), so nothing goes unchecked. A refused write cannot be a step: jrn: needs every step's operation to succeed, and on v4 the four `org director` writes always end WRONG_STATE naming their `org board` command (board.director-commands). run_company needs its second Director before the Board proposals that follow, so it takes the v4 command; govern_company has them already. The alternative endings stay: the old lists name them as steps (journeys.onto-target). Left out: get_paid 3, get_started 6, pay_someone 5, run_company 30 (11 books operations, 19 along the way).

### journeys.variants: Old variants survive only as properties the target step events carry

- **Question:** The old catalog gave journeys variants (entry_path, surface, subject_kind, funding_source, payer_kind, recipient_is_capxul_account, recipient_kind, issuer_scope_kind, pay_path) as funnel breakdowns. A target variant is a property of a step's event. Which survive?
- **Chosen:** Each old variant becomes the target event property that answers the same question, or goes: get_verified's subject_kind is verification's `subject`; pay_someone's payer_kind is the payment's `owner_kind` and its two recipient variants are its `route` (claim_escrow for a recipient with no account); get_paid's issuer_scope_kind is the request's `owner_kind` and its pay_path the request's `payer_kind` (inbox, escrow or link).
- **Why:** A variant the step events do not carry cannot break a funnel down, and adding properties to another concept's events is that concept's change, not this file's. `surface` is carried by no target product event (P7 keeps events to state changes; the surface is the operation's), `entry_path` only by waitlist_entry_transitioned's `via`, which proves no step (`waitlist join` is left out), and `funding_source` by none, so those go and money_in, get_started and run_company book no variants. The mapped ones keep their old questions: a person or a company, a recipient with an account or not, paid from the inbox, the escrow or the link.

## access (22)

### access.agent-sign-in: An agent signs in with a stateless challenge, proved on chain

- **Question:** How does an agent prove its key, and how is a challenge kept from being replayed?
- **Chosen:** The challenge is Capxul's MAC over the Safe, a nonce and the time; the session it opens keeps the nonce under a unique index.
- **Why:** Issuing a challenge then writes nothing, so anyone can ask for one without growing a table, and the one write is the session that spends it: a replay fails on the nonce's unique index in that same transaction. The proof is the chain's, read at `latest` in parallel: AgentOwner's `agentOf(safe)` names an owner, is not frozen and names the key, and the Safe's only owner is the key. That is the designed v4 refusal: NOT_AUTHENTICATED when the key signs for no agent account or the agent is frozen. A key rotation shows up as a new signer at the next sign-in.

### access.assistant-grant-process: An assistant grant is a long-lived object with one durable process

- **Question:** Who expires an unexchanged code or a grant whose refresh window lapsed?
- **Chosen:** One process per grant: it waits for the code exchange (10 minutes) and then on the sliding refresh window, signalled by the token and revocation routes.
- **Why:** A grant waits on a person (consent made it), on the client (the exchange) and on a clock (the refresh window), and it has an expiry, like an invitation: A3's shape, an object rules.durable-processes lets a designer add. Its own timer ends it on time, where a daily sweep would leave a lapsed grant active for up to a day, and P3 turns what the old system swept for into a process's timer.

### access.assistants: OAuth 2.1 for MCP clients on one grants table, with stateless registration

- **Question:** How does an assistant (an MCP client) get to act as a person, and what does the backend store for it?
- **Chosen:** Stateless client registration (the client id is Capxul's MAC over its name and redirect URIs), a consent page, PKCE codes, rotating refresh tokens and opaque access tokens on one assistant-grants table, which the runtime resolves like a session.
- **Why:** A registration row has nothing to say that the client id cannot carry, and open registration endpoints get flooded, so signing the metadata leaves no client table to sweep. One row per consent holds the code, the tokens' hashes and the refresh window. The runtime resolves an assistant's bearer to the person and its scopes as it resolves a session, so the MCP server passes the token through the Core SDK and needs no exchange (its MCP_EXCHANGE_SECRET is unused). The scopes keep the old grammar; none signs or sends money: the person signs every approval. The discovery documents and registration are stateless protocol routes and are not booked as rpcs.

### access.dropped: Old access functions with no place in the target

- **Question:** What else of old access is not rebuilt, and where did each go?
- **Chosen:** The indexer read scope and the session's `safes` claim (row-level security), application consent (applications), the waitlist and referrals (accounts), operator proof identities and purges (devnet worlds), reserved e2e identities and the fixed code (the devnet mail sink), currentUser (access.sessions.current), the token exchange for the MCP server (the runtime resolves assistant tokens), features.list and setMine (no caller), features.set (devnet's `dev gates set`).
- **Why:** Each either has no caller (P2), is plumbing the runtime now does (P3), lives in the concept the map moved it to (concepts.access, concepts.accounts, concepts.applications), or existed only to test the old system by hand (P8: evals restore worlds instead of seeding proof identities).

### access.email-rule: Sign-in normalises a mailbox by the Safe salt's rule and keeps non-ASCII letters

- **Question:** Sign-in said it normalises the email to ASCII. Accounts' world setup-refused-non-ascii-email signs in with a mailbox whose local part is not ASCII, so that the claim can fail with EmailNotNormalized (`account retry` INVALID_INPUT). Does sign-in refuse such a mailbox, or let it through for the chain to refuse?
- **Chosen:** Trim and lowercase, as the Safe salt does; refuse only what is not an address. A non-ASCII mailbox signs in and its claim is refused on chain.
- **Why:** `waitlist status` says signing in always works, and the old sign-in normalised with normalizeSafeSaltEmail (trim, lowercase) and refused nothing more. The salt rule fixes every person's Safe address, so access must apply it unchanged. Refusing at sign-in would lock such a person out of every read and leave `account retry`'s frozen INVALID_INPUT (EmailNotNormalized) with no world. Accounts' setup-refused-non-ascii-email therefore stays buildable.

### access.gates: One flags table synced each minute, read by every gated concept

- **Question:** What does access keep for feature gates, and how do other concepts check one?
- **Chosen:** One feature-flags row per catalogue gate, rewritten by the minute sync with `checked_at`; devnet's gate table feeds the PostHog fake; every gated concept reads the table through access' one evaluator.
- **Why:** The old copy, its devnet override table and a run log that grew a row a minute were three stores for one answer. Staleness needs only the last good sync's time, which every row's `checked_at` carries; a money gate still reads off when it is older than 10 minutes. Writing gates on a devnet is devnet's (`dev gates set`), seen through the PostHog fake, so production code has one path. A gate check is a read, not a money fact, so it is no procedure (rules.procedures): a concept that refuses FEATURE_NOT_AVAILABLE lists this table in its reads. features.list and setMine had no caller.

### access.local-budgets: Local commands get a tighter budget than the read class

- **Question:** The read class assumes one rpc (600 / 1,200 ms). What budget does a command with no network call get?
- **Chosen:** Operation 300 / 600 ms (the CLI's start and one file); its flow 20 / 100 ms.
- **Why:** P5's floor for these commands is the CLI's 300 ms start and a file read; the read default would let a command that quietly started calling the network pass. Each override carries its why. `auth logout` and `auth switch` call the backend and keep their class (write, chain-read).

### access.local-flows: The local commands are Core SDK flows over the host's saved state

- **Question:** Where does the state of `auth list`, `auth switch`, `auth logout`, `use`, `whoami` and `config telemetry` live, and who raises CLI_STORAGE?
- **Chosen:** The Core SDK's Node host keeps this machine's state in the CLI's settings directory; each command is one flow whose cache steps read or write it and raise CLI_STORAGE.
- **Why:** A4: the CLI calls one SDK method per command, and the flow is the operation's `implementedBy` (concepts.local-commands). The same methods then serve the MCP server's stdio mode and any Node host. A flow cannot call another flow, so `auth switch`'s offer to sign in someone else answers the `capxul auth login` argv instead of running it.

### access.logout-claim: `auth logout` claim 1 is rewritten as what a black box sees

- **Question:** `auth logout` #1 says it "asks the backend to revoke" the session, a mechanism. What does the eval check?
- **Chosen:** "It removes the current person's session on this machine, and the backend refuses that session's token afterwards."
- **Why:** P1: a black box cannot see a request being made, only its outcome: `auth list` no longer lists the person, the world shows the session revoked and the collector shows its transition. The old system passes the rewrite: its sign-out deletes the BetterAuth session, so the token is refused. The old line and its citation are in `was`.

### access.no-device-flow: The native client device flow is not ported; the daily sweep keeps sessions

- **Question:** The concept map names the CLI's native client grants (device flow) and P3 keeps their daily sweep. Are they rebuilt?
- **Chosen:** Drop the native client contexts, requests and grants, their 14 routes and the browser continuation; the daily cron sweeps sessions, codes and ended grants.
- **Why:** P2: nothing starts a device request. The CLI signs in first-party with an emailed code and keeps the old grant records only to delete them (`native-grant-cleanup.ts`); `auth login` never opens a browser; no other client calls /native/device/code. The browser continuation page has no request to continue. The daily cron P3 keeps survives as access.sessions.sweep, and an assistant grant's expiry is its own process's timer.

### access.no-procedures: Access' door has no procedures; the caller is the runtime's

- **Question:** Which procedures do other concepts invoke on access?
- **Chosen:** None: the runtime resolves the caller before the rpc runs, and gates and Safes are reads of access' tables.
- **Why:** A procedure exists for a write that is one money fact with its invoker's (rules.procedures), and no concept's money fact writes a principal, a session, a grant or a flag. Who is calling is resolved once per request by the runtime, which also sets the tenant for row-level security, so it is no more a record than the outbox is (rules.outbox). The old door's read-scope and consent guards went with the indexer scope and application consent.

### access.own-sign-in: Sign-in and sessions on access' own tables, not BetterAuth

- **Question:** Does the rebuild keep BetterAuth (email OTP, bearer, JWT and the OIDC provider) behind access, or build sign-in on access' own tables?
- **Chosen:** Access' own tables and rpcs: an emailed code, an agent's signed challenge, opaque session tokens stored as hashes, OAuth for assistants on one table.
- **Why:** BetterAuth's route handlers write its own models through its adapter, outside the rpc's one transaction (A3), so a session's birth could not send session_transitioned in its transaction (P7); its schema is its own, not generated from the book with row-level security (P8); and its lookups run before any tenant is set. The old system used about eight of its models and mounted some forty routes nobody called (the as-is `access.auth.request`). What Capxul needs is small: a hashed six-digit code with a cooldown, a random token stored as a hash, a challenge proved on chain, and OAuth's code, token and revocation steps for MCP clients. Each is a few rpcs over access' tables, checked by the same evals as every other concept.

### access.principals: One principals table for people and agents, keyed by person_id

- **Question:** How does access know an agent, which has no email and whose record is agents', when access may read no other concept?
- **Chosen:** One principals row per person or agent; `person_id` is the principal id for both, the Safe is on the row, an agent's row is born at its first proved sign-in.
- **Why:** Access depends on nothing (concepts.access), so it cannot key a session into agents' rows; the chain proves an agent (AgentOwner and its Safe's owners), so access can keep its own principal for it. One id column for both kinds lets row-level security set one personal tenant whoever calls. The Safe on the row is what approvals and every personal read need (dependencies.approvals-low: an agent's Safe is on its principal); a person's is a pure function of the email, computed without a chain call. The designed v4 login signs an agent in by key alone, with no inbox.

### access.schema-catalogue: `schema` and `--help` read one catalogue generated from the book

- **Question:** Where does `capxul schema` get each command's arguments, flags, output and errors?
- **Chosen:** A catalogue generated from the book's operation records ships in the Core SDK; `schema` and `--help` both read it.
- **Why:** The operations already hold each command, its returns and its errors; generating the catalogue makes `schema`, `--help` and the book agree by construction, which claim 1 asks of `--help`. It is still a local read.

### access.sessions: Opaque, hashed session tokens: 30 days for a person, 24 hours for an agent

- **Question:** What is a session token, how is it checked on every request, and how long does it last?
- **Chosen:** 32 random bytes handed back once; the runtime finds the session by the token's SHA-256 in the request's own transaction.
- **Why:** A hash lookup is one indexed statement in the transaction every rpc already opens, about the cost of a JWT check (P5's hop table), and a revoked session is refused at once, which a JWT cannot do without a lookup anyway. The old JWKS existed for Convex and for Ponder's read scope, both gone, so the backend needs no JWKS route. A person's session lasts 30 days, then they sign in again; an agent's lasts 24 hours and the SDK signs a new challenge itself (the old agent exchange gave one hour). The session machine records every end, so the funnel sees sign-outs and expiries; the daily sweep writes the expiry.

### access.sign-in-codes: One row per email holds the code and its cooldown; send first, then store

- **Question:** How are the emailed code, its tries and the send cooldown stored, and in what order are sending and storing done?
- **Chosen:** One row per email: code hash, tries, expiry and sent time (the cooldown). Check the cooldown, send, then store in one transaction.
- **Why:** The cooldown and the code are one fact about one address, and the old code kept them in two stores with a claim and release dance between them. Sending before storing means a failed send leaves nothing to release: the person can ask again at once, and a crash after the send leaves only an email whose code was never stored. The numbers stay: six digits, five minutes, three tries, 60 seconds between sends. Three wrong codes are RATE_LIMITED, as the old SDK mapped TOO_MANY_ATTEMPTS; one wrong code is INVALID_INPUT and an old one OTP_EXPIRED, as the frozen book says.

### access.surface-flows: Flows for the browser and for an agent's SDK host reach the sign-in rpcs

- **Question:** Which access flows sign anyone in, now that the CLI's sign-in commands are accounts'?
- **Chosen:** A browser sign-in (web app, approval page, consent page) and an agent key session (an SDK host with the agent's key); accounts' CLI flows use the same rpcs.
- **Why:** A person signs approvals and allows assistants in a browser, which needs its own session; an agent's program on the Core SDK opens and renews its session with its key, as the designed SDK example shows. Neither is a CLI command, so each flow names its surface (P2's rule for non-CLI flows) and no operation implements it.

### access.switch-setup-state: `auth switch` reads the Safe's setup from the chain

- **Question:** `auth switch` answers `setupState`, which is accounts' to know, but access depends on nothing. Where does it come from?
- **Chosen:** access.sessions.current reads the person's Safe: ready when it has code and Companies House is no longer an owner.
- **Why:** The claim is the setup, and it is on chain: Companies House's claim swaps the person's signer in as the Safe's only owner. Two reads give the truth without a dependency; a saved value goes stale the moment setup finishes in the browser. Moving the command would split the local-session commands across two concepts for one field.

### access.tenancy-before-sign-in: A sign-in code's tenant is the email it was sent to

- **Question:** Whose row is a sign-in code, when no principal exists until the code is verified?
- **Chosen:** Tenancy person with tenantField email: the row belongs to the address, and its policy admits only the email the request names.
- **Why:** A principal born for every typed address would put unverified people in the table every concept names. Global is for rows only the system writes (prices, flags), and a code is one person's secret. Keying by the email keeps the row the address owner's, which is what the code proves. The other lookups made before a tenant is known (by token hash, by code hash, by challenge, by Safe) are crossTenant indexes, each saying why.

### access.wallet-recovery: The embedded wallet's recovery session is an access rpc at browser sign-in

- **Question:** Who asks Openfort Shield for the encryption session the person's embedded wallet needs in a new browser?
- **Chosen:** access.sessions.wallet-recovery, a step of the browser sign-in, so the wallet is ready before any approval.
- **Why:** The backend holds the Shield secrets, and the session belongs to the signed-in person; asking once at sign-in keeps the vendor call out of every approval's wait. The CLI and agents sign locally and never ask.

### access.whoami-signer: The designed `client.me.signer()` is `client.whoami()`'s signer field

- **Question:** The designed whoami names the SDK method `client.me.signer`; the CLI calls one method per command. Which?
- **Chosen:** `client.whoami()` answers user, acting-as, Safe, environment and signer; the designed signer is its `signer` field.
- **Why:** A4 allows one SDK method per command, and the signer is read from the same saved session as the rest, so splitting it adds a method with nothing of its own. An SDK host reads `signer.kind` from the one answer, as the designed text intends.

### access.worlds: Three worlds for access' refusals

- **Question:** Which named worlds reach access' refusals, given that most of them are about this machine's files?
- **Chosen:** empty-devnet (v4 deployed, no principal, a fresh CLI home); person-signed-in (Dana signed in and current); two-people-signed-in (Dana and Sam signed in, Dana current). Each devnet snapshot is taken with the eval machine's CLI home, and a CLI_STORAGE refusal makes the state file unreadable or read-only after the restore.
- **Why:** Every refusal access owns is reached from one of three states of this machine: nobody signed in, one person, two people. A file the eval cannot read is a step after the restore, not a world of its own, so the old whoami note (the runner cannot make the state unreadable) no longer holds: the harness owns the eval's CLI home.

## accounts (18)

### accounts.admission-paths: The request form, the held sign-in record and the list email are not ported

- **Question:** Old access also put people on the list through a public request form (paced per IP), recorded every sign-in the gate held, and emailed a held person that they were on the list. Keep them?
- **Chosen:** Drop all three: `waitlist join` is the way onto the list, and admissions come from operators, invites, invitations and rewards.
- **Why:** No frozen operation reaches the form or its window (P2), and the system's HTTP routes are the bootstrap, OAuth and Bridge's webhook. The hold wrote a row inside a refusal, which the target's refusals forbid ('changes nothing'), and it existed for the operator's list, which is dropped. The 'on the list' email followed old profile onboarding; any email after a change is notices', from `waitlist_entry_transitioned`.

### accounts.door: The door holds only `accounts.admit`; the concepts above read accounts' tables

- **Question:** What does accounts offer the concepts above it (organizations, agents, payments, equity, notices)?
- **Chosen:** One procedure, `accounts.admit`; the rest read personal-safes and admissions, or follow accounts' events.
- **Why:** A procedure is for a write that must commit with the invoker's (P4); reads need none, because a concept may read the tables of what it depends on. Organizations needs 'the founder is admitted with a claimed Safe' (the two tables) and follows no accounts event, since an invitee takes the Seat on accepting and no Seat waits; payments reads a live Safe from the chain. `accounts.admit` takes the invitation's Organization, answers whether it admitted, and never welcomes an invitee twice, as the shared ruling needs.

### accounts.dropped-rows: Old accounts' and access' internal rows are not ported

- **Question:** Which old rows of accounts and of access' waitlist and referrals have no target counterpart?
- **Chosen:** safes.provision, confirm-deployment, both loads, read-safe and record-deployment; the six claim store mutations; the lanes; the telemetry posters; the grants' four transitions and batch; waitlist hold, list, record, request and its preflight; both delivery workflows.
- **Why:** The setup start, the two setup reads and the setup process do what provision, claim, the loads and confirm-deployment did, and Ponder proves a deployment, so nothing confirms one by hand. The store mutations, lanes and grant transitions were steps of one action (P3); the posters were telemetry, which transitions now send (P7). The waitlist rows go with the form and the hold (`accounts.admission-paths`); the welcome and on-the-list emails are notices'. Balances, summaries, approvals and agents left for activity, approvals and agents by the shared decisions.

### accounts.events: Accounts' events carry no mailbox

- **Question:** Each machine's event carries the ids a funnel needs. Admission and referral rows are keyed by email. Does the event carry it?
- **Chosen:** No email in any property: `person_id` where it is known, the setup's person and Safe, and the runtime's actor.
- **Why:** Events leave Capxul for PostHog through the outbox; an email there is personal data in an analytics store, and the old events never carried one (they were sent as the person or the referrer). The setup's event carries the person and the Safe, which is enough for the activation funnel; an admission of a mailbox nobody has signed in with has no person to name yet, and its later `personal_safe_transitioned` does.

### accounts.faucet: `dev fund` sends one faucet mint and answers at broadcast

- **Question:** The old faucet minted from a backend-owned faucet Safe as a sponsored UserOperation and waited for inclusion inside the request. The target has a faucet service key, and an rpc may not wait for the chain. What does `dev fund` do?
- **Chosen:** One action rpc: check, send the mint through the service signer, answer the hash at broadcast.
- **Why:** Rule 5 keeps waits out of rpcs, and a test mint is not a long-lived object worth a table and a process. The service signer records the signed bytes before broadcasting, so a lost answer lands or is looked up, never sent twice. It is refused off test networks, and `--org` off a devnet, with WRONG_ENVIRONMENT (no devnet world reaches that refusal; the staging `live:*` evals do). Admission is required, as `waitlist status` says of the faucet, and the person's Safe is the one their setup names. The mint is the test tokens' open `ERC20.mint(recipient, amount)` (shared `contracts.test-token-mint`), named in the `chain` of this rpc and of the setup's staging welcome step.

### accounts.mailbox-tenancy: Admission and referrals are keyed by the mailbox; a referral is its referrer's

- **Question:** P8 gives a person's rows a `person_id`. An invite, an operator or an Organization's invitation admits an email that may never have signed in. What is the tenant of the waitlist, the codes and the referrals?
- **Chosen:** Tenancy `person` with the normalized email as the tenant column; a referral row is the referrer's, with a unique cross-tenant index on the referee.
- **Why:** Access knows a person by their email, so the mailbox is the person's identity before and after they sign in, and the row-level policy can match the session's verified email as it matches its person id. `global` is for rows only the system writes, and a person writes their own note. A referral is acted on by its referrer (invite, list, counts), so it is theirs; the friend sees only who referred them, through `by-referee`, which is cross-tenant by design because the first referral of a mailbox wins whoever made it. personal-safes is a signed-in person's, so it keys on `person_id`.

### accounts.no-acceptance: dev fund and the waitlist grant get no acceptance lines from the design

- **Question:** `dev fund` and `waitlist invites grant` had no acceptance lines in the old book, so each shows a no-acceptance gap. Should the design write lines for them?
- **Chosen:** Write none. Their returns, refusals, events and budgets are still evals; new lines need Aaron.
- **Why:** An acceptance line is frozen surface (P1), and a line the design wrote would be a new public claim, which is Aaron's to accept (equity.acceptance, devnet.no-acceptance). Both are operator and test tooling, and their refusals and returns are checked.

### accounts.operator-tools: The operator admits through its tool's flow; grant and void are one rpc
