- **Question:** Only `waitlist invites grant` of the operator's tool is frozen (with `invites void` beside it), but `waitlist status` claims a person waits 'until an operator admits them'. What of the operator's admit, void and list?
- **Chosen:** `accounts.admission.admit`, reached by the operator tool's flow; grant and void one rpc that implements the frozen operation; `waitlist list` dropped.
- **Why:** The concept map gives accounts 'an operator's admissions', and the second `waitlist status` line cannot be checked without one, so it stays; P2 lets it be reached by a flow that names its surface. Grant and void are the frozen operation's two subcommands (its synopsis and its `referral_transitioned voided` event), so they share its rpc. `waitlist list` is read by no frozen operation and no claim, so it goes (P2). An operator acts with the deployment's operator key; without it every operator rpc is NOT_AUTHENTICATED.

### accounts.profile-before-setup: Setup follows the profile; the person's Party is parties' own

- **Question:** The person's Party must route money to their Safe, and the old onboarding asked for a profile before the Safe. Does the setup write the Party, and does it need the profile?
- **Chosen:** The start needs the person's completed profile (PROFILE_NOT_FOUND) and writes nothing in parties.
- **Why:** Parties gives every mailbox its Party at the email-derived Safe from the start and claims it for the person when they complete their profile; `parties.register` is for Organizations' and agents' births only. The Safe is the same pure function of the email either way, so a write from accounts would only repeat what parties holds. Requiring the profile keeps one order on the setup page (profile, then Safe) and gives `account show` the PROFILE_NOT_FOUND its frozen mistake names, which parties' design expects accounts to answer.

### accounts.qualify-from-chain: Referral rewards follow the chain's CapxulSafeClaimed

- **Question:** A referrer earns an invite when the person they referred gets a live Safe. What starts the qualify?
- **Chosen:** A workflow per referral, triggered by the indexed CapxulSafeClaimed, finds the mailbox by the Safe.
- **Why:** The concept map counts invites 'from the chain's Safe claims'. The chain event is the proof a reward pays for, it still arrives when the setup that sent the claim died before recording it, and it keeps referrals out of the setup's code. Its run is one transaction, keyed by the friend's mailbox, so a replayed or reindexed event earns nothing twice. The old qualify, scheduled from the claim action, did the same work.

### accounts.setup-in-web-app: The web app starts setup; the CLI only reads it

- **Question:** No frozen operation starts a Safe's setup: `account retry` 'never opens a browser and never sets up the account from the CLI'. P2 keeps no rpc nothing reaches. How is the setup start reached?
- **Chosen:** `accounts.setup.start` is a step of `client.account.setup`, the web app's setup page, which also reads the signer and follows the setup.
- **Why:** The designed text says setup in the browser ends with the claim, and the person's embedded signer lives only there. P2 allows an rpc reached by a flow that serves another surface and says so; the start is an rpc (one transaction), and the waiting is the process's. The start is idempotent on the person: the same signer answers the setup as it stands, another signer is refused while a setup is live, and a failed setup is retried. The old provision, confirm-deployment and claim rows collapse into it.

### accounts.setup-process: One durable process per personal Safe; ready at the claim, collection after

- **Question:** The old claim was one Convex action the browser awaited: a ten-minute lock, a recorded submission, a send under the service key's lane, a receipt wait and six store mutations. The designed v4 text adds collecting the claim escrows. What is the target's shape, and when is the account ready?
- **Chosen:** A workflow instance per personal-safes row (claiming, collecting, ready, failed), run after each setup start: the claim through the service signer, a wait for the chain and Ponder, then one collection approval; ready at the claim.
- **Why:** A personal Safe's setup is on the shared list of long-lived objects (`rules.durable-processes`): it waits on the chain and on a person and must survive a crash between broadcast and receipt, which an rpc cannot (A3). The instance is the lock, and the service signer's recorded send is CAP-1097's never-twice rule, so the lock, lane and store mutations go. Only chain proof moves it to `claim` (the claim's CapxulSafeClaimed, or the Safe already owned by exactly this signer), and only the chain's refusal or a send that never left fails it. Ready does not wait on the collection: the Safe is the person's at the claim, the escrows stay theirs to collect or their payer's to refund (D12), and holding sign-in at `setup-required` for an unsigned approval would block everything else.

### accounts.setup-reads: Two setup reads: `account show`'s lifecycle and `account retry`'s check

- **Question:** `account show` prints a failed lifecycle with its step and code; `account retry` refuses a chain-refused setup with WRONG_STATE or INVALID_INPUT (designed); `auth login` and `auth verify` must report the setup and still succeed. One read or several?
- **Chosen:** `accounts.setup.lifecycle` for show (SMART_ACCOUNT_MISSING before any setup) and `accounts.setup.check` for retry; the sign-in flows call check and read its refusals as `setup-required`.
- **Why:** Each command is then one rpc (A4), and the server, not the client, decides each refusal, so `rpc:<id>/errors` sees it. `account show`'s mistake says it refuses before setup with PROFILE_NOT_FOUND or SMART_ACCOUNT_MISSING: the first before the profile, the second after it and before the setup starts (`accounts.profile-before-setup`). The old `loading` lifecycle was the SDK's own state before a session; without a session the target refuses NOT_AUTHENTICATED, so it is gone. A claimed Safe reads `ready` while its escrows are collected. Show takes an optional `personId` that must be the caller's, so its fourth acceptance line stays checkable.

### accounts.sign-in-flow: `auth login` is one flow for a person's code and an agent's own key

- **Question:** `auth login` signs a person in by an emailed code; designed W9 adds `--own-key`, an agent signing a challenge. The old `--own-key` also made an AI's account. One SDK method or two, and what of the account-making flags?
- **Chosen:** `client.auth.sign-in` with both branches; the agent's account is `agent create`'s, so `--own-key` only signs in.
- **Why:** A4: the command calls exactly one SDK method, whichever flags it has. The designed text supersedes the old note that `--own-key` makes an account (P10): `agent create` makes it, and `--name`, `--country` and `--handle` stay the CLI's CLI_USAGE when given without `--own-key`, as the frozen mistake says. Both sign-in flows end by reading the setup, which is why they are accounts' (shared `operations.sign-in-in-accounts`); their refusals are access' rpcs' (INVALID_INPUT, OTP_EXPIRED, NOT_AUTHENTICATED), surfaced once the terminal's tries are spent.

### accounts.stored-states: The waitlist entry and the referral store their state; births are transitions

- **Question:** The old waitlist entry was admitted when `admittedAt` was set, and a referral's stage was the last of four timestamps. P7 makes every status write a named transition with one event. What do the machines look like?
- **Chosen:** A `status` and a `stage` column, with `waitlist-entry` (join, admit, admit_unlisted) and `referral` (attribute, admit, qualify, reward, void); a birth is a transition from no state, so it sends its event too.
- **Why:** P7 sends events only from transitions, and the frozen operations name `waitlist_entry_transitioned` and `referral_transitioned` on joins, admissions, attributions and voids, so each change must be a transition. A birth (`from: []`) is how a row that starts life admitted (`admit_unlisted`) or attributed sends its one event without a fake earlier state. The stage timestamps stay as columns, because `referral list` prints them. The old `self_referral` void reason, which no code produced, is dropped; the old `joined` and `admitted` transition names become verbs.

### accounts.tables: Four tables: the old eight admission and Safe stores shrink to what is used

- **Question:** Old accounts kept accounts, smartAccounts, welcomeFundingGrants and serviceLanes; old access kept betaWaitlist, betaWaitlistWindows, referralCodes and referrals. Which does the target keep, and in what shape?
- **Chosen:** admissions, referral-codes, referrals and personal-safes. The logical Account row, the welcome-grant rows, the service-key lane and the request form's IP window go; no chain id column.
- **Why:** The logical Account (`account_` + ULID bound to the Safe) was a second identity next to the Party; in the target the person's Party in parties is the name money is routed to, and the Safe is a pure function of the email, so the row holds nothing a reader cannot get from parties and personal-safes (P2). Welcome grants are a step of the setup process, whose instance makes them once, so their rows and machine are plumbing (P3). The lane is the service-signer integration's (shared `responsibilities.service-lanes`). The IP window served only the public form, which is not ported. One deployment runs one chain, so a chain column would be the same value in every row. The entry and the code stay apart: a code is made for a person who never joined, and making an entry for them would send a `join` that never happened.

### accounts.welcome-grants: Welcome grants are a staging-only step of the setup, with no table

- **Question:** Old staging minted welcome test tokens into each new Safe through a grants table, a machine and a batch action. Keep them?
- **Chosen:** One step of the setup process, after the claim, on staging with welcome funding on; once per asset by the instance.
- **Why:** The concept map keeps welcome grants. The old table existed to make a mint happen once across repeated provisions and repairs; one process instance per Safe already does that, and a failed mint is in its trace. An Organization's welcome grant, if organizations keeps it, is a step of its own setup.

### accounts.worlds: The devnet worlds accounts' refusals and claims start from

- **Question:** Which named devnet snapshots do accounts' refusals and acceptance lines need?
- **Chosen:** signed-out (nobody signed in here); person-signed-in (access': signed in, not on the list); person-on-waitlist (signed in, on the list, closed beta); person-admitted-no-profile (admitted, no profile); person-unclaimed (admitted, profile done, Safe not set up); person-admitted-no-invites (all invites spent); two-people-admitted (two admitted mailboxes with profiles, one signed in); person-ready (Safe set up); setup-refused-other-signer (claim refused by SwapRefused); setup-refused-non-ascii-email (claim refused by EmailNotNormalized); person-on-waitlist-open-beta (person-on-waitlist with the `beta-access` gate on for everyone).
- **Why:** Names already used by other concepts for the same state (signed-out, person-signed-in, person-on-waitlist, person-unclaimed, person-ready) are reused, since a world is one shared snapshot. Each is made by running operations as the devnet's fixed identities and saved (shared `responsibilities.operator-proof-tools`): sign-in, `waitlist join`, the operator's admission, invites, the web setup flow. The refused setups need the chain to refuse: the first restarts setup with a second signer after a claim landed with the first; the second signs in with a mailbox whose local part is not ASCII (sign-in keeps it, access.email-rule). The open beta is a world, not a `dev gates set` step: that command scripts only Organization gates (devnet.scripted-gates), so the world builder seeds the PostHog fake with `beta-access` on at rollout 100 for everyone before the sync. Only `dev fund`'s WRONG_ENVIRONMENT has no devnet world (`?`). No acceptance claim names a mechanism, so none was rewritten; every operation is `normal`, since no pilot of the spine is among them.

## activity (19)

### activity.acceptance-rewrites: Six acceptance lines rewritten as outcomes

- **Question:** Which of the 28 frozen claims of activity's six operations name a mechanism a black box cannot see?
- **Chosen:** events #3 ('the newest 100 rows miss it': rows and a page size); get #2 ('the stored evidence'); address #1 ('a stored Safe'); address #3 ('writes nothing and schedules nothing'); balance #3 (PostHog and the per-asset event P7 drops); balance #4 ('in the backend').
- **Why:** P1 C: a claim keeps its words unless it names a mechanism, and then it becomes an outcome one of the five observers can check, with the old line in `was`. Each rewrite still passes against the old system: an old Payment's change was read; the payee saw evidence without execution data; the address was lower case and email-derived before setup; a read changed nothing; balance did not wait on PostHog and its wide event named the assets; the refusal was SMART_ACCOUNT_MISSING. The other 22 lines are kept, with what each observer sees.

### activity.address-from-principal: `address` reads the principal's or the Organization's Safe; nothing is derived

- **Question:** Where does `address` get a person's Safe, now that old accounts' smart-account rows are gone?
- **Chosen:** Access' principal row, which holds the email-derived Safe from the first sign-in; the company Safe from organizations.
- **Why:** Access computes a person's Safe from their email with Companies House's pure function when the principal is born, so every signed-in person has it before their Safe is claimed, and it never changes. Reading it is the claim's outcome without the salt setting at read time. An agent's principal holds its own Safe. The answer's chain is the deployment's; the old answer said Base Sepolia even for a row on another chain, and v4 has one chain (P9).

### activity.available: Available is the balance less what is already promised

- **Question:** The old balance answered `available` equal to the balance. What does `available` subtract now?
- **Chosen:** Per asset: the Safe's Payments sent and not settled (submitted), its dated Payments held for their day (scheduled, due), and for a company Safe what its open budgets hold reserved for agreements (`committed`).
- **Why:** concepts.activity places balances above payments because 'available' needs pending payments. A submitted Payment's money has not left yet but will; a dated one is signed or reserved for its day; an agreement's reservation in Budgets is the payee's to collect. An escrowed Payment has already left the Safe, and a prepared one is not signed, so neither is subtracted. Budgets' reservations are chain facts, read from indexing's `chain-budgets`; budgets' own tables hold names only, so the budgets dependency is not needed for it (activity.budgets-through-indexing).

### activity.balances-from-views: A balance is indexing's safe-balances view, refused while Ponder rebuilds

- **Question:** Where does `balance` read a Safe's holdings, and what does it answer before the Safe or the indexer is ready?
- **Chosen:** Indexing's `safe-balances` for the Safe, one position per admitted asset; no `safe-owners` row is SMART_ACCOUNT_MISSING; views not ready is PROVIDER_ERROR.
- **Why:** Indexing holds every Transfer of every admitted asset from its deployment block, so the view is exact and costs one query; a live `balanceOf` per asset is a chain read with its own failures, and the components decision keeps the SDK off the indexer. A send that must not overdraw reads `balanceOf` at build time, which is payments', not this read. A person's `safe-owners` row is the chain's answer to 'is this Safe claimed'. A view that is rebuilding answers less than the chain holds, which is a provider failure to report, not a zero (integrations.indexer).

### activity.budgets-through-indexing: Activity reads what budgets hold through indexing, not budgets' tables

- **Question:** The concept map lets activity read budgets for 'what an Organization's budgets have left'. Does any rpc read budgets' tables?
- **Chosen:** No: the reservation `available` needs is `chain-budgets.committed`, a chain fact in indexing.
- **Why:** Budgets keeps only what the chain cannot name (a budget's key, name and kind); amounts, spends and commitments are chain facts in indexing. `balance` answers totals per asset, not per budget, so no name is needed. The dependency stays on the map for the day a feed row names the budget it was paid from.

### activity.change-log: `activity events` pages a change log activity appends from payments' transitions

- **Question:** `activity events` must answer every change after a cursor exactly once: a change to an old Payment however many rows are newer, and a Movement indexed late. What does it read in order?
- **Chosen:** Activity owns `activity-changes`, one row per change per feed with a gap-free `seq`, appended by small workflows triggered by payments' transitions through the outbox; the read pages it by `seq` and reads each row as it stands.
- **Why:** A time column cannot promise exactly once: a transaction that stamps time T can commit after a reader has read past T, so a reader skips it, which is why the old CLI replayed fifteen minutes and carried up to 500 row versions in its cursor. A `seq` taken under the feed's transaction lock commits in order, so paging by it skips nothing and repeats nothing, and a Movement is noted when it is recorded, not dated by its block. P4 names this shape: a projection that may lag is a workflow in the reacting concept triggered by the other's transition, and payments' events already say activity follows them. A commit-order column would put activity's need into payments' table and indexes. Stored cursors would make every read a write. This is the one store activity owns, the 'change cursor' of concepts.activity.

### activity.dropped: What old activity, balance and treasury code is not ported

- **Question:** Which old activity, accounts and organizations functions have no target record here, and why?
- **Chosen:** activity.overlays.get (responsibilities.overlays); activity.documents.get, verify and render (documents' own `document get`, `verify` and `render`); activity.annotations.save, emitSaved and clearRetired (books' `activity annotate`; telemetry and retired fields go); accounts.balances.readBinding and organizations.treasuries.load (one scope check in each rpc); accounts.summaries.home, me and loadNeedsYou (responsibilities.summaries); the `since` input of items.changes; account_balance_read (P7).
- **Why:** P2: an old function no frozen operation reaches is not ported, and one whose behaviour belongs to another concept moves there. The overlay joined two stores; documents owns content and verification; books owns annotations; the internal binding and load queries were steps of one read, now its first lines. The `since` time input had no CLI flag and is what made changes depend on clocks. A read sends no product event: its wide event is the runtime's (P7).

### activity.followed-transitions: Every Payment transition is a change; of a Movement's, only its birth

- **Question:** Which of payments' transitions does the change log follow?
- **Chosen:** All eleven Payment transitions, and the Movement's `record`.
- **Why:** The frozen page promises 'new Payments, changed Payments and new Movements'. Every Payment transition changes the status a feed row shows, so each is a change a follower must see; skipping one would leave a reader with a stale status. A Movement never changes. A reorg's `remove` and `restore` have no event in the frozen vocabulary: a removed Movement leaves the feed and `activity get` answers null; a restored one was already reported once. The schema's trigger names one transition, so each followed transition is its own small workflow of one shape; two may run out of order, so a feed's first change for a row is `new` and the rest `updated`, and no reader sees a row change before it is born.

### activity.no-procedures: Activity's door has no procedures

- **Question:** Which procedures do other concepts invoke on activity?
- **Chosen:** None: nothing reads or writes activity, and its change log is appended by its own workflows.
- **Why:** A procedure is for a write that is one money fact with its invoker's (rules.procedures); a change log entry is not: it may lag its Payment by a second and can be rebuilt from payments' rows. An append procedure would also make payments depend on activity, which sits above it. The old door exported the annotation read for payments' Movement detail; that read is books' now.

### activity.one-method-for-events: `activity events` is one SDK flow over one rpc; the CLI composes nothing

- **Question:** The old CLI read two SDK methods (changes for Payments, list for Movements) and merged them. What does the target do?
- **Chosen:** One Core SDK flow, `client.activity.changes`: the change read, and with `--wait` a five-second wait between reads.
- **Why:** A4: the CLI calls one SDK method. The change log already holds both kinds of change in one order, so one rpc answers them, up to 5,000 per read as the frozen note says, with `truncated` when more wait. `--wait` waits on the host, so the method is a flow with a `wait` step rather than the rpc itself. The frozen note that it 'reads two SDK calls together' describes the old composition; the text stays as frozen, and the behaviour it promises holds.

### activity.one-read-per-page: A page is one query over payments' three streams, from a fixed checkpoint

- **Question:** Where does a page of activity come from, now that the old SDK's merge of Convex rows and Ponder's answer is gone?
- **Chosen:** One query merging the feed's Payments (owner index), those it receives (payee index) and its Movements (by block time), newest first, from a checkpoint the first page fixes.
- **Why:** Payments is the ledger of record: a Payment row exists before anything is on chain (prepared, scheduled), and Movements are payments' own classification of outside money. Reading chain facts first would miss unsent Payments and re-decide outside money. A fixed checkpoint keeps later pages from repeating or shifting rows. Movements are dated by their block, so `--from` and `--to` mean when money moved; a Movement indexed late appears where its block puts it, and the change feed reports it once. `readWarning` comes from indexing's progress: when Ponder is rebuilding or a minute behind, Movements may be missing and the page says so instead of failing.

### activity.row-ids: A Movement's id is its Transfer and side; a Payment's is payments' id

- **Question:** What id does `activity get --kind movement --id` take, and what does an annotation name?
- **Chosen:** `<chain id>:<tx hash>:<log index>:<side>`, the key of payments' Movement row without its owner.
- **Why:** A Movement is one side of one indexed Transfer for one owner, and payments keys it so; the id repeats that key, so no column or lookup is added, and both sides of an internal Transfer have different ids. Books' annotations name the same string as their reference. Documents names a Movement's Memo by its Transfer alone (`<chain id>:<tx hash>:<log index>`, one Memo for both sides), so `activity get` drops the side to find it. A Payment keeps its `payment_` id.

### activity.row-shapes: Feed rows carry the reader's side only; a Payment in full is payments' own view

- **Question:** What does a feed row and a row in full show, and what does a payee not see?
- **Chosen:** A row is its kind, id, status or direction, an exact amount (asset, token, decimals, raw), the counterparty's label and Safe, the receipt and its times. `activity get` shows a Payment as payments' `PaymentView`, from the reader's side, with chain evidence, the annotation and, for a Movement, its documents.
- **Why:** Amounts stay in raw units of one exact token, so same-peg tokens never merge and WETH keeps 18 decimals. Reusing payments' view keeps `activity get` and `payment get` one answer. A payee sees no approval, proposal or command of the payer's: that is the payer's private execution data. Evidence names the Payments contract that emitted the PaymentSent and what sent it through (the Safe, Budgets, an escrow, Agreements), and `final` replaces the deprecated always-`finalized` fields. Old ledger kinds (Payout, Deposit Payments) are gone with payments' Deposit Payments.

### activity.scope-codes: A scope you are not in answers as each frozen page names it

- **Question:** What does a feed read, `balance` and `address` answer for an Organization the caller has no Seat in?
- **Chosen:** The feed reads (list, summary, events) refuse INVALID_INPUT ('names nothing you can see'); `activity get` answers null, so the CLI says CLI_USAGE; `balance` and `address` refuse ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED.
- **Why:** The surface is the frozen book's (P10 rung 1). The feed pages list no ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED and the old feed refused a foreign scope as invalid input; `activity get` lists only CLI_USAGE for a row 'this session cannot read'; `balance` and `address` name ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED for a foreign `--org`. Each check is the active Seat of the caller's principal, before any row of the Organization is read.

### activity.sealed-cursors: Cursors are sealed by the backend and stored nowhere

- **Question:** How does a read refuse a cursor it did not issue, and where does a reader's position live?
- **Chosen:** Each cursor is an opaque string the backend seals (an HMAC under its cursor key) over the feed, the read that made it and its position: a `seq` for changes; the checkpoint, the last row and the filters for a page.
- **Why:** 'It refuses a cursor it did not issue' needs a check a client cannot fake; the old JSON cursor passed any well-shaped forgery, and one from another feed read that feed's positions. A seal binds the cursor to the feed and the read, so a foreign, forged or cross-read cursor is INVALID_INPUT, as the frozen pages name, and the reader keeps it, so a read writes nothing. The CLI still refuses a value that is not shaped like a cursor with CLI_USAGE before any call. The contacts book's cursors follow the same rule.

### activity.static-links: Documents' table is a plain import, read as payments stores each Memo

- **Question:** activity/links.ts loaded documents' `table_documents` dynamically while documents was being designed. Documents has landed, and payments' Movement record now stores each Movement's Memo (documents.movement-memos). What changes?
- **Chosen:** Delete links.ts and import `table_documents` from ../documents/data.ts in the feed and the decisions.
- **Why:** Activity depends on documents, so a plain import is the conventional link, and a renamed export now fails the typecheck instead of dangling. `activity get` keeps finding a Movement's Memo by the by-subject index on `<chain id>:<tx hash>:<log index>`, which payments.movements.record now writes through `documents.store`.

### activity.unreachable-refusals: Two INVALID_INPUT refusals keep their frozen text and have no world

- **Question:** `address`'s 'the Safe salt namespace setting is malformed' and `balance`'s 'the chain ID is not positive, or the stored Safe does not match the account' name things the target never meets at read time. What becomes of them?
- **Chosen:** Keep both refusals as frozen, produced by input that does not decode, with world `?`.
- **Why:** The frozen surface keeps its codes (P10 rung 1), and INVALID_INPUT is still what either rpc answers for input that does not decode. But the salt namespace is checked when the backend starts and used only when a principal is born, the deployment has one chain so `balance` takes no chain id, and the Safe is the principal's own, so no binding can disagree. No devnet world can reach the conditions named; the gap `refusal-without-world` says so.

### activity.valuation: The USD headline uses books' Chainlink prices, valued by the backend

- **Question:** Which rates value a balance in USD, and who computes the headline?
- **Chosen:** The backend, at the latest rate of each asset's pair in books' `book-prices`.
- **Why:** Books' price sync is the one source of USD rates (P3 keeps the Chainlink cron), and its devnet fake answers the devnet's scripted rates, so devnet and staging value the same way. Rates in the SDK were a second, unsynced copy. Only non-zero positions need a rate; if one has none, both valuations are null and the positions still show.

### activity.worlds: The devnet worlds activity's refusals and claims start from

- **Question:** Which named devnet snapshots do activity's operations need, and which does this concept introduce?
- **Chosen:** New: person-with-activity (a signed-in person with a live Safe: Payments sent, received, to oneself, settled, pending in and cancelled; outside money in and out in USDC, a second USD-pegged token and WETH; one annotated row); person-with-long-payment-history (151 Payments, the oldest still waiting); person-with-over-5000-changes (more than 5,000 changes after a printed cursor). Reused: signed-out, person-ready, person-unclaimed, person-with-funded-safe, person-with-empty-safe, two-people-signed-in, org-with-budgets, person-with-credited-deposit.
- **Why:** A world is one shared snapshot named for what it holds, so names other concepts already made for the same state are reused (payments' funded and empty Safes, organizations' and accounts' person states, access' two people, payments' org-with-budgets with its signed-in outsider, banking's credited deposit). The three new ones are made by running `payment send`, outside transfers from the devnet's funded addresses and `activity annotate` as the devnet's fixed identities, then saved. Two refusals keep `?` (activity.unreachable-refusals).

## agents (24)

### agents.acceptance-rewrites: Two `agent sweep` lines name a mechanism and are rewritten as outcomes

- **Question:** Which of agents' 26 frozen acceptance lines name a mechanism a black box cannot see (P1)?
- **Chosen:** sweep #3 ('only when the provider supplies the operation's logs; omitted or null logs') and sweep #5 ('Aborting waitChange during its polling sleep'); the other 24 stay word for word.
- **Why:** Sweep #3 is about the old provider's log payload, which the target does not have: settlement reads the indexed facts of the carrier's transaction. Its outcome stays: amounts are the actual ones, an emptied token reports zero, and a wrong or doubled event fails the change. Sweep #5 names an SDK method and its sleep; the outcome is that cancelling the follow answers CANCELLED and leaves the sweep waiting. Both still hold against the old system. Lines that name the CLI or the SDK as the actor (rotate #4 to #6, sweep #6) are observable at the command and stay.

### agents.account-at-first-sign-in: An agent's accountId is access' principal, null until its first sign-in

- **Question:** The old create minted the agent's accountId at once. Who makes the agent's Account in the target?
- **Chosen:** Access makes the agent's principal at its first signed challenge; agents reads it by the agent's Safe and answers null until then.
- **Why:** The frozen meaning of `accountId` is 'the Account the agent signs in to; it is bound at the agent's first sign-in', and access owns principals and has no procedure for another concept to write one (access.no-procedures). An id minted here would be a principal access does not know. Reading by Safe needs nothing new: principals are unique by Safe. `agent create`'s answer reads `accountId` null, and `agent show` names it once the agent has signed in.

### agents.chain-proof: A change lands only on its own AgentOwner event in its carrier's transaction

- **Question:** How does the process know a change landed: the carrier's success alone, AgentOwner events as triggers, or the carrier's transaction's facts?
- **Chosen:** When the carrier landed, read every fact of its transaction (`indexer.factsOf`, at finality) and land only on the event the change asked for, for this Safe, key, tokens and recipient; anything else is a mismatch.
- **Why:** The frozen claims bind the event to the approved operation ('a landing whose event names another key fails'; 'settlement binds the successful receipt to the approved operation and owner, checks each event's Safe, token and recipient'). An event trigger alone cannot tell whose operation emitted it, so a sweep landed by another path would settle the wrong change; the carrier's success alone would land a sweep that moved to another recipient. Reading the carrier's transaction is the one settlement path approvals and budgets use too. So AgentCreated, AgentFrozen, AgentUnfrozen, AgentSignerSwapped and AgentSwept are read, not triggers; AgentRemoved also triggers organizations.

### agents.create-one-transaction: The birth is one transaction: agent, Party, change and carrier

- **Question:** The old create reserved the handle, read the chain, built and sponsored the operation, then recorded the agent and approval in a second mutation. What is the target create?
- **Chosen:** One mutation: checks, then `parties.register`, the agent `creating`, its create change `waiting`, and the carrier (`approvals.open` or `board.propose`); approvals' process sponsors it afterwards.
- **Why:** A handle and its Safe are one money fact (dependencies.parties-registration), and so are a chain write and its carrier (rules.procedures): a create that registered its Party but not its carrier would hold a handle for nothing. Sponsorship is approvals' (concepts.approvals), so the create needs no outside call, and the predicted Safe is a pure function (agents.safe-address-in-process). Two procedures from two concepts per transaction (parties and approvals, or parties and board) stays within sys:tx-width.

### agents.designed-surface: Where a designed block exists, its who and its refusal codes are the operation's

- **Question:** Six agent operations carry a designed (v4) block whose `who` and refusals differ from the top-level text. Which wins?
- **Chosen:** The designed `who`, and the union of the top-level and designed refusals, each reached from its own world.
- **Why:** P10 rung 1, as payments.designed-surface reads it. The designed text adds what v4 can answer: a Director for `--org`, the agent reading itself, an empty Safe as WRONG_STATE. The top-level refusals stay where they still hold (SMART_ACCOUNT_MISSING, a second waiting change), so each operation lists both, a code twice when two worlds reach it in different ways. The SDK method `agent show` names (`client.me.agents.get` designed, `show` top-level) does not matter here: the operation is one rpc.

### agents.events: One event for the agent's state, one for every change of it

- **Question:** The old `agent_transitioned` carried created, frozen, unfrozen, swept, key_rotated and removed. How does it map onto one event per machine?
- **Chosen:** agent_transitioned for the agent's state; agent_change_transitioned (with `kind`) for every change, so a sweep and a rotation are proved there.
- **Why:** A sweep and a key rotation change no agent state, so as agent transitions they would be self-loops that send an event without a status change. As landings of a change they are real transitions (waiting to landed) with their kind on the event. The create's `from` is null at birth on both, and a failure carries its code.

### agents.failure-codes: How a change's end maps onto the shared codes

- **Question:** A carrier can end eight ways without the change landing. Which code does each answer with?
- **Chosen:** Someone said no (declined, withdrawn, a Board rejection): CANCELLED. It can never run (expired, displaced, a replaced proposal): WRONG_STATE. The chain ran it and it did not do what was asked (reverted, a failed proposal, a mismatch): TRANSACTION_FAILED. A sponsorship refusal keeps the approval's own code.
- **Why:** CANCELLED already covers a person declining on the approval page (vocabulary: SIGNER_REJECTED merged), and a Board's rejection is the same no. A lapse or a used nonce is a state that no longer allows the change. A revert or a landing without its event means the chain refused what was asked, which is TRANSACTION_FAILED's message. A refusal at sponsorship already carries the right code (NotOwner is ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED, integrations.sponsor-refusals).

### agents.flows: One flow per change command; it makes the key, signs, hands over and follows

- **Question:** Which agent operations are flows, and what does each flow own?
- **Chosen:** create, freeze, unfreeze, rotate-key, sweep and remove are flows: prepare (one rpc), sign at once with a key on this machine or hand over the approval page, follow live until the change ends. create and rotate-key make the key here and keep it before anything is prepared. list and show are one rpc each.
- **Why:** A4: the SDK owns every flow and all signing, and the CLI calls one SDK method. The old SDK returned at the approval and left the CLI to call `wait`, `waitChange`, `approvals.approve` and its own key file (four calls per command). Making the key in the flow keeps the frozen guarantee that only its address leaves the machine (rotate #4), and keeping it before preparing means a create that lands always has its key. An Organization's change hands over the proposing Director's own approval that board.propose opens.

### agents.key-of-its-own: A new key must be a key of its own, checked before anything is signed

- **Question:** Which keys may an agent be created or rotated to?
- **Chosen:** Not the zero address, AgentOwner, the owner's Safe or signer, any Party's Safe, the agent's key now, or the key of another live agent (`by-signer`): INVALID_INPUT `signer`.
- **Why:** The frozen claims say a key that is not a key of its own is refused before anything is signed (rotate #3) and name its cases (rotate INVALID_INPUT). AgentOwner refuses only the zero address, the sentinel, itself and the owner Safe, so a key shared with another agent would pass on chain and let one key sign for two accounts, and access' sign-in could not tell which agent signs in. A failed or removed agent's key no longer counts as live.

### agents.live-balances: Sweep, remove and show read the agent's balances live from the chain

- **Question:** What the agent's Safe holds: from indexing's safe-balances view, or `balanceOf` at `latest`?
- **Chosen:** chain-rpc `readContract` of `balanceOf` per settlement asset, in parallel, before the transaction; PROVIDER_ERROR when the node does not answer.
- **Why:** The frozen text says the balances are 'read from the chain' and PROVIDER_ERROR 'when the chain does not answer. No balance is made up', and the sweep's empty refusal decides whether anything is signed: a view behind Ponder's head could refuse a funded Safe or show a stale amount on the page. A handful of parallel reads fits the chain-read class. The amounts on the page are each balance at preparation; the sweep moves whatever the Safe holds at execution, and settlement reports that.

### agents.machines: Frozen and removed are states; a change has no `sent`

- **Question:** The old agent stored creating, active and failed and read frozen and removed from two flags; a change had waiting, sent, landed and failed. What are the target machines?
- **Chosen:** agent: creating, active, frozen, removed, failed, each write a named transition. agent-change: waiting, landed, failed; the carrier's own states (sponsoring, signed, sent; awaiting approvals) are the carrier's.
- **Why:** P7 makes every write to a status a transition that sends its machine's one event; a flag written beside the state would be a status change with no event, and the funnel could not see a freeze. `sent` and its hand-back to waiting were the old send relay's; the approval or the proposal now carries the send, and the change only waits for its carrier's end. Removed and failed are terminal: AgentOwner refuses every owner power on a removed agent, and a failed create never had a Safe.

### agents.no-door: Agents' door has no procedures

- **Question:** Which procedures do other concepts invoke on agents?
- **Chosen:** None: no concept above agents writes an agent or a change with a money fact of its own.
- **Why:** A procedure exists only for one money fact (rules.procedures). Concepts that pay or name an agent name its Party, whose state parties already keeps (`pending` until the create lands); organizations ends Seats from the chain (dependencies.agent-seats); access proves an agent's sign-in on chain (concepts.access).

### agents.one-process: One process per change replaces the follows and the settle-on-read

- **Question:** The old system settled agents in seven places: get, show and every change action read receipts first, two self-rescheduling follows, and two signed-send actions. What survives?
- **Chosen:** `agents.changes.carry`, one instance per change, triggered by its birth and resumed by its carrier's end through the outbox; reads never settle; approvals and board own the send.
- **Why:** A3 makes each long-lived object one process that owns its machine, and rules.durable-processes lists an agent change. A read that writes is a read no caller can trust to be cheap, and it made the old list show a lapsed create as creating until someone read it (as-is finding). The follow loops (every ten seconds, sixty reads) were the old scheduler's; the carrier's own transition wakes this process. With the process, `agent create` #3 ('whether or not anyone reads it') holds by construction. Not ported: load, load-owner, load-change, record, record-submission, clear-submission, settle, record-change, record-change-submission, clear-change-submission, settle-change, send-signed, send-change-signed, emit-transitioned, follow and follow-change; the agent's own EntryPoint lane becomes each approval's own nonce key.

### agents.one-waiting-change: One change waits for an agent at a time, the create included

- **Question:** The frozen notes say only one change waits for an agent at a time. How is that kept, and does it hold for an Organization's slow proposals?
- **Chosen:** A unique index on (owner, agent) among waiting changes; the create is a change too, so nothing else is opened while it waits.
- **Why:** The frozen refusals of freeze, unfreeze, rotate, sweep and remove all say another waiting change is WRONG_STATE, and a unique index makes it hold under concurrency. Two waiting changes could also land in either order on chain and leave the agent in a state neither asked for. A person's approval lapses within the paymaster's window, so it never blocks for long; a Board that must freeze while a proposal waits rejects that proposal first (board's reject), which ends the change and frees the agent. The surface wins (P10 rung 1).

### agents.org-owned: An Organization can own an agent; every change to it is a Board proposal

- **Question:** The top-level text of `agent create` refuses `--org` with FEATURE_NOT_AVAILABLE; its designed v4 text has a Director create the agent for the Organization. Which is built?
- **Chosen:** Build it: with `--org` the company Safe is the AgentOwner owner, and the create and every later change is one Board proposal (`board.propose`) whose proposing Director's approval comes with it. FEATURE_NOT_AVAILABLE stays reachable through the proposal's own `owner-safe-proposals` gate.
- **Why:** P10 rung 1: where a designed block exists its v4 text is the surface (P9), and it names `--org`, a Director as who, and ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED for a non-Director. The concept map put agents above board for exactly this (concepts.agents, dependencies.of-agents), and AgentOwner takes any Safe as owner. Keeping the frozen refusal as a gate costs nothing: a Board proposal needs the Organization's proposals gate, so an Organization without it still hears FEATURE_NOT_AVAILABLE. The top-level note that says it is not available yet is the old build's status, kept in the frozen text.

### agents.removal-seats: A removal names the Seats it ends; organizations ends them from the chain

- **Question:** The designed removal ends every Seat the agent holds and reports `seatsEnded`. Who ends them, and what does the removal report?
- **Chosen:** Organizations ends them from AgentRemoved (dependencies.agent-seats); the removal names the Seats its Safe held at preparation and reports their count.
- **Why:** A removal is final only when AgentOwner.remove lands, so the Seats follow the chain (dependencies.agent-seats), and organizations sits below agents, so agents could not be invoked from there anyway. The page names the Seats so the owner sees what leaves before approving; the frozen note 'agents cannot hold Seats yet' is the old build's status.

### agents.remove-names-every-asset: A removal names every settlement asset, held or not

- **Question:** The old removal named only the tokens its Safe held at preparation. What does `AgentOwner.remove(safe, tokens)` name in the target?
- **Chosen:** Every settlement asset of the deployment; AgentOwner skips an empty one with no event.
- **Why:** A removal is final: money that arrives in a settlement asset between preparation and execution would otherwise be left in a Safe no key can sign for again, and only AgentOwner could ever move it. Naming every asset still names every asset its Safe holds (remove #1), and AgentOwner's sweep returns zero without a call for an empty token (AgentOwner.sol:351). P10 rung 3: the stricter version wins for money.

### agents.roster: `agent list` adds a Director's Organizations' agents; `agent show` adds Seats

- **Question:** What do `agent list` and `agent show` read, now that an Organization can own an agent and an agent can hold a Seat?
- **Chosen:** list: the caller's own agents, then those of each Organization they are a Director of, each with its owner. show: the agent, its live positions and its active Seats with the hats its Safe wears; no balances in list.
- **Why:** A Director acts for an Organization's agents (agents.who-acts), so they must be able to find them; adding them keeps list #1 and #2 true (another person's agents are still never listed). The designed show names 'the Seats it holds and what each Seat was given', which organizations' Seats and indexing's hat views answer without a copy. The designed example's 'signed in 2 minutes ago' is access' session of another tenant (the agent), which row-level security does not show the owner, so it is left out.

### agents.safe-address-in-process: The agent's Safe is AgentOwner's pure address, computed in-process

- **Question:** The old create read `AgentOwner.safeAddressFor` with an eth_call. How does the target name the agent's Safe before it exists?
- **Chosen:** Compute it in-process: CREATE2 of the canonical Safe 1.5.0 proxy factory, the fixed initializer and salt keccak256(owner, signer), at the deployment's AgentOwner address.
- **Why:** `safeAddressFor` is pure over constants and AgentOwner's own address (AgentOwner.sol:327), as organizations found for the company Safe (companySafeAddressFor). Computing it keeps the create one write with no node call. A mistake cannot misroute money: the Party stays `pending` until AgentCreated names that very Safe, and a mismatch fails the create; a conformance eval checks the computation against the contract on the devnet.

### agents.sweep-recipient: A sweep's recipient is resolved in the sweep's own transaction

- **Question:** The old sweep resolved `--to` through parties' public resolver as a separate read. How is the recipient bound in the target?
- **Chosen:** `parties.resolve-recipient` inside the sweep's transaction, so the change and the Party its money routes to commit together.
- **Why:** parties.resolve-recipient names 'a sweep's destination' among its invokers: a recipient and the Safe its money routes to are one money fact, and an email no Party holds yet is named at its derived Safe in the same transaction, so the page, the change and the chain agree on whose Safe it is. Omitted, the owner's own Safe is the recipient, so a person's sweep and an Organization's sweep both come home.

### agents.tables: Two tables: the agent and its changes; the old copies go

- **Question:** Old accounts kept `agents` and `agentChanges` with copies of other concepts' facts and send plumbing. What does the target keep?
- **Chosen:** `agents` (whose agent, its Safe, its key now, where the key is kept, its state) and `agent-changes` (one row per change: what it asked, its carrier, what the chain made of it). Name and handle are the Party's, the accountId access', the owner's email and Safe the owner's own rows; the operation hashes, submission notes, expiries, lanes and the frozen and removed flags go.
- **Why:** A copy can disagree with its source: the old row's handle could outlive a released Party, its frozen flag disagree with its state. Every copied fact has one owner in the target (parties, access, accounts, organizations), and agents depends on each. The send notes (`userOpHash`, submission and hand-back) were the old relay's, and approvals and board now own the one send (P3: plumbing goes). A change is its own durable object (rules.durable-processes lists an agent change), with its own history, so it needs its own row; an inline last change would lose what earlier changes did. Both tables are tenant `owner` because a person or an Organization can own an agent (P8), and every row carries its owner, so nothing is scoped by a join.

### agents.transaction-width: An Organization's create or sweep spans parties, board and approvals

- **Question:** sys:tx-width allows at most 3 procedures from at most 2 other concepts per transaction. An Organization-owned create or sweep invokes parties and board, and board.propose itself invokes approvals. Is that within the rule?
- **Chosen:** Count the procedures a transaction invokes directly: 2 from 2 concepts (parties' register or resolve-recipient, and approvals.open or board.propose). Board's own approvals.open is board's, and a reviewer reads it as such.
- **Why:** Each write is forced by a shared decision: a birth registers its Party in its own transaction (dependencies.parties-registration), a chain write is born with its carrier (rules.procedures), and a Board proposal opens its proposer's approval (board.propose). Splitting any of them would leave a held handle, a named recipient or a proposal without the intent it carries. A person's create or sweep is 2 procedures from 2 concepts either way. Assembly: rules.transaction-width closes the open question this left: one run counts what it invokes itself, on its widest branch, and a door's own invocations count against the door, so both are within sys:tx-width.

### agents.who-acts: The owner or a Director acts; not yours and none are one refusal

- **Question:** Who may change or read an agent, and how is someone else's agent refused?
- **Chosen:** A person-owned agent: its owner. An Organization-owned agent: a Director, whose own Safe is an owner of the company Safe on chain (indexing's safe-owners). `agent show` also admits the agent itself. Anyone else, and a ref that names no agent, get the same ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED.
- **Why:** AgentOwner's own check is `msg.sender == owner`: only the owner's Safe acts, and an Organization's Safe acts only through its Board, so the Directors are who acts for it; reading the company Safe's owners from the chain is the same truth board.propose checks. One refusal for not-yours and none is the frozen rule ('so a handle never tells who owns it'). The agent may read itself (designed `agent show` who: 'or the agent itself'), found by its principal's Safe. A member who is no Director reads the Organization's agents only through its Seats.

### agents.worlds: Three worlds reach agents' refusals and claims

- **Question:** Which devnet snapshots does agents introduce, and what does each hold?
- **Chosen:** person-with-agents: Dana (dana@acme.test, claimed Safe) owns @ledger (active, 312.50 USDC and 20.00 EURC), @spare (active, empty Safe), @paused (frozen) and @gone (removed); Sam (sam@acme.test, claimed Safe) owns @scribe. person-with-waiting-agent-change: that world with a freeze of @ledger and an unfreeze of @paused each waiting for Dana's approval. org-with-agent: Acme with Directors Dana and Lee (threshold 1), the proposals gate on, a member Max who is no Director, and @acme-bot, which Acme owns. Reused: signed-out, and accounts' person-unclaimed.
- **Why:** One world with one agent in each stable state reaches most refusals by the handle the command names, and restores fast. Waiting changes change what every write refuses and their approvals lapse in minutes, so they are their own world, saved with the devnet clock stopped. An Organization's agent needs a Board, a non-Director and the gate. The chain-rpc fault switch and the gate switch, not more worlds, reach PROVIDER_ERROR and FEATURE_NOT_AVAILABLE.

## applications (23)

### applications.acceptance-rewrites: Three acceptance lines that named a mechanism, rewritten as outcomes

- **Question:** Which frozen claims name something a black box cannot see, and what do they become?
- **Chosen:** `doctor` #3's 'the row' becomes its trace; `key revoke` #3 and `key rotate` #5's 'before restoring a session' become 'before any call, whether or not anyone is signed in here'.
- **Why:** P1. 'The row' is the old backend's telemetry row, which the target replaces with traces; the trace observer sees the same mode. Restoring a session is a CLI step no observer sees; what it guaranteed is that a script without `--confirm` fails as usage before touching anything, signed in or not. Both rewrites pass against the old system: its CLI refused before the session read, so it refused alike with no one signed in. Every other line is word for word.

### applications.budgets: Budgets: class defaults, and one override for `doctor`

- **Question:** Which budget class does each piece take, and where are the defaults wrong?
- **Chosen:** Class defaults everywhere: reads `read`, writes and the two HTTP routes `write`, the grace process `background`; `doctor` overrides `read`.
- **Why:** P5. The bootstrap is a transaction that may write the stamp, so its server share is a write's. `doctor --online` makes two backend calls in sequence, the bootstrap and then the health check at the backend the envelope names, so it gets two reads' time (1,000 ms and 2,000 ms); offline it is local and far inside that. `key rotate` and `key revoke` keep the write default and exclude the person's confirmation.

### applications.dev-key-flow: `dev key` is a flow in the Core SDK's Node-only test entry

- **Question:** The frozen note says `dev key` has no public SDK method and its SDK layer stays unmapped. A4 says the CLI calls one SDK method. Which holds?
- **Chosen:** A flow in the Core SDK's Node-only test entry, beside the devnet entry: an SDK method, but not one of the public client's.
- **Why:** A4 and the CLI component: the CLI calls no backend route itself (the old CLI reached around the SDK here, a finding of the foundation review). Putting the flow in the Node-only test entry keeps the frozen note true, since no public client gains a method, and keeps file writing out of browsers. It works on staging too, so it is not the devnet entry, which talks to the supervisor.

### applications.doctor-exit: `doctor` exits 124 at a deadline, and a wrong echo is an unusable answer

- **Question:** How does `doctor --online` fail at its deadline, and when the backend's answer does not echo the nonce?
- **Chosen:** NETWORK_ERROR with mode `timeout` and exit 124 at either deadline; a missing or wrong echo is PROVIDER_RESPONSE_INVALID.
- **Why:** Decision vocabulary.doctor-deadline-exit leaves NETWORK_ERROR at exit 5 everywhere else and asks this concept to book `doctor`'s 124, which its frozen notes and acceptance name: the CLI maps the flow's deadline failure to 124. An answer that arrived but does not echo the run's nonce is an answer Capxul cannot use, which is what PROVIDER_RESPONSE_INVALID means; PROVIDER_ERROR says the service did not complete, which it did.

### applications.dropped: Old applications plumbing that is not rebuilt

- **Question:** Which other old applications functions have no place in the target?
- **Chosen:** The two CORS preflights (the runtime's), admit-key and record-use (steps of the bootstrap), claim (a step of the mint), issue and revoke-by-id (operator scripts), trusts-origin (applications.origin-token) and emit-transitioned (P7).
- **Why:** Convex split one request into a query, a mutation and an action (P3); on Node the bootstrap and the mint each read and write in one transaction. A preflight is an HTTP answer the runtime gives every route. `issue` and `revoke-by-id` were run by hand with `convex run` and no frozen operation reaches them (P2). The telemetry action is replaced by the transition's event in the outbox (P7, rules.outbox).

### applications.envelope-from-deployment: The bootstrap envelope comes from the deployment, not from the key's row

- **Question:** Each old application and key row copied the deployment's routing and vendor keys at mint. Keep the copies?
- **Chosen:** Store no routing or vendor keys: the bootstrap builds the envelope from this deployment's settings when it is called.
- **Why:** A key belongs to one deployment and can only ever be resolved by it, so what it serves is that deployment's settings now. The copies were why the old concept needed `backfillMetadata` and `migrateNativeRouting` (repairs that rewrote every row when a URL or a vendor key changed) and why a registration made through the old rpc carried placeholder routing. With no copies there is nothing to drift and nothing to repair. The envelope is version 2: one backend origin replaces the Convex, site and auth URLs, and the indexer URL goes, since the SDK never calls Ponder.

### applications.flows: Which commands are flows, and one owner input for both accounts

- **Question:** Which of the six operations are one rpc, which are Core SDK flows, and how does `--org` reach the SDK?
- **Chosen:** `key create` and `key list` are one rpc each; `key rotate` and `key revoke` (confirm, then one rpc), `doctor` and `dev key` are flows; the owner is an input.
- **Why:** A4: the CLI calls one SDK method and the SDK owns every step past the command line. Rotate and revoke ask at a terminal before their write, as contacts' guided commands do; doctor composes local reads with two backend calls; dev key checks and writes a file around one call. The owner input replaces the old `client.org(id).keys` twin of `client.account.keys`. `createCapxulClient` serves the web surfaces and has no operation; its flow is what every browser runs first.

### applications.grace-process: A rotated key's grace window is its own durable process

- **Question:** What moves a key from grace to expired when its window ends?
- **Chosen:** One workflow per rotated key, started by `rotate`: a timer to `grace_ends_at`, then `expire`; a revoke signals it to end.
- **Why:** A window is a date, and anything that waits on a date is a process with its own timer (A3, P3); a new sweep is not one of P3's crons. Only rotated keys have a window, so most keys never have an instance. The bootstrap and `key list` still treat a grace key past `grace_ends_at` as expired, so a timer that fires late never lengthens a window. A revoke during the window would otherwise leave the instance waiting up to 30 days, so the revoke rpc signals it.

### applications.health-only: Of old operator's diagnostics, only the health check is ported

- **Question:** Old diagnostics answered health, the environment, the business clock and a devnet's chain head. Which land here?
- **Chosen:** Only the health check, for `doctor --online`, with the deployed commit added.
- **Why:** P2: `doctor` is the frozen operation that reaches diagnostics, and it calls only health. The environment answer reported an e2e auth gate the target does not have; devnet time and its offset are devnet's (`dev time`); the chain head is indexing's progress. Health now names the commit as well as the deployment, which is what an operator asks after a deploy, and it still reads nothing, so it answers even when a dependency is down.

### applications.invoicing-hat: An Organization's keys: any member reads them, the Invoicing hat changes them

- **Question:** Who may list, create, rotate and revoke an Organization's keys, and how is it checked?
- **Chosen:** Read: a current Seat (organizations' members). Write: that Seat's Safe wears the Organization's Invoicing hat now, named by its deployment and worn in indexing's hat wearers.
- **Why:** The frozen `who` lines say 'for an Organization, a member who holds issue invoices' for every change and 'any member' for the list. In v4 issuing invoices is the Invoicing officer hat, and applications depends on organizations and indexing, so it reads the hat id and its wearers directly, as requests does; a procedure is only for a money fact (rules.procedures). A key for someone else's Organization is ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED before anything is read.

### applications.key-format: Key format, hash and environment stay as they were

- **Question:** What does a publishable key look like, what is stored, and when is it `live`?
- **Chosen:** `cap_pk_test_` or `cap_pk_live_` and 32 Crockford characters (160 random bits); store its SHA-256, prefix and last four; `test` on devnet and staging, `live` on production.
- **Why:** The format is what developers paste into `.env.local` and what the SDK checks before any request, so keeping it keeps their apps working with the rebuilt SDK. A random 160-bit value needs no salt or key: a hash cannot be reversed or guessed, and a MAC would only add a secret to rotate. The target deploys devnet and staging, so every key it mints is `test`; `live` stays on the wire because `key list` shows each key's environment.

### applications.key-list-pages: `key list` pages from the server, newest first by an index

- **Question:** The old list read every key of every application and sorted them in memory, in one page. Keep that?
- **Chosen:** One query on (owner, created_at, key_id), a limit and an opaque cursor bound to the owner, as the frozen `next` says.
- **Why:** The frozen returns name `next`, where the next page starts, which the old code never filled. An index in the list's order makes the page one range read, and the key id, a ULID, breaks a tie inside one millisecond, which is the frozen claim the old code met by sorting on storage insertion time.

### applications.key-machine: A key's state is a stored status with five named transitions

- **Question:** Old keys derived active, grace, expired and revoked at read time from two timestamps. How is it kept now?
- **Chosen:** A `status` column on the key, the machine `app-key` with create, rotate, retire, expire and revoke, and one event.
- **Why:** P7: every write to a status is a named transition, with the machine's one event. Derived, the end of a grace window changed nothing anyone could observe, and the two old readers disagreed for one millisecond at the boundary. `retire` is `--grace 0`, which the old code stored as a window ending now. The machine is `app-key` so its event keeps the old funnel name, `app_key_transitioned`, now sent by every transition through the outbox instead of by a scheduled telemetry action.

### applications.no-bootstrap-counter: No per-key bootstrap counter; the last use is stamped once a minute

- **Question:** The old bootstrap counted every call on the key row (60 a minute) and stamped its last use. Keep both?
- **Chosen:** Keep the last-use stamp, written by one conditional update at most once a minute; drop the per-key counter.
- **Why:** `key list` shows when a key was last used, so the stamp stays. The counter made every bootstrap a write and capped a real app at 60 browser starts a minute for all its users, which is a defect, not a limit; the protocol's per-origin limit was never applied at all. An envelope lasts five minutes and the SDK caches it, so a well-behaved client bootstraps rarely, and flooding an unauthenticated route is the deployment edge's to stop, as for every route.

### applications.no-consent: No application consent table

- **Question:** The concept map gives applications a person's consent to an application (old access' `userAppConsents`). Rebuild it?
- **Chosen:** No consent table: nothing in the target writes or reads one.
- **Why:** P2. Its one writer was the native client accept of the device flow, which is not ported (access.no-device-flow), and its one reader outside access was operator's probes. An assistant's consent is access's assistant grant, and the bootstrap grants no consent and no authority: a person signs in and acts on their own Safe. If a later frozen operation needs an application's consent, it lands here, by this concept's map entry.

### applications.no-door: Applications' door has no procedures

- **Question:** The old door exported a credential hash, an owned-application read, the native client metadata and an observation hint. Keep any?
- **Chosen:** None: no money fact writes applications' tables, and nothing below it may read them.
- **Why:** A procedure is for one money fact (rules.procedures), and a key is not one. The credential hash was a pure function access borrowed; access hashes its own secrets. The owned-application read served operator purges, which worlds replace. The native client metadata served the device flow (access.no-device-flow). The observation hint had no caller. The constants and HTTP helpers the door also exported are the runtime's.

### applications.no-unowned-registrations: No unowned applications: the devnet seed, host proofs and native clients go

- **Question:** The devnet seed, disposable host proofs and the staging CLI's pinned native client registered applications with no owner. Keep them?
- **Chosen:** Every application has an owner; worlds make keys through `key create`; the CLI needs no pinned key.
- **Why:** P8 has no tenant for them. The devnet seed is replaced by worlds, restored snapshots made by running the frozen commands (P8's evals restore worlds). The host-proof applications existed to be retired by the repairs (responsibilities.repairs). The pinned native client served the device flow, which is gone (access.no-device-flow), and its key value was written in the code.

### applications.origin-token: The bootstrap hands the browser a short-lived origin token

- **Question:** A key lets a web app sign people in, so the backend must accept requests from the key's origins. Sign-in once asked applications whether a live key listed the origin. Access now sits below applications. How does the origin reach it?
- **Chosen:** The bootstrap answers a token, Capxul's MAC over the key id, the admitted origin and an expiry five minutes on; the runtime admits that origin while the token is good.
- **Why:** Access must depend on nothing (concepts.access), so it cannot read applications' tables, and a per-request read would put a table lookup on every browser call. The token is checked with no read, and its five minutes match the envelope's life, so a revoked or expired key stops working at the app's next bootstrap, as `key revoke` promises ('Apps that use it stop at their next sign-in'). TRUSTED_ORIGINS still names Capxul's own pages. The old envelope's `bsk_` session token, which nothing stored or checked, is gone.

### applications.origins-on-the-application: Origins belong to the application, and every key follows them

- **Question:** Old keys copied their application's origins at mint and kept an origin index. Where do origins live now?
- **Chosen:** On the application only; the bootstrap reads them through the key's application, and every key follows a change.
- **Why:** `--origin` sets the web origins of the app (the frozen text), so they are the app's. The per-key copy meant a key made before an origin change kept the old origins, and a rotation's successor took the app's current ones while its predecessor kept its own, which no one could see from `key list`. The origin index existed only for sign-in's trusted-origin read, which the origin token replaces (applications.origin-token). The bootstrap already reads the key, so its application is one more read by primary key.

### applications.owner-tables: Applications and keys are owner rows, each carrying its own owner

- **Question:** How are a person's and an Organization's applications and keys stored under P8?
- **Chosen:** `applications` and `publishable-keys`, both tenancy `owner` (`owner_kind` person or organization, `owner_id`), the owner on every key row.
- **Why:** Every key command runs the same way for a person and for an Organization (`--org`), so one pair of tables and one set of rpcs serve both, with the `owner` tenancy P8 gives a row either can own. The old key row had no tenant column and was scoped through its application, which P8 forbids (tenancy by join); here a key carries its owner, so its row-level security needs no join. `owner_id` leads the key and every index with `owner_kind` second, as contacts does. The one lookup across owners, a raw key's hash at bootstrap, is a unique crossTenant index.

### applications.quickstart-keys: Quickstart keys are anonymous rows of their own, counted by address

- **Question:** `dev key` mints a key with no sign-in. Where does an unowned key live, and how is the mint capped?
- **Chosen:** A `global` table of quickstart keys, each with one loopback origin and a keyed hash of the minting address; the mint counts that address's rows in the last hour.
- **Why:** P8 has no tenant for a row nobody owns: an unowned key in the owner tables would be a hole in their row-level security, and a pseudo-owner a tenant that is not one. A quickstart key can never be listed, rotated or revoked, so it shares nothing with an owned key but the bootstrap's lookup, which reads both tables by a unique hash. Counting the keys an address minted in the last hour replaces the window table, which kept a row per address forever and stored the raw address; the hash keeps it unreadable. The route refuses WRONG_ENVIRONMENT outside a devnet or staging, where the old one minted a production backend's test key.

### applications.registrations-in-keys: No registrations rpcs: `key create` makes and updates applications

- **Question:** Old registrations had a list, a create and an internal register. Port them?
- **Chosen:** Drop the three rpcs; `key create` makes the application it names, or replaces its origins.
- **Why:** P2: no SDK method, CLI command or frozen operation called the registrations list or create, and the internal register served only the quickstart mint, the devnet seed and operator scripts, which no longer register applications (applications.no-unowned-registrations). `key create --app --origin` is the frozen way to make or change an application.

### applications.worlds: The worlds applications' refusals and claims are reached from

- **Question:** Which named devnet snapshots do the six operations need?
- **Chosen:** Reuse access's `empty-devnet` and `person-signed-in`; add `people-with-keys`, `person-at-key-limit`, `org-with-keys` and `quickstart-quota-spent`.
- **Why:** `people-with-keys`: two people signed in; the first holds an active key, a key in its grace window and a revoked key, two of them made in one millisecond, the second one active key; one quickstart key. `person-at-key-limit`: a person holding 20 active keys. `org-with-keys`: the Organization acme with one key; Ivy wears its Invoicing hat, Max is a member without it, Olu is outside it, all signed in here. `quickstart-quota-spent`: one address minted 15 quickstart keys in the last hour. A world per case keeps each diff small.

## approvals (18)

### approvals.budgets: Budgets at their class defaults

- **Question:** Which budget class does each approvals record take?
- **Chosen:** get live-read; review, sign and decline write; the page flow vendor (the Openfort signer); the process background. No overrides.
- **Why:** None of the signer rpcs calls out: recovering a key is local, and sponsorship and the send are the process's. The flow's only outside calls are the signer's, and the person's wait is excluded. The chain-submit budget belongs to the operations that compose these rpcs; the process's system time (two node reads, sponsorship, one send, one facts read) fits the background default.

### approvals.chain-proof: The indexed event at its nonce ends a signed approval; an unmoved nonce expires

- **Question:** What proves how a signed approval ended, and when is it final?
- **Chosen:** The UserOperationEvent at its sender and nonce, read through the indexer at the finality depth: its hash lands or reverts it, another displaces it; at the window plus two minutes an unmoved nonce (getNonce) expires it.
- **Why:** The EntryPoint event is the chain's own answer, and it names whichever operation used the nonce, so one event decides the approval it landed and every approval it displaced, with no sweep and no `unknown` state. At finality a landing is never reversed, so approvals needs no reorg path; Payments still reverse their own facts. The window's 2-minute grace is the old one, and an expiry needs the nonce unmoved, so 'chain proof overrides a ledger expiry' can no longer happen. Ponder already indexes UserOperationEvent (concept indexing).

### approvals.dropped: The old internal steps, sweeps and per-kind relays are not ported

- **Question:** What of old accounts' approvals and payments' executions has no target record, and why?
- **Chosen:** Steps of the one process: record-approval, consume-approved, reschedule-approved, record-expiry, store, load, load-active, load-lane-holders, claim, complete, fail, classify, reset-claim, record-send-attempt. Gone: the claim sweep and hold-claim, list-expired-claims, the expire job, the five open-for-* rows and the per-kind sendSigned relays.
- **Why:** P3: load, record and intent hops are steps, and every sweep is a process's own timer or chain event. The open-for-* rows differed only in how they built the summary (now the opener's) and each relay to an area's send existed only because each area sent its own kind; one send path ends that. `complete` is the opener following `approval_transitioned`. The service-key lanes went to the service signer (responsibilities.service-lanes).

### approvals.gate: An approval may name a gate, checked at the signature and before a held send

- **Question:** `org proposal approve` keeps 'With the owner-safe-proposals gate off, no approval opens, and a gate turned off later stops the send.' The opener checks the gate before it opens; who stops the send?
- **Chosen:** The opener names the gate (`gate`); the sign rpc refuses FEATURE_NOT_AVAILABLE and a held send waits while it is off.
- **Why:** The send is approvals', so only approvals can stop it; naming the gate keeps approvals free of its openers' kinds. Refusing at the signature keeps the frozen 'nothing is signed' wording true. A held operation whose gate is off on its day is not sent and is checked again hourly, never ended early, because its signature exists (K6). Gates are read from access' `feature-flags` through access' evaluator, as access' door says every gated concept does.

### approvals.held-sends: A dated operation is held until its day and checked before it is sent

- **Question:** A personal dated payment is 'a signed operation Capxul sends on the day' (payment send, designed). Approvals owns the one send. What does the process do between the signature and the day, and on the day?
- **Chosen:** Hold until `send_after`; then check each need (a holder's token balance) and the gate; while one fails, wait an hour and check again inside the window; the window's end expires it.
- **Why:** Keeps K16 (sent on the date, retried hourly while short inside the signed window, failed when it closes) where the send is, so K4 stays in one place. A blind send would revert on chain at the paymaster's cost and fail the Payment for a balance that arrives an hour later. The needs are data the opener gives (`needs`), so approvals still decides no authority and reads no opener. The day-ahead warning stays the opener's (payments, through notices). The window is the old three days from the day, so the process lives at most a year and four days (dates up to 366 days ahead).

### approvals.machine: Twelve states: before the signature anyone may end it, after it only the chain

- **Question:** Which states does an approval have, and which ends may happen before and after the signature?
- **Chosen:** sponsoring, pending (before a signature: refused, declined, withdrawn or expired end it); signed, sending, sent (after: only landed, reverted, displaced or expired).
- **Why:** A signed, sponsored operation can land whenever its window is open, whoever holds the bytes, so after the signature only chain proof is safe (K6): a decline, a withdrawal or a refusal then would let the opener retry while the first could still land, and the money would move twice. Before it, nothing exists that can land. The old page states map for any operation that prints them: pending is sponsoring or pending, approved is signed or sending, consumed is sent or later, rejected is declined, expired is expired; refused and withdrawn never reached the old page.

### approvals.no-mail: Approvals sends no email

- **Question:** The old `notify` workflow emailed the signer when an approval opened (approval-pending, pay-run-approval). Does approvals keep it?
- **Chosen:** No: an email belongs to notices, triggered by the opener's transition that says who waits (a proposal waiting for a Director).
- **Why:** Notices sends every email and depends on the openers, not on approvals, and the opener knows what the email should say. No frozen line names an approval email; the person running a command is already looking at the page it opened.

### approvals.no-operations: Approvals owns no operation; its rpcs are reached by flows and its process

- **Question:** With no frozen operation of its own, how is each approvals rpc reached (P2), and which surface does its flow serve?
- **Chosen:** `get` is a step of every opener's flow, devnet's `dev approve` flow, payments' follow and this concept's page flow; `review` a step of the page flow; `sign` and `decline` the process's signals and flow steps.
- **Why:** Every signed command is owned by the concept of its intent (operations.owners), so approvals serves them. The page flow `client.approvals.sign` serves the approval page (a React SDK surface, not the CLI), so the build lists it as an unused flow until surface flows can say so; that gap is expected. Approvals introduces no world: it has no refusal of its own; its openers name the worlds their refusals need.

### approvals.nonce-key: Each approval rides its own nonce key; a void shares the nonce it replaces

- **Question:** The old code put a Payment command on a key named for it and every Permission, invitation and setup on key 0, in order, refusing a second live holder of a key. Which nonce does an approval use?
- **Chosen:** keccak256 of the approval id, cut to 192 bits (never 0), at its next sequence; a replacement takes the replaced approval's key and nonce.
