The frozen line 'Current Organization membership decides reads, not the stored creator' holds because an Organization participant is its company Safe, matched through the reader's active Seats at read time. 'Strangers and accountless callers get the same missing-pair refusal' holds because a caller with no Party of their own has no Safes, and every refusal is `document not found` on `documentHash`.

### documents.render-budget: `document render` takes a longer read budget for its browser

- **Question:** Which budget does `document render` take (P5)?
- **Chosen:** The read class at 2.5 s p50 and 6 s p95, the first-run browser download excluded.
- **Why:** After its one read the command launches a headless browser on this machine, loads the embedded font and prints an A4 PDF: local work the read class does not anticipate, measured in seconds in the old CLI. A browser that is not installed is downloaded once, which is its own span (`sdk.step.browser-install`) and excluded. Get, verify and export keep the read default: one read and, for export, one small file write.

### documents.render-names: Render names parties by their public names, and adds no link

- **Question:** What does a rendered document show beyond its stored fields?
- **Chosen:** The current public name of each party it names (an Organization's name; a person's or agent's display name, else handle, else Safe shortened; never a Party id or a mailbox), read after the checks; no checkout link.
- **Why:** The names are read from parties and organizations, which documents depends on, only after access and integrity pass, and they never enter the stored bytes. A mailbox is left out because a counterparty who named someone by handle never typed it (the contacts rule). The checkout link came from requests, which documents may not read; the request's own reads and notices print it.

### documents.rpcs-and-flows: Three read rpcs; export and render are SDK flows

- **Question:** Old activity owned the three document rows (get, verify, render) and `document export` reused `get`. How are the four commands implemented (A4)?
- **Chosen:** Documents' `reading` area has get (with the bytes), verify and render. `document get` and `verify` are implemented by their rpc; `export` and `render` by a flow that adds the local file work, each its own SDK method under `client.paymentDocuments`.
- **Why:** The rows read documents' content, so they are documents' rpcs, not activity's (old review-agent named the three names for one thing). A4 puts every local step in the SDK, so writing the exported file and printing the PDF are flow steps, and the CLI calls exactly one method. `get` keeps the bytes because its frozen first line says it 'returns the exact stored bytes', and export needs exactly that answer: a second rpc would serve the same read twice. The SDK namespace stays the frozen pages' `client.paymentDocuments`; `export` is the one new method.

### documents.store-builds: documents.store builds the envelope from the subject's facts

- **Question:** Old payments and requests imported documents' builders, built envelopes themselves (or took a client's bytes), then asked documents to admit them against the prepared payment. What does the target door take?
- **Chosen:** One procedure, `documents.store`: the storing concept gives the subject, the owner, the participants, the chain, the issue time and the typed facts (or a receipt file); documents builds the canonical bytes, computes both hashes, writes the row and returns the id and hashes. A replay of the same pair, subject and readers writes nothing.
- **Why:** Money fact (rules.procedures): a hash on chain with no content behind it is a money bug, so the document must commit with its subject (K2). Building inside the door keeps the encoding, the hashing and the size and text limits in one concept, so payments, requests, payroll and equity cannot disagree on a hash; the separate admission step (`admitPaymentDocumentPairInTx`) existed only because a client's bytes had to be checked against the payment, and built bytes cannot differ from the facts they came from. One procedure, invoked once per document, keeps the invokers inside sys:tx-width (payments' prepare invokes it beside parties.resolve-recipient and approvals.open). A replay must be a no-op because a retried birth and a replayed Transfer store the same document again.

### documents.subject-kinds: Subjects are named by the concepts that store them, equity's included

- **Question:** Which subjects may a document belong to?
- **Chosen:** A closed list: a Payment and a Movement (payments), a request (requests), an agreement (payroll), and equity's share or option grant, round, investor commitment and share transfer offer, whose contracts each take a document hash.
- **Why:** The subject binds the envelope (its origin id) and is how activity finds a Movement's Memo, so its kinds are part of the schema, not free text. Equity is being designed at the same time and depends on documents (dependencies.of-equity: 'round, SAFE and grant documents are stored with their offers'); its v4 calls taking a document hash are offerGrant, grantAndIssue and grant (options), createSafeRound and createPricedRound, signSafe and commit, and offerTransfer, so its kinds are listed now rather than left for an edit to this folder.

### documents.tenancy: A document is its subject's owner's row, readable by its participants' Safes

- **Question:** A document is read by two or three tenants (a payer and a payee, an Organization, its payee and the officer who offered). Whose tenant is its row (P8), and how do the others read it?
- **Chosen:** `owner` tenancy, the tenant whose subject it is; the participants' Safes on the row; the generated policy admits the owner or a session whose Safes include one; the hash-pair index crosses tenants.
- **Why:** It is the shape payments' Payments and requests' payer side already take (payments.two-sided-tenancy, requests' `by_payer_created`): one row, its owner's tenant, its other readers by a column on the row, so row-level security fails closed with no join. A copy per reader would need a tenant for every reader at store time, and a payer outside Capxul has none yet. A global table would leave private invoices to the rpcs alone. The participants are Safes because a Safe is the one name stable across sign-up: a person's Safe is derived from their mailbox before they join, so an outside payer who later signs in reads the invoice they were sent.

### documents.unsigned: Capxul signs no document; the signature column goes

- **Question:** Old envelopes could carry an EIP-712 signature, checked on read. Does the target keep it?
- **Chosen:** No signature column: every envelope is built by `documents.store` and answers `absent`, a receipt file `opaque`; `verified` stays in the wire type because the frozen notes list it.
- **Why:** In the target no client supplies an envelope (documents.store-builds), and nothing signs one: the CLI's own line says 'Capxul does not sign documents; Verified checks the stored bytes against the document hash'. Keeping a column nothing writes is P2's orphan. The frozen surface's three values are kept, so a signed kind can return without changing what the commands print.

### documents.verify-answers: Verify answers ok false; get, render and export refuse

- **Question:** The old backend's verify threw UNKNOWN on any stored-row mismatch and never answered `ok` false, though the frozen line says 'A negative verification still completes, reporting ok false'. What does the target do?
- **Chosen:** Verify runs the integrity checks as its question and answers `ok` false when one fails; get, render (and so export) treat a failure as a defect, UNKNOWN naming the check.
- **Why:** P10: the frozen book wins where it and the old code disagree, and its line is only checkable by a black box if the backend can answer `ok` false (the old test reached it only by mocking the SDK). A verify that cannot say no is not a check. The readers that hand the document on (its bytes, its PDF) must not hand on bad bytes, so they still refuse, as the frozen get line requires ('tampering with a hashed note is detected').

### documents.worlds: Two worlds reach every documents refusal and line

- **Question:** Which devnet worlds do the documents operations name?
- **Chosen:** `org-with-documents` (new): an Organization with a member who stored documents for it and was then removed and a member who joined after; a person it paid with a note; an Invoice the person issued it; a salary agreement's terms; a receipt file on a Payment; a Movement's Memo from an outside Transfer to the person; a signed-in stranger; and a signed-in person with no Party of their own. `org-with-tampered-note` (new): the same with one stored note's bytes altered after it was stored. `signed-out` (shared) for NOT_AUTHENTICATED.
- **Why:** Every refusal (CLI_USAGE, INVALID_INPUT) and every kept line is reached from one world that holds a document of each kind and each kind of reader; the tampered note is its own world because altering stored bytes is a fault no operation can make, and every other run must see clean bytes.

## equity (25)

### equity.acceptance: Twenty-three equity operations keep no acceptance line

- **Question:** Equity was never built on the old devnet, and 23 of its 38 frozen operations have no acceptance line. Does the target write new ones?
- **Chosen:** Keep none: the `no-acceptance` gap shows them until Aaron accepts new lines (spec 30).
- **Why:** P1 freezes the old lines and numbers them; new lines are a separate matter Aaron reviews per area. The designed v4 text is still the specification of each (P10), and every refusal is booked from a world.

### equity.acceptance-rewrites: Thirty claims rewritten as outcomes (P1)

- **Question:** Equity's frozen lines name old telemetry events (`reports equity_shares_transitioned once`), storage ('is stored', 'is noted', 'in the writing transaction') and the component that checks ('The SDK opens', 'The SDK signs'). Keep or rewrite?
- **Chosen:** Rewrite each as the outcome a black box sees: proposed once, reported once, the approval's calls on chain; the old line moves to `was`.
- **Why:** P1 rewrites a line that names a mechanism, and each rewrite still passes against the old system. Rewritten: setup 4, 6; issue 1-3; cap table 5; options grant 1, 2, 4; sell 1; transfer approve 1; round create 1-4; SAFE create 1, 2; vote create 4-6; vote execute 2-5; vote list 2; vote cast 3-6; vote list (mine) 2. A claim naming `fundraise_round_transitioned` or `equity_grant_transitioned` is rewritten too, since births and rejections now send those events (equity.reports). The other 23 lines are word for word.

### equity.assembly-rulings: What equity takes from the shared assembly: tx-width, events, checkpoints

- **Question:** Equity's report left four items with the shared records: its Board-matter prepares' transaction width, 12 events its processes name in prose, the Elections record, and checkpoint reads for `org cap-table --at`. The shared pass ruled on each. Does equity change?
- **Chosen:** No record changes but the vote matters' text: the prepares are within sys:tx-width (rules.transaction-width counts direct invocations: documents and board); the events are recorded (contracts.equity-events) and equity's processes keep following them through their waits and factsOf; `--at` stays NOT_IMPLEMENTED (contracts.no-checkpoint-reads); the Board-seat matters wait for board (equity.vote-matters).
- **Why:** A3 gives each long-lived object one process that waits on its chain events; a workflow per event would split the object's one writer into many. The build's unconsumed-chain-event gap counts only triggers, and the schema's waits carry no reference to a chain event, so those gaps stay until a wait can name its event. The frozen `org cap-table` designed call says `--at` is not built yet, so NOT_IMPLEMENTED is its frozen answer.

### equity.chain-is-truth: Equity keeps what the chain cannot name and reads the rest

- **Question:** The old code copied chain state into its equity rows (the deployment's clones, earlier clones, the cap table as JSON, issued and granted timestamps) and re-read the deployment on every answer. What does the target store?
- **Chosen:** Store only Capxul's ids, who an instrument is for (Party, mailbox, Safe), the documents behind the hashes, the request a repeat answers and the carrier of each write; read classes, balances, vesting, the pool, positions and the Governor's standing from indexing's views or the clones when asked.
- **Why:** Ponder already indexes every equity clone (indexing's equity instruments and positions, chain-deployments, chain-logs), so a mirror would be a second copy that drifts, the problem the indexing concept removed. What the chain cannot name is what the frozen surface prints and Capxul alone knows: `og_`, `rd_`, `tr_`, `vt_` ids, member names, documents and whether their hashes match. A status machine on each object is still Capxul's (P7), driven only by chain proof.

### equity.contract-functions: Add the equity clones' functions equity reads and builds

- **Question:** Equity's rpcs read and build v4 functions shared/external.ts did not list. Which are added?
- **Chosen:** Captable setTransferRule, freeze, convertibleReserved, closedForExit; Options vestedOf; Rounds pricedRound, position, consent; Exits transfer, sale; Governor getVotes, proposalThreshold, hasVoted, isAllowedTarget.
- **Why:** Frozen refusals must come before anything is signed (BELOW_THRESHOLD, AUTHORISED_EXCEEDED, NOT_VESTED, RESERVED_MATTER), and a sponsorship revert maps only to TRANSACTION_FAILED. Each signature is the v4 interface's (ICaptable, IOptions, IRounds, IExits, IGovernor). No chain event was added: equity's processes are triggered by their objects' births and read their carriers' facts with indexer.factsOf.

### equity.director-from-chain: A Director is read from the chain, not from board's tables

- **Question:** Every Board matter is refused for someone who is not a Director. What does equity read to know?
- **Chosen:** The caller's own Safe owns the company Safe and wears the Director hat now: indexing's Safe owners and hat wearers, the hat id from organizations' deployment.
- **Why:** Being a Director is a chain fact (the Signer Gate's valid signers wear the Director hat), so the check survives a Director seated outside Capxul; it needs no read of board, whose tables are being designed at the same time. Board still decides the proposal: equity only refuses early.

### equity.dropped: Old equity plumbing that is not ported

- **Question:** Which old organizations equity, rounds and votes functions have no target record?
- **Chosen:** The emit actions (telemetry is the runtime's, P7), the load, find and list-rows queries (reads inside the rpcs), the record and record-outcome mutations and the send-signed actions (approvals send, the processes decide), the accounts lanes' reserve and release (one send per Safe is approvals'), the earlier-clones list and the share transfer read (`transfer`, served by equity.writes.watch).
- **Why:** None is reached by a frozen operation (P2) except through what replaces it: P3 drops plumbing, P7 makes telemetry the runtime's, and approvals owns every UserOperation's send (concepts.approvals).

### equity.events: Seven machines, seven events; four keep the old catalog's names

- **Question:** The old catalog had five equity events (equity_shares_transitioned, equity_grant_transitioned, fundraise_round_transitioned, fundraise_investment_transitioned, vote_transitioned), each the report of a landed write. P7 gives each machine exactly one event named after it. Which machines, and what names?
- **Chosen:** One machine per object (install, write, round, investment, grant, sale, vote), named so that the round, investment, grant and vote keep the catalog's event names; equity_shares_transitioned has no entity and goes.
- **Why:** A machine is an entity's lifecycle (P7); 'equity shares' covered an install, an issue, a sale, a conversion and an exit, five lifecycles with nothing in common. Naming the round, investment and grant machines `fundraise-round`, `fundraise-investment` and `equity-grant` keeps those funnels' names. The old transition words survive as the write's `change` (equity.reports).

### equity.failed-proposals: A Board proposal that ran and failed fails the equity write it carried

- **Question:** Board added a `failed` proposal (the company Safe ran it and its call failed; board.failed-proposal) and asked every follower of proposal_transitioned to read it as 'ran without effect'. What does an equity write, and an install's batch, become?
- **Chosen:** The write fails with `execution_failed`, as a reverted operation does; an install whose batch's proposal failed ends `failed` (batch_reverted). Rejected and replaced proposals still abandon.
- **Why:** Abandoned says nothing reached the chain, and a failed proposal used its nonce: the company Safe ran it. The install machine already calls a batch that ran without effect `failed`, and its failure literal `batch_reverted` is the same outcome, so no new install state is needed. The write keeps the cause apart so a show can say the Board ran it and it failed. The write flows answer each carrier's end with approvals.machine's codes: declined CANCELLED, expired unsent WRONG_STATE, refused, beaten or failed TRANSACTION_FAILED.

### equity.holder-reads: The company is the tenant; a holder reads their own rows by their Safe

- **Question:** Investors, grantees, buyers and voters often hold no Seat in the company, yet must read their own invitation, grant, sale or ballot. Whose rows are they?
- **Chosen:** org_id on every row; a second row-level policy admits the holder by the Safe column that names them.
- **Why:** Each fact is one company's instrument, written in the company's transactions, so P8's tenant is the company. A copy per holder would be two writes of one fact. The holder's own Safe is already on the row (investor_safe, grantee_safe, seller_safe, buyer_safe, signer_safe), and its cross-tenant index names the query it serves. Payroll's payee reads set the same pattern.

### equity.invest-pay-buys: `invest pay` also buys an approved secondary sale

- **Question:** `org equity transfer approve` tells the buyer 'the buyer pays with: capxul invest pay tr_01K9…', but `invest pay` is 'Pay for your shares' in a round. Which command does the buyer run?
- **Chosen:** `invest pay` takes a round (`rd_`, Rounds.pay) or a sale (`tr_`, Exits.buy).
- **Why:** The frozen designed example names `invest pay tr_…` for the buyer, and without it a sale could never complete from the CLI. Both are 'pay for your shares and receive them in the same transaction'; the id's prefix chooses the call.

### equity.no-door: Equity has no procedures

- **Question:** Does any other concept write equity's tables inside its own transaction?
- **Chosen:** No: equity invokes approvals', board's and documents' doors; payments settles equity's payments from chain facts.
- **Why:** A round payment, SAFE, exercise, purchase or exit payout ends in one PaymentSent, which payments settles as a Payment born from a chain fact (concepts.payments), and the round's process marks the investor paid from the same indexed fact. The two need not commit together: neither moves or owns money the other does not already record on chain (rules.procedures).

### equity.one-writes-table: One writes table for every chain write equity carries

- **Question:** Every equity command ends in a chain write carried by a Board proposal or a holder's approval, and many need 'the same request answers the same write' and 'one on its way at a time'. Where do the carriers live?
- **Chosen:** One `equity-writes` row per write, with its change, its object, its carrier, its request digest and its outcome.
- **Why:** An object has many writes over its life (a round's create, invites, allocation and close; a vote's propose, ballots and executions), so carrier columns cannot hold them. One table gives one idempotency index (the request digest while it waits), one 'on its way' guard per Safe and object (a second ballot) and one per Board matter (a second execution, enforced inside the writing transaction), one machine with one event, and one live follow rpc for every flow. Payroll's agreement-writes set the same pattern.

### equity.processes: One process per install, grant, round, sale and vote; one per lone write

- **Question:** rules.durable-processes names a round. Which other equity objects are long-lived, and who decides a write that changes no long-lived object?
- **Chosen:** Add the install, the grant, the sale and the vote, each owning its machine and deciding its own writes; a round owns its investors; an issue, conversion, delegation or exit payout is decided by a small process of its own.
- **Why:** Each added object waits on something outside a request (A3, P3): an install on a service-key receipt and the Board; a grant on its grantee within 30 days and on its exercise window; a sale on the Board, the buyer and its expiry; a vote on the Governor's clock and on an execution anyone may send. A flow cannot be what decides, since the CLI may stop. Deciding a write in the object's own process moves the object in the same transaction as the write's landing, as payroll does. A lone write needs only its carrier and its facts. No cron: the old system swept nothing for equity, and every expiry is the object's own timer.

### equity.reports: What 'reports' means in equity's claims

- **Question:** Frozen claims say a write 'reports `equity_set_up` once', 'reports `cast` once with its side' or 'reports nothing'. The old event fired only for a landed write; the target sends one event per transition of seven machines. How does a claim's report map onto them?
- **Chosen:** A report is equity_write_transitioned to `landed` with its `change` (the old transition word), at the block's time, prepared_by its preparer; 'reports nothing' is no landed write.
- **Why:** P7 sends events for births and failures too, so the funnel sees a proposal opened and a write that failed; counting any event would make 'once' and 'a rejected one never' false. The catalog's words survive as the write's `change` column, so claims without an old event name stay word for word (P1). Each line's `observes` names the event the trace observer counts.

### equity.returns: Board matters whose frozen returns lack it also print their proposal

- **Question:** `org round close` and `org options end` are Board proposals, but their frozen returns have no status or proposal. What do they print?
- **Chosen:** The frozen fields, then `status` and `proposal`, as every other Board matter prints.
- **Why:** A Board proposal on a Board of more than one waits for the other Directors; without its status and id the person cannot tell whether it executed or what to approve. Adding fields keeps every frozen one (P10).

### equity.round-refusals: Round create and close keep their designed refusals, each where it arises

- **Question:** The designed v4 page of `org round create` adds RESERVED_MATTER (the class does not exist) and CONSENT_MISSING (a down round without the affected classes' consent); that of `org round close|cancel` adds WRONG_STATE (cancel after a payment). The target booked none of them. Where does each come from?
- **Chosen:** Restore all three. Create refuses a class the company does not have with RESERVED_MATTER, as preview does, and INVALID_INPUT keeps the pay-by day and the whole share; create refuses a down round with CONSENT_MISSING when an affected class with a class Governor is not in the `approve-round` vote `--approved-by` names; close refuses WRONG_STATE for a round closed or cancelled, or with an allocation still payable before its pay-by day.
- **Why:** P10 rung 1: the designed v4 text wins on refusal codes, so a missing class is RESERVED_MATTER (exit 2, as before), the answer `org round preview` already gives. Rung 3: catching a down round before the Board signs is stricter, and Rounds still checks the consent itself at close (`ConsentMissing(classId)`). WRONG_STATE is what the close rpc raises for a round that is not open or allocated, and `Rounds.close` reverts `InvalidRound("allocations still payable")` before the pay-by day while an allocation is unpaid, as org-with-round-allocated holds (Jo's). The designed row's own case, a cancel after a payment (`PaymentsStarted()`), is the cancel half of the `close|cancel` page: `org round cancel` is its own frozen operation and refuses it with WRONG_STATE. CONSENT_MISSING has no world: Rounds asks consent only of a class with a class Governor, and neither a catalogued world nor any target surface gives a class one yet (`Captable.setClassGovernor` is the company Governor's, and no vote matter builds it), so its `given` is `?` on create and on close (whose org-with-round-allocated has no such class and meets WRONG_STATE first), and the refusal-without-world gap shows the missing design.

### equity.safe-close: A full SAFE round is closed by a Director's own call

- **Question:** The designed text says 'close needs no proposal for a full round'. Who sends it?
- **Chosen:** A Director's own Safe sends Rounds.closeSafeRound when the round is full; otherwise it is a Board proposal.
- **Why:** The frozen returns say `closed`, or the proposal's status when the round is not full, so a full round closes at once; only a Director may ask (ROLE_PERMISSION_DENIED for the rest), and their own Safe is the only one an approval can carry.

### equity.safe-create-refusals: `org safe create` does not refuse ROUND_CLOSED: inviting is its sibling's

- **Question:** The designed v4 page of `org safe create` covers `create · invite · show · close` and lists ROUND_CLOSED for inviting after the round closed (`RoundClosed()`). Must `org safe create` refuse it?
- **Chosen:** No: book it on the commands that meet a closed round, `org safe invite` and `org safe close`, where the target has it.
- **Why:** It cannot happen on create. `org safe create` names no existing round: it opens a new one, and `Rounds.createSafeRound` reverts only for a zero cap, a discount of 100% or more, a close date passed (the frozen close-date refusal) or an unsupported token, so there is no closed round to invite into. The page's `also` names the sibling commands, each a frozen operation of its own that keeps ROUND_CLOSED with its exit code: org.safe.invite and org.safe.close refuse it from org-with-safe-round, so the CLI's surface keeps it.

### equity.setup-budget: Setup's p95 is 8 s, not chain-submit's 4 s

- **Question:** `org equity setup` waits for a service-key transaction and its indexing before the Director can sign. What budget?
- **Chosen:** chain-submit with p95 8,000 ms, the person's wait and chain confirmation excluded.
- **Why:** deployEquity's receipt and its indexing at finality happen before the proposal exists, and that wait is the system's, not the person's: the service signer's waitForReceipt has a 4 s p95 on its own, plus Ponder's poll.

### equity.setup-in-process: Setup deploys and proposes from the install's process

- **Question:** `org equity setup` needs `Installer.deployEquity` (sent by Companies House's tier key) before the Board's batch can be built, since `buildEquity` names the clones. Where do the deployment and the proposal happen?
- **Chosen:** The rpc plans and begins the install; its process sends deployEquity once, waits for EquityDeployed, builds the batch and opens it through board.propose, with each founder's vesting grant born proposed on the same proposal.
- **Why:** An rpc is one transaction and never waits on the chain (A3); a lost answer after broadcast must be looked up, never re-sent, and a deployment with no receipt yet must hold other terms back (setup line 4), which only a durable record of the send can do. Founders' grants born with the batch give `org options end` a grant to end, decided by its own process when the batch lands. The flow follows the install live until it is proposed, then signs.

### equity.tables: Seven tables in place of nine

- **Question:** Which tables does equity need, given the old organizations' nine equity tables?
- **Chosen:** Installs; one writes table; rounds of both kinds; each investor's place in a round; grants (options and founders' vesting); secondary sales; votes.
- **Why:** Old `orgRounds` and `orgSafeRounds` were the same shape twice: one `fundraise-rounds` with a kind. Issues, ballots and executions are each one chain write with no life of their own: rows of `equity-writes` (equity.one-writes-table). Investors had no table at all (the old code built none of rounds' investor side), yet commit, allocate, pay and sign each need the investor's place, so `fundraise-investments` is new. Founders' vesting grants join option grants because `org options end` ends both. Every table is the company's (P8).

### equity.vote-list-refusals: `org vote list` does not refuse INVALID_INPUT: it names no vote

- **Question:** The designed v4 page of `org vote list` covers `list · show <vote> · wait <vote>` and lists INVALID_INPUT for no such vote (`GovernorNonexistentProposal`). Must `org vote list` refuse it?
- **Chosen:** No: book it on `org vote show` and `org vote wait`, which take a vote, where the target has it.
- **Why:** It cannot happen on list. `org vote list` takes only the company, never a vote, so no vote id can be missing: the list reads the company's own votes and the Governor's state of each, and `GovernorNonexistentProposal` answers a read of one named proposal. The page's `also` names `org vote show` and `org vote wait`, each a frozen operation of its own that keeps INVALID_INPUT (no such vote) with its exit code from org-with-votes, so the CLI's surface keeps it.

### equity.vote-matters: Six vote matters built; the Board-seat matters wait for board's seat groups

- **Question:** `org vote create` names nine matters. `board-settings`, `elect` and `remove` call a Hats Elections clone and the Installer's election batch (recorded since: contracts.hats-elections), and their seat groups and terms are board's, which board has not designed (`org board claim --seat` names none yet). What do they answer?
- **Chosen:** Build the six whose calls are equity clones; the Board-seat three answer NOT_IMPLEMENTED until board designs seat groups.
- **Why:** NOT_IMPLEMENTED is the code a frozen command answers until it is built (vocabulary.keep-unnamed), and the Board-seat matters belong with board's seat design (`org board claim`), whose seat groups are still null (CAP-1153). The shared records now hold the Elections calls (contracts.hats-elections), so that blocker is gone; choosing seat groups and terms in equity would design board's seats from outside board, so equity builds the three once board defines them.

### equity.worlds: The thirteen worlds equity's refusals are reached from

- **Question:** Which named devnet snapshots do equity's refusals need?
- **Chosen:** signed-out (shared); org-with-equity-off; org-without-equity; org-with-equity-deploy-held; org-with-equity; org-with-option-offered; org-with-option-active; org-with-round-open; org-with-round-allocated; org-with-safe-round; org-with-sale-offered; org-with-votes; org-with-exit-approved.
- **Why:** Each is built by running operations on a fresh devnet and saved: a company of two founders (a 2-of-2 Board), a member who is not a Director and an outsider, then each step of equity's life. `org-with-equity-off` turns the gate off with `dev gates set`; `org-with-equity-deploy-held` holds the service signer's receipt with its fault switch; `org-with-votes` warps the clock so votes stand pending, active, defeated, succeeded and executed, with one lapsed unsigned. Reusing worlds keeps their number small.

## indexing (18)

### indexing.admission: Ponder admits Capxul's contracts and what their events make known

- **Question:** Which logs does Ponder index: everything on chain from Safe, Hats and the EntryPoint, or only Capxul's?
- **Chosen:** Capxul's v4 contracts; the Safes, hat trees and escrows a Capxul event makes known (CapxulSafeClaimed, CompanySafeMinted, AgentCreated, MoneyDeployed, EscrowDeployed), as Ponder factory sources; UserOperationEvents sent by those Safes; every Transfer of an admitted asset.
- **Why:** Rows nobody can be scoped to would only cost index time and storage on a public testnet. A Capxul event is the provenance of each Safe, tree and escrow, which also retires the old singleton and code-hash checks (they verified Safes Capxul did not make). UserOperationEvents are admitted by sender, not by paymaster, so an operation that takes an approval's nonce is seen even when Capxul did not sponsor it. Transfers are the exception: money can reach a Safe or an escrow before the event that makes it known (a CREATE2 escrow funded first, a person's Safe paid before its claim), so every Transfer of an admitted asset is indexed and a balance is exact.

### indexing.agents-on-safe-owners: An agent's AgentOwner state rides on its Safe's row

- **Question:** AgentOwner's events (created, frozen, unfrozen, key swapped, swept, removed) describe an agent's Safe. Where are they kept?
- **Chosen:** On the agent Safe's row in safe-owners: `agent_owner`, `frozen`, `removed`; the events stay in chain-logs.
- **Why:** An agent is a Safe with an owner and a frozen flag: one Safe, one row, and its signer is that Safe's one owner, kept current by the Safe's own owner events when the key rotates. `agent list` reads the agents an owner holds by the `agent_owner` index, and an agent's sign-in proof checks the same row beside `AgentOwner.agentOf`. A separate table would repeat the address and owners.

### indexing.balances-view: A Safe's balance is a view over transfers, not a stored table

- **Question:** Activity shows balances and books reconciles them. Does Ponder store balances, or are they computed?
- **Chosen:** A view summing transfers per Safe or escrow and asset; a send that must not overdraw reads `balanceOf` live.
- **Why:** Transfers holds every Transfer of each admitted asset from its deployment block, so the sum is exact and rolls back with its rows. The by-from and by-to indexes keep one Safe's sum cheap at the beta's scale. A stored balance is a second row for the same fact, and a live read per Safe per token would put every balance list in the chain-read class. The old `balance` was a view too, over every address on chain; this one covers only the Safes and escrows Capxul knows.

### indexing.chain-payments: PaymentSent carries its budget, agreement or escrow; spends need no table

- **Question:** Every v4 movement of money ends in a PaymentSent, and the same transaction names its source (Budgets' Spent or ScheduledPaid, Agreements' Collected, an escrow's EscrowPaid). Where is that link kept?
- **Chosen:** On the PaymentSent row: Ponder fills `budget_id`, `scheduled_id`, `agreement_id` or `escrow_address` from the event that names its payment id.
- **Why:** Budgets' spent figures, books' reconcile and activity's feed all ask 'what paid this, from where', on lists. A spends table would store the Spent log a second time (it is in chain-logs), and a join by transaction on every list read is the work Ponder can do once. The payment id ties the two logs exactly, and Payments consumes an id once, so the row is unique by it. The old `payment` table copied the v4 evidence row, and its `commitment` sibling had no v4 writer (P9); both go.

### indexing.deployments: The Installer's deployments are indexed, so Ponder can attribute hats and equity

- **Question:** Hats events name a hat, not a company, and the equity clones' events do not name the company Safe. How does Ponder attribute them?
- **Chosen:** chain-deployments: one row per company Safe from MoneyDeployed (hat ids, officer hats by position, Roles, the Gate) and EquityDeployed (the clones); Ponder attributes a hat by its top hat and a clone's event by its address.
- **Why:** Ponder reads only the chain and its own schema (components.ponder), so attribution must come from a chain event, and the Installer's two events carry every address and hat id. The officer hats arrive as an array in install order (Books, Invoicing, People), so the row names them by position. Organizations keeps its own once-written copy of the money half for its setup; equity reads the clones here, replacing old `v4_equity_company`.

### indexing.equity-two-tables: Equity's twelve read models become instruments and positions

- **Question:** The old indexer had a table per equity struct (share classes, priced rounds, SAFE rounds and invitations, sales, claims, share transfers, balances, vesting and option grants, round positions, SAFE notes). What does the target keep?
- **Chosen:** equity-instruments (a company's classes, rounds, sales and transfer offers, with decoded terms) and equity-positions (what a holder holds of each); votes stay in chain-logs.
- **Why:** Equity is low priority and its readers ask two questions: a company's instruments, and a holder's positions across companies. The old tables had the same key shape and differed only in their terms, which a decoded `terms` holds. A cap table folded from logs at every read is too slow for `shares`. Votes are read by the company's Governor (its ProposalCreated and VoteCast), which chain-logs' by-safe index serves.

### indexing.hats-indexed: Hats and their wearers are indexed; a write that must be exact reads Hats live

- **Question:** On v4 every permission is a hat (responsibilities.keepers). The old system read hats from the chain on each request and copied grants into permissions' tables. How does the target know who wears what?
- **Chosen:** Ponder keeps every Capxul hat (chain-hats) and every wearer (hat-wearers) from Hats' events; `wearing` is held, in good standing and active. A check that must be exact at a write reads `isWearerOfHat` live.
- **Why:** Board, budgets, books, requests, banking, applications and organizations ask who wears a hat on most reads, and two of the questions are lists (an Organization's Directors, the hats a Safe wears) that Hats cannot answer: it has no enumeration. Budgets plans a new hat's id from its admin's children, which only an index of HatCreated can count. A copied table drifts, which is why old permissions kept receipts and old indexing a reconcile. The install gives every hat an address for eligibility and toggle, so Hats' events are the whole story until a company attaches elections (low priority): the Elections module rules a Director out at a term's end with no event, so that hat's readers read Hats live.

### indexing.keys: A projection is keyed by its chain identity; one deployment indexes one chain

- **Question:** Chain tables have no tenant column. What leads their keys, and does every key start with the chain id?
- **Chosen:** A fact is keyed by its `log_id` (which carries the chain id); a projection by the object's own chain identity: a Safe or escrow address, a hat id, a Budgets or Agreements id. `chain_id` stays a column on every row.
- **Why:** Each environment runs one chain with one v4 deployment (components.chain: Base Sepolia on staging, an Anvil fork on a devnet), and Budgets and Agreements number their ids from one global counter (`++_budgetCount`, `++agreementCount`), so an address or an id is unique within it. A chain-id prefix would lead every index with a constant. The column stays so a row says where it came from, and the lookups by a hash or a payment id (`BY_TX`, payment id) keep it first because those values are only unique per chain. A second chain is a new deployment.

### indexing.log-identity: A log is its chain, tx hash and tx log ordinal; its block index is a column

- **Question:** What identifies one indexed log, so that a Payment's consumed log or a book fact's key never moves?
- **Chosen:** `log_id` = chain id, transaction hash and the log's ordinal in its transaction's receipt; `log_index`, the block-global index, is kept as a column.
- **Why:** A reorg can re-include the same transaction in another block, which changes the block number and the block-global index of each of its logs but not the transaction's own log sequence. Payments' consumed log (K9), books' money key and a Movement's key are keyed by the log, so a key that moves with a reorg would book the same money twice. The old indexer chose the same identity (SPEC D3) and kept `log_index` because receipts and UserOperation membership name it; both readers of a log must agree on it (P12 #2003). Ponder's handler derives the ordinal from the transaction's receipt, as the old one did.

### indexing.no-machines: No state machine and no event: a standing in a view is the chain's

- **Question:** Agreements, escrows, hat wearers and equity positions have a standing that changes. Does indexing model them as state machines with `<machine>_transitioned` events?
- **Chosen:** No: a standing column is written by Ponder from a chain event; a concept that reacts is triggered by that chain event; the concept that owns the business object keeps its machine.
- **Why:** A target transition is driven by an rpc or a workflow, and its event goes to the outbox in that transaction (P7). Ponder is neither and writes no outbox, so a machine here would have no driver and its event would repeat the chain event the reacting concept is already triggered by. The business objects that wait on these facts keep their own machines: payroll's agreement, requests' checkout, accounts' setup, approvals' approval. An agreement offer past its expiry reads as lapsed from `offer_expires_at`, because no event marks it.

### indexing.not-ported: What of old Ponder and old indexing is not ported

- **Question:** Which old tables, endpoints and backend records of the indexed chain have no place in the target?
- **Chosen:** Ponder's `/sql` HTTP endpoints, the `safes` claim and compiled query shapes; the `safe` table's singleton and code-hash checks; `native_transfer`; `activity_transfer`; `commitment`; the `payment` copy; Roles' tables; old indexing's drift cycles and movement-scan checkpoints; old permissions' receipt-verified chain record.
- **Why:** Each existed for a store or a shape the target does not have. The HTTP path and its claim served readers outside Postgres; views under row-level security replace them (components.ponder). The singleton checks, `activity_transfer` and `commitment` are pre-v4 (P9), and native ETH has no reader (indexing.transfers). Drift cycles reconciled Convex's copies against Ponder, and the receipt record proved a Convex copy of chain state: nothing copies chain rows now (concepts.indexing, concepts.permissions). Movement-scan checkpoints followed Transfers Ponder already indexes; payments' Movements are triggered by the Transfer itself, and an added token is books' scan.

### indexing.one-row-per-log: Each log is stored once: in a typed fact table, or in chain-logs

- **Question:** The old indexer kept one `v4_<contract>_event` evidence table per contract (15), and v4's PaymentSent handler wrote the same log again into `payment`. How are the logs stored in the target?
- **Chosen:** A typed fact table for each log readers query by its columns (Transfer, PaymentSent, UserOperationEvent, ApproveHash, ExecutionSuccess and ExecutionFailure); every other admitted log once in chain-logs, decoded.
- **Why:** Settling a Payment by payment id, ending an approval by sender and nonce, counting a Board proposal's approvals by Safe transaction hash and listing a Safe's transfers by address are column lookups that need typed columns and indexes. Every other log is read in one of two ways: in canonical order per contract event (each chain-event trigger) or by the Safes it concerns, and one table with `contract`, `event` and a GIN index on `safes` serves both. Fifteen evidence tables were one shape repeated; a typed table for each of some sixty events would be read by one trigger each. Storing a log once means `indexer.factsOf` reads a transaction's facts by (chain, tx hash) from six tables with one index each, and no two rows can disagree about one log.

### indexing.reorgs-are-ponders: Ponder handles reorgs; finality is the reader's, through the indexer integration

- **Question:** Who handles a chain reorganisation, and how does a reader know a fact will not be rolled back?
- **Chosen:** Ponder rolls back a reorganised block's rows and replays the canonical ones, so every view is the chain at Ponder's head. A reader that books money reads at the deployment's finality depth (`indexer.factsOf`, `awaitBlock`) and re-reads later where a fact could still go.
- **Why:** Ponder already owns canonical ordering, checkpoints and rollback, and the old indexer added no finality wait, checkpoint or retraction protocol on top. Indexing only finalized blocks would hold every read (a hat just granted, an approval's landing, the chain-settle budget of 3 s p95) behind finality, which on Base Sepolia is minutes. A retraction protocol is the copy-and-correct the drift reconcile did, and it goes with it (concepts.indexing). So the views show the head, and the readers that must not act on a fact that may vanish (payments settling, books booking, a Movement's confirm step) wait for depth through the integration, which fails with a code while Ponder lags or rebuilds. On a devnet Anvil mines at once and reorgs only when a test scripts one.

### indexing.roles-not-indexed: Roles is not indexed: the payout ceiling is read live

- **Question:** The old indexer kept `roles_module`, `role_assignment` and `role_authority` for the Zodiac Roles permissions. What does v4 need from Roles' events?
- **Chosen:** Index nothing of Roles; budgets reads the payout ceiling with `Roles.allowances` when it builds a ceiling batch.
- **Why:** On v4 Roles carries only the payout role the install wires and its per-token ceiling; the Roles commands that granted permissions are gone (P9, concepts.permissions). Nothing lists Roles assignments, and the one reader of the ceiling builds a write from it, which must read the chain at the latest block anyway. The old projection existed because Convex could see confirmations out of order. The Roles address stays on chain-deployments.

### indexing.scope-by-parties: A view's row-level security joins parties' directory on its Safe columns

- **Question:** P8 reads chain facts only through views joined to the Safe-ownership table, which is parties' directory (concepts.parties-scope). How does one view decide which rows a caller sees, and what does a workflow see?
- **Chosen:** Each view names the columns that hold Safes (`scope`). Its generated policy shows a row when any of them is the Safe of a Party whose owner is the session's tenant. A workflow runs as the system role and sees every row.
- **Why:** With a policy, a reader that forgets the filter gets no rows instead of another tenant's (P8 option B). A row is visible when any Safe it names is the caller's, so a payment between two Organizations shows to both, an escrow to its payer and its payee, and a hat's wearers to the company and to each wearer. A row that names no Capxul Safe (a Transfer between two outside addresses of an admitted asset) is visible to no session, only to workflows, which act for the system: settling a Payment, collecting a claim escrow, following an agreement. Indexer-progress names no Safe and is not scoped. The old claim needed the indexer to verify the backend's keys (the component cycle components.ponder removes) and a compiled shape for every query; a hand filter in every rpc is the option P8 rejected.

### indexing.tables-only: Indexing is tables only: no procedure, rpc, workflow, flow or operation

- **Question:** Indexing owns every chain view and no workflow (concepts.indexing). Does it own any behaviour: read rpcs for other concepts, the indexer service, a procedure, a flow?
- **Chosen:** Indexing owns its views and nothing else. A concept that depends on indexing reads the views in its own rpcs and workflows; what depends on Ponder's progress is the shared `indexer` integration; Ponder alone writes.
- **Why:** One Postgres holds the views and every concept's tables, so a reader joins a view in its own transaction, under row-level security, with no hop. A read rpc in indexing would be reached by no operation, flow, signal or trigger (P2's unreached-rpc), and an rpc calling an rpc is not a step the book has. Ponder's progress is a call with a timeout and a failure, not a read (integrations.indexer), and it serves every concept the same way, so it is the shared integration. Nothing in `app` is indexing's to write and nothing writes Ponder's schema but Ponder (components.ponder), so there is no procedure, and with no rpc there is nothing for a flow to compose. OPERATION_OWNERS gives indexing no frozen operation (operations.owners): every command that shows a chain fact is owned by the concept whose noun it shows.

### indexing.transfers: Transfers of the admitted assets only, whoever is party; no native ETH

- **Question:** The old indexer had `transfer`, `native_transfer` and the `activity_transfer` view that paired a Transfer with a V2 PaymentSent. What does the target index of money moving?
- **Chosen:** Every ERC-20 Transfer of the deployment's admitted assets from each token's deployment block; no native ETH; no pairing view; tokens an Organization adds at run time are books' scan.
- **Why:** A filter on known Safes misses money sent before the Safe is known (indexing.admission), and a balance summed from transfers must see every one. No frozen operation moves or shows native ETH, so its table has no reader. The `activity_transfer` view matched a Transfer to a V2 PaymentSent by amount and ordering, a pre-v4 shape (P9); on v4 payments explains a Transfer by the facts of its transaction (`indexer.factsOf`). A token added at run time has no Ponder source, and its only reader is books (concepts.books-token-scan).

### indexing.user-operations: Index UserOperationEvent, so an approval ends on an indexed chain fact

- **Question:** An approval ends only on chain proof: landed, reverted or displaced (concepts.approvals). The old backend read receipts through chain-evidence and asked the bundler. Where does the target read that proof?
- **Chosen:** Ponder indexes UserOperationEvent from Capxul's Safes, unique by hash and by sender and nonce.
- **Why:** Displacement is another operation on the same sender and nonce, which no receipt for the approval's own hash shows; a unique (sender, nonce key, sequence) index answers it in one read. An indexed event is also reorg-safe and comes with the transaction's other facts, so approvals' process waits on Ponder like every other chain fact instead of polling a receipt, and a bundler forgets an operation it dropped.

## media (10)

### media.admission-at-upload: A file is admitted when it is uploaded, for the purpose it names

- **Question:** Old media admitted an image when an owner bound it; old parties checked a document at upload and again at submission. Where does the target admit a file?
- **Chosen:** The upload names a purpose and media checks the type, the bytes' signature, the size and an ID image's pixels before storing; a consumer checks purpose and state.
- **Why:** One rule in one place: a file never changes after its upload, so checking it again at a bind finds nothing new, and at-bind would put media's rule into parties, organizations and banking. A consumer reads only a fact: the file is the caller's, of its purpose, and kept or held, which is what the old `findImageRefusal` door call gave it. A refused file is never stored, so no stray upload is left behind. The upload also checks the bytes' signature, which the old image rule did not (Convex recorded only the declared type): a declared PNG that is not one now fails closed, as old identity documents did. `org create`'s invalid logo is still refused by organizations, before any write, because an id that names no kept image of the founder's names nothing it can use.

### media.budgets: The upload's rpc and web flow leave the write default

- **Question:** Which budgets do media's records take (P5)?
- **Chosen:** The upload rpc 900/3000 ms and its web flow 1200/4000 ms, both class write; the serving rpc and flow read; the process background.
- **Why:** A write's 700/1500 ms fits a row, not receiving up to 24 MB: at 100 Mbit/s 24 MB alone takes about 2 s. The upload is still one transaction with no outside call, so write stays its class (budget-class-fit). The web flow adds the browser's send of an image of up to 5 MiB. Serving one image by its key is a read, and the document's process waits on its timer, which is never system time.

### media.document-process: One durable process per identity document, with its own timer

- **Question:** What ends an identity document's lifetime: a process per document, a check at read time, or the submission that used it? And does a file need a state machine at all?
- **Chosen:** A file machine (kept, held, expired); `hold` starts one process per document, whose timer at expires_at runs `expire`.
- **Why:** An identity document is a long-lived object with an expiry, so it gets one durable process (rules.durable-processes lets a designer add an object); its own timer replaces the old scheduler's delete, with no sweep (P3). Its expiry is a change of state that deletes bytes, so it is a named transition with its event (P7), and the two births tell an image (`keep`, no process) from a document (`hold`, which triggers one). Ignoring expired rows at read would keep a person's documents in the database for good. A delete by banking would be banking writing media's table for no money fact (rules.procedures), and media may not follow banking's changes.

### media.door: Media has no procedure, and the old bind rpcs are not ported

- **Question:** Old media had three rpcs: an upload URL, `setProfileImage` and `setOrgLogo`, the last two writing the owner's row. What is media's door on the target?
- **Chosen:** No procedure: one upload rpc and one serving rpc. Each owner binds a file in its own write, after reading `files`.
- **Why:** No stored file is a money fact, so media has no procedure (rules.procedures), and with owners pointing at files the bind is the owner's own write: `parties.profiles.save` for a photo, `organizations.setups.begin` for a logo, `banking.verification.start` for documents. Changing a logo after setup has no frozen operation and no caller in the old system beyond an unused React hook, so it is not ported (P2); if it comes, it is organizations' rpc over `files`. The old upload URL and its separate POST to storage become one rpc, so no storage URL outlives a request and the SDK's provider failures for the POST go. OPERATION_OWNERS gives media no operation.

### media.flows: Two web-app flows; banking composes the upload in its own flow

- **Question:** Media owns no operation. How do clients reach its two rpcs (A4: clients call SDK methods)?
- **Chosen:** `client.media.upload` and `client.media.fetchImage` serve the web app's pages; `verify start`'s flow calls the upload rpc as a step.
- **Why:** A4: the Core SDK owns every flow, and a page calls one SDK method. The web app's setup page and Organization creation upload an image (`client.media.upload`, the old `client.media.uploadImage` without its storage POST), and every page that shows one reads it (`client.media.fetchImage`). Banking's `client.verification.start` reads each path on the machine and calls `media.files.upload` as one of its steps (banking depends on media), so `verify start` stays one SDK method. Each flow says it serves the react surface.

### media.one-table: One `files` table holds every stored file, its bytes included

- **Question:** The old system kept files in three places: Convex's `_storage` for every blob, old parties' `verificationDocuments` for the identity documents' metadata, and a storage id on each owner's row. What does the target store, and where?
- **Chosen:** One `files` table: uploader, purpose, state, declared type, size, the bytes and a document's expiry. Owners keep only a file id.
- **Why:** One table gives one upload, one admission rule, one serving path and one retention column for the three uses (concepts.media). Two tables would repeat the key, the tenancy and the upload for a difference of limits and lifetime, which `purpose` and `state` already carry. The bytes are a column behind the BlobStore service (components.files: one Postgres, files of at most 24 MB); Postgres keeps a large value out of line, so reading a row's metadata does not read its bytes. The owners' storage ids stay with the owners as file ids (a Party's `photo_file_id`, an Organization's `logo_file_id`), and old parties' document metadata is this table's row.

### media.purposes: Three purposes, each with its limits: image, id-image, document

- **Question:** Which kinds of file does media admit, and with which types and sizes?
- **Chosen:** `image`: JPEG, PNG or WebP, at most 5 MiB. `id-image`: JPEG or PNG, at most 15 MB, at least 200 by 200 pixels. `document`: JPEG, PNG or PDF, at most 24 MB.
- **Why:** The limits differ in exactly three ways. An image is the old image rule (`rules.ts`: JPEG, PNG or WebP, 5 MiB). An ID image and any other identity document are Bridge's published limits as old parties enforced them (documents.ts: 15 MB and 200 by 200 pixels for an ID image; 24 MB, PDF allowed, otherwise), which the frozen `verify start` repeats in its notes. Two purposes would lose the ID image's pixel and size rule. Bridge's nine fields are banking's vocabulary: banking matches each field to its purpose, and the 24 MB total of a person's two ID images is a rule of the submission, so it stays banking's too.

### media.retention: Images are kept for good; a document's bytes go 30 minutes after its upload

- **Question:** The old bind deleted the image it replaced, and old parties deleted a document 30 minutes after its upload and after each submission. How long does the target keep each file, now that media points at no one?
- **Chosen:** A kept image stays for good; a held document's bytes are deleted 30 minutes after its upload.
- **Why:** Media cannot see a replacement: owners point at files and media points at no one (concepts.media). A procedure from the owner would be a write that is no money fact (rules.procedures); media cannot react to parties' or organizations' changes, because it may not depend on them; and a sweep is neither a process's timer nor one of P3's crons, and would have to read media's users. An image is small (5 MiB at most), rarely replaced and public, so keeping a replaced one costs little and shows nothing that was not shown; this drops the old delete-on-replace. Identity documents are private, and Capxul keeps no copy of them once Bridge has them: their bytes are deleted 30 minutes after the upload. The old 30 minutes was Convex's action ceiling; it stays because it is enough for the one submission that follows the upload in `verify start` (every run uploads again). The expired row stays, without bytes, as the record of what was uploaded.

### media.serving: A kept image is served by its id to anyone; a document never is

- **Question:** Owners keep only a file id. How does a page show a Party's photo or an Organization's logo?
- **Chosen:** `media.files.get` answers a kept image's bytes by its id with no sign-in: one cross-tenant index and the row policy's one exception.
- **Why:** The old serving URL was public: Convex served a blob to anyone holding its URL, and the views handed it out. An image is what any counterparty sees of the Party or Organization pointing at it (the Party is global), and a ULID-based id is not guessable, so serving it by id shows nothing new. Owner-serves would put bytes or URLs into every view and media's reads into three concepts. Signed URLs add a key and an expiry for files that are public anyway. A file never changes, so the answer is cacheable for good by its id. Identity documents were never served (old submissions.ts: never create a serving URL) and still are not: the rpc answers a document as it answers an unknown id, null.

### media.uploader: A file is its uploader's: tenancy by the uploader's principal

- **Question:** Whose row is a stored file (P8), and who may use it?
- **Chosen:** Tenancy `person` on the uploader's principal id; only the uploader can use the file in a write, by reading it at their own key.
- **Why:** At its upload a file's owner may not exist yet: an Organization's logo is uploaded before `org create` births the Organization. The uploader is always known, and every write that points at a file is the uploader's own (`org create` by the founder, a profile save by its person, `verify start` by the person whose documents they are), so reading at the caller's key both finds the file and proves it is theirs. The old check was weaker: any storage id of the deployment could be bound, so anyone who learned a blob's id could bind it. Global would keep that hole. An agent's principal may upload too; no consumer takes its files today, and refusing it would be a rule with no use.

## notices (24)

### notices.admission: Welcome every admission but an invitation's; account-ready after the setup

- **Question:** Which admissions send the beta welcome, and where does the account-ready message come from?
- **Chosen:** Welcome on `admit` and `admit_unlisted` unless admitted `invitation:<org>`; the referrer named when admitted by a referral; nothing for a reward of invites; account-ready on the personal Safe's `finish`.
- **Why:** `org member invite` admits the invitee 'with no second welcome email' (#1), and procedures.accounts-admit says the invitation email is the welcome. `referral invite` 'sends them the invite email': old code sent the welcome, so the welcome names who invited them. A referrer rewarded with an invite got no email before, and still does not; rewarded with their own admission, they get the welcome. The 'on the list' email went with the hold (accounts.admission-paths). Account-ready moved from profiles to the Safe's setup (parties.no-process).

### notices.agreements: Agreement emails follow landed writes, a refused collection and the end

- **Question:** payroll.notices-elsewhere hands every agreement email to notices, fed by payroll's two events. Which transitions, and what of old notices with no target cause?
- **Chosen:** A landed write sends by its change (none for a withdrawal); a payee's reverted collection sends collection-refused; `finish` sends agreement-ended; the calendar notices are the daily cron's.
- **Why:** The frozen lines are about writes: 'a landed resume tells the payee once; a prepared one tells no one' (`org agreement resume` #2) and 'a landed withdrawal sends the payee no email' (`withdraw` #2). A write lands once, so its outbox id makes one email; a rate change, a cancelled lowering and a collection move no status, so status alone would miss them. The end is the process's timer now (payroll.one-process), so it sends agreement-ended and the sweep no longer looks for ended agreements. The old payout-ceiling alert to the Directors needed the revert reason, which a failed write does not keep; the low-balance alert still warns them of a short Safe.

### notices.allowance-amount: The allowance email follows budgets' apply, at the chain's amount when sent

- **Question:** 'Once a v4 assign executes, the member is emailed the allowance at its amount now, after any change that applied' (org allowance assign #5). budgets.notices-need-budgets asks for the edge. What is needed?
- **Chosen:** One workflow on `budget-change.apply`; for an allowance it reads `Budgets.budgetOf` when it sends. Budgets is already in notices' dependsOn (dependencies.notices-budgets), so foundation.ts is unchanged.
- **Why:** The terms hold the amount proposed, which a later `org allowance change` may have replaced before the assign executed; the chain's amount at send time is 'its amount now, after any change that applied'. The shared map already carries the edge the budgets designer asked for, recorded by dependencies.notices-budgets, so the concept map needs no change. The same workflow tells department holders (old department-holder emails), so one workflow follows budgets.

### notices.banking: Banking's notices: the named person, verified once, a payout's three moves

- **Question:** Which of banking's transitions send mail, and to whom?
- **Chosen:** The named person's invitation; verified at the first approval, once per verification; a payout funded, delivered and returned, to the person who made it.
- **Why:** banking.verification-people gives the invitation email to notices. Each further currency's approval is its own transition (banking's machine), and old Capxul sent one verified email, so the id is the verification's. The payout emails are old banking's, to the payer; a return can come after delivery (K13) and leaves the payer's money on its way back, so dependencies.of-notices names it.

### notices.board: Board's notices follow its changes, its proposals and its officer grants

- **Question:** Board, designed beside notices, sends no mail and says notices follows its events. Which of its transitions send, and to whom?
- **Chosen:** A seat (`seat`) to the member, a removal (`remove`) to the Director, a threshold (`set_threshold`) and a proposal born (`propose`) to the Directors, an officer grant applied (`apply`, grant) to the member.
- **Why:** The frozen note says Capxul 'emails the member once they are seated', and board decides when a change counts: on its approval's landing with the effect in its facts. Chain events would bypass that and need indexing, which notices does not depend on. The waiting email moved off approvals (approvals' decision on notify) to the opener that knows who waits, which for a Board proposal is board. The removal, threshold and officer emails are old owner-removed, approvals-changed and the permission emails, whose subjects live on as board's changes. A claim and a lapsed refresh are the claimer's own act or a hat already gone, and a revoke sent nothing before; they send nothing. The change rows carry the member's email as planned, so a seat reaches them even before they have a principal at that Safe.

### notices.chain-reads: Live chain state for a notice is read through chain-rpc, not indexing

- **Question:** Some notices need the chain as it is now: an allowance's amount, what a payee can collect, a Safe's balance, the Directors. Notices does not depend on indexing. Where does it read them?
- **Chosen:** Read the view functions through chain-rpc when the notice is sent.
- **Why:** The concept map gives notices no indexing edge (dependencies.of-notices), and a designer does not add one. These are live state, not facts that happened: an amount after any change that applied, what an agreement has released by now, a balance, the current owners; the view functions answer exactly, which is what chain-rpc is for. Producers copying figures would put chain state in rows that drift, which payroll and budgets designed away (payroll.live-reads, budgets.chain-is-the-record). A failed read is the workflow's own retry.

### notices.claim-escrow: Money waiting in a claim escrow sends no email, as before (for Aaron)

- **Question:** When a payment to an email with no account goes into a claim escrow (`escrowed`), the payee may never learn the money is waiting. The old system sent nothing. Does notices tell them?
- **Chosen:** Send nothing, as the old system did; the payer tells the payee themselves.
- **Why:** No frozen operation or acceptance line names such an email, and adding a first email to an address Capxul has no account for is a product choice (who may be emailed unasked, and how often), not one the design can make (W1). The workflow would be one more outbox follower of `escrow` if Aaron wants it; nothing in the map blocks it.

### notices.daily-agreement-notices: The daily agreement notices: a cron, notice ids as claims, a balance alert

- **Question:** P3 keeps the daily agreement notices as a cron. What does it send, and what stops it sending twice?
- **Chosen:** Pay ready per release period and a fixed term's last week, keyed by notice ids that name the period; a balance-alerts row per Organization and token, raised and cleared, so a short Safe alerts once per episode.
- **Why:** A notice id that names its agreement and period is its own claim, so a second run, a devnet warp or a resume finds the rows. Only the low-balance alert must re-arm after the Safe recovers, which is a state: a machine with its event (P7), not a row deleted by hand. It runs at 00:15 UTC as before. Its budget leaves the background class: it reads every running agreement and each paying Safe on chain, Organization by Organization (P5).

### notices.dated-payments: Dated payments send five notices; the payer is the requester, once per batch

- **Question:** Payments gives notices `come_due` for the day-ahead warning and `lapse` for a failed payment. Which Payment transitions notify whom?
- **Chosen:** For a Payment with `pay_at`: schedule, settle and cancel tell the payee; come_due with a shortfall tells the payer; lapse tells both. An undated Payment sends nothing. The payer of an Organization's Payment is its command's requester, told once per command.
- **Why:** These are old payments' notices (ADR-0034 ruling 6A): a dated payment happens while nobody watches, so both sides need telling; an undated one is followed live by its payer (`payment send` follows it, `payment retry` is offered there), and old Capxul sent no general payment-received email, so adding one is a product choice left open. Old M9 looked the payer up through the execution and found no one for a Board payment; the command's `requested_by` always names a principal. Old R8 sent a batch's payer one email per Payment; the payer's notice id is the command's, so they get one.

### notices.delivery-record: One delivery record per notice and recipient, keyed so a notice goes once

- **Question:** What does the delivery record hold, and what makes a notice go once?
- **Chosen:** One row per (recipient, notice id), the id `<template>:<occasion>` with the transition's outbox id as the occasion; the template's facts, not the body; status, attempts, last error and mail id; an index per filter `dev mail notices` takes.
- **Why:** The outbox id is unique per transition and the same on every redelivery, so the key itself is the idempotency, with no claim table and no event naming scheme; the daily notices name their agreement and period instead. Facts let a retry render the same message without storing every body. Old noticeDeliveries took any status from any status (P7 makes each a transition with its event), and its template filter ran after the 200 newest rows were taken, so it missed older matches; an index serves it. `notice_transitioned` replaces the old `[CAPXUL_MAIL_FAILED]` and `[CAPXUL_NOTICE_RECORD_FAILED]` log lines: a record that cannot be written is the workflow's own retry, not a log line.

### notices.failed-proposals: A payee Organization's collection whose proposal failed is refused

- **Question:** Board added a `failed` proposal (board.failed-proposal) and asked every follower to read it as 'ran without effect'; payroll now fails a write whose proposal failed (`execution_failed`, payroll.carrier-endings). Does the payee hear of it?
- **Chosen:** `collection-refused` follows a payee's `collected` write that reverted or ended `execution_failed`.
- **Why:** Either way the chain ran the collection and nothing was paid, so the payee's Directors should learn it the same way and can collect again. A failed Payment follows payments' `lapse` (payments.board-outcomes), which `notices.payments.failed` already tells for a dated Payment, so no other notice changes.

### notices.first-attempt: The outbox workflow makes the first attempt; a held notice gets a retry process

- **Question:** Three tries, a minute then four apart (integration mail). Who makes them, and when is the delivery record born?
- **Chosen:** Each outbox workflow sends to each recipient and records the outcome as the row's birth (sent, retrying or failed); a row born retrying starts one retry process, instance per notice, which makes the other two tries.
- **Why:** `dev mail notices` shows each record 'sent, retrying or failed' (frozen about); a queued state would show a fourth, and start a second workflow for every notice that is sent at once, nearly all of them. Retries in every outbox workflow would repeat the backoff and its guard in each and make each one wait minutes. Here a notice is the long-lived object only when it is held, and then it has one process (A3) that owns its last two attempts. The provider's idempotency key is the notice's, so an attempt repeated after a crash is taken once; the mail call itself never retries (integrations.timeouts).

### notices.invitations: The invitation email follows send, open and resend; a refusal is notices'

- **Question:** organizations.invitation-delivery gives the email and its delivery to notices. Which transitions send it?
- **Chosen:** `send` (a Seat-only offer, open at once), `open` (an offer with grants, once the Board's batch ran) and `resend`; never to an `@handle`.
- **Why:** An offer with grants cannot be accepted until it opens, so emailing at its birth would invite someone to nothing. Each resend is its own transition with its own outbox id, so each delivers exactly one message and a redelivery none (`org invite resend` #2, as organizations rewrote it). A provider refusal is a row here and never touches the offer (#3). An agent has no mailbox and finds its offer with `invite list` (`org member invite` note 5).

### notices.invoices: Invoice emails as before, with a decline added

- **Question:** Old receivables emailed only for Invoices, and told a payer only when no inbox showed it. Keep that?
- **Chosen:** Invoices only: issued to a payer Organization or an outside payer; paid (settle or match) to the issuer, with a receipt to an outside payer; declined to the issuer.
- **Why:** A person on Capxul finds a request in `inbox`, and a basic or link request's issuer shares its checkout link (old: 'only a customer paying through an escrow hears from Capxul about it'). Widening that is a product choice no ruling makes. A bank deposit match pays it as surely as a settlement, as before. The decline is added because dependencies.of-notices names it. Part-received went with the state it followed (requests.escrow-process).

### notices.kinds: Which notices the target sends

- **Question:** Old Capxul sent 44 kinds of email (the sign-in code among them) from a dozen notify actions. Notices owns no frozen operation, so no frozen text lists them. Which does the target send?
- **Chosen:** Every old notice whose subject lives on in the target, to its old audience and under its old template name unless that name no longer says what it is, plus the ones the architecture and the designed concepts name; none for a subject the target dropped.
- **Why:** A notice tells a person their money or authority changed (old G5), so each one is behaviour people rely on even where no frozen line names it; claims-only would silently stop the invoice, payout and dated-payment emails. Port-all would invent subjects for notices whose objects are gone. Kept, by source: the beta welcome and the account-ready message (accounts), the invitation (organizations), the allowance and department-holder emails (budgets), a Director seated or removed, the threshold changed, a proposal waiting and an officer hat granted (board; old owner-added, owner-removed, approvals-changed, approval-request and the three permission emails, renamed for v4's words), the five dated-payment emails (payments), eleven agreement emails (payroll), person verification, verified and payout sent and delivered (banking), the Invoice emails (requests). Added because a decision names them: a payout returned and an Invoice declined (dependencies.of-notices), a round or SAFE invitation (equity's design). Dropped with their subjects: pay runs' pay-run-approval and pay-run-payment, approval-pending (an approval's own signer is at the page; approvals' decision on notify), beta-waitlist (accounts.admission-paths), invoice-part-received (requests.escrow-process) and payout-ceiling-alert (notices.agreements). The sign-in code stays access' (concepts.notices).

### notices.mailbox-tenancy: A delivery record is its recipient's, keyed by mailbox

- **Question:** Whose row is a delivery record (P8)? Old noticeDeliveries had no tenant column at all.
- **Chosen:** `person` tenancy with the mailbox as `tenantField`, as accounts' admissions; devnet's reads across are cross-tenant indexes.
- **Why:** A notice is what was said to its recipient, at their mailbox, and many recipients have no principal yet (an invitee, an outside payer, a referred friend), as with accounts.mailbox-tenancy. The subject's tenant would put a payee's email under the payer's tenant. Global would leave personal data with no row policy. Only devnet's `dev mail notices` reads across mailboxes, so the two cross-tenant indexes say so; `org_id` and `person_id` are kept on the row for the event and for readers that know them.

### notices.no-door: No procedures, rpcs, flows or operations: notices only reacts

- **Question:** What does notices offer other concepts and clients, and what of old operator's notice functions?
- **Chosen:** Nothing: devnet reads the delivery records for `dev mail notices`; old `operator.notices.record` is each workflow's record step, `operator.notices.list` devnet's rpc over this table.
- **Why:** Nothing depends on notices but devnet (dependencies.notices-top), and a notice is never one money fact, so a procedure would be the `notice-in-transaction` case (rules.procedures). A list rpc here would be a devnet-only rpc in a concept staging runs, which operations.dev-mail-notices-in-devnet refuses; devnet reads the table through its dependency. Old record had no environment gate and list read real addresses on production. Every old concept's notify action, notice-facts read and scheduler, and payroll's notice-claim table, become these workflows.

### notices.no-staleness-check: A notice tells the transition that committed, even if the subject moved on

- **Question:** Old notify actions sent nothing when the subject had left the state its event put it in by the time they ran (a scheduled payment already sent, an invoice already paid). Keep that check?
- **Chosen:** Send what the transition says; each later transition sends its own notice.
- **Why:** The outbox delivers a transition within a second, so a moved-on subject is rare, and each move it made sends its own true notice in order (scheduled, then cancelled). The old check made a notice depend on a second read, and a read that failed sent nothing at all with no record (old R8). Facts are still read when the notice is sent, so an amount is the one current then (the allowance's, `org allowance assign` #5).

### notices.one-workflow-per-transition: One outbox workflow per transition a notice follows

- **Question:** concepts.notices says one workflow per notice kind, triggered by the source's transition. But a trigger names one transition, and the invitation email follows three (send, open, resend) and the welcome two (admit, admit_unlisted).
