With `--org`, a payee Organization's Safe acts only by Board proposal, which payroll opens with `board.propose` in its write's transaction (the call and the proposal are one fact).

### payroll.live-reads: Reads ask Agreements and Budgets now; they never write

- **Question:** Where do lists and shows get earned, collectable, the current rate and a department's budget, and may a read settle anything?
- **Chosen:** From the chain at read time (agreementOf, availableOf, budgetOf, leftOf), refusing PROVIDER_ERROR when it does not answer; reads write nothing.
- **Why:** The frozen notes say budgets are 'read from the chain as it is now', and earned pay accrues every second by the Calendar, which only `availableOf` answers exactly. A read that writes is what made the old lists slow and racy; the agreement's process now settles everything as it happens. The class is chain-read, no override.

### payroll.no-door: Payroll has no procedures

- **Question:** Does any other concept write payroll's tables inside its own transaction?
- **Chosen:** No: payroll's door is empty; readers above it (notices) read its tables and follow its events.
- **Why:** Every old door procedure served runs, departments or notices, all gone or moved. A collection's Payment is born in payments from the indexed PaymentSent, a fact both concepts read, so no money fact spans payroll and a concept above it. What follows an agreement's change (an email, a projection) lags harmlessly and goes through the outbox (P4).

### payroll.notices-elsewhere: Agreement emails and the daily agreement notices are notices'

- **Question:** Old payroll emailed on every landed write and ran a daily sweep (pay ready, a fixed term's last week, ended, a Safe too short for what its payees can collect). Where does that go?
- **Chosen:** Every email, and P3's daily agreement notices cron, are notices', fed by payroll's two events and its tables; the sweep's lapse and end are the process's timers.
- **Why:** concepts.notices puts every email after a change in notices, which depends on payroll ('an agreement offered, changed or ending'). The notices a claim requires (one email when a resume lands, none for a withdrawal) follow agreement_write_transitioned. The daily notices are calendar work with no single trigger, which P3 keeps as a cron, and it only sends mail, so it is notices'. Its notice-claim table goes with it.

### payroll.one-awaiting-per-signer: One write on its way per Safe and agreement

- **Question:** When may a holder or payee open a write while another write on the same agreement still awaits?
- **Chosen:** Any Safe may have one awaiting write per agreement; a second from the same Safe is WRONG_STATE naming the first's approval.
- **Why:** Two holders' pauses may race (a frozen claim), so per-agreement is too strict. Replacing an unsigned write left its approval signable, so a 'replaced' write could still land unreported; with one awaiting write per Safe nothing lands that payroll is not following. A waiting approval ends within the paymaster's window (approvals.open: at most 10 minutes), or its signer declines it, so the block is short. The unique partial index is the guard.

### payroll.one-process: One durable process per agreement, following its chain events

- **Question:** What durable work does an agreement need, and how many workflows carry it?
- **Chosen:** One instance per agreement owns both machines: chain events, write outcomes and its own timers; the write rpcs signal it.
- **Why:** rules.durable-processes names the agreement, and A3 gives its process the machine. A write lands in the same transaction as the status move its event makes, which two processes could not share. A lapse and an end are dates, so they are the process's timers (P3: what the old system swept for becomes a process's own timer). Ponder indexes every Agreements event, so the process follows them at finality instead of the old settle-on-read. The chain events are its waits, not triggers: its trigger is the offer's birth.

### payroll.payee-reads: An agreement is its payer's row; the payee reads it by its own Safe

- **Question:** An agreement belongs to the paying Organization and to a payee in another tenant. Whose tenant is the row?
- **Chosen:** Tenancy organization (org_id, the payer); the payee reads, and opens its own accept and collect, under a second policy on payee_safe.
- **Why:** Every write on an agreement is the payer's money authority (Budgets' reservation) or the payee's acceptance of the payer's terms, so the row is the payer's record. A payee's own Safe is what the agreement pays, and P8 already scopes chain reads by Safe ownership, so a policy on payee_safe admits exactly the payee (a person, or an Organization's members) to read the agreement and open its own accept and collect, and no one else. Two rows would be one fact stored twice. Writes carry the payer's org_id too, so their tenant is never a join.

### payroll.reports: What 'reports' means in payroll's claims

- **Question:** Frozen claims say a write 'reports `accepted` once' or 'reports nothing'. The old event fired only for a landed write; the target sends one event per transition of two machines. How does a claim's report map onto them?
- **Chosen:** A report is agreement_write_transitioned to `landed` with its `change` (the old transition name, `rate_changed`, `accepted`), at the block's time, prepared_by the holder or payee; 'reports nothing' is no such event and no status move.
- **Why:** P7 wants every transition to send its machine's event, births and failures included, so the funnel sees an offer prepared and a write that failed. The frozen claims were written against an event that marked only a landed write, and their `change` names survive as the write's `change` column, so the claims stay word for word (P1) and still pass against the old system. Each acceptance line's `observes` says which event the trace observer counts.

### payroll.scope: Payroll designs agreements only; runs, groups, terms, departments are not here

- **Question:** The design brief named payroll runs (one durable workflow per run), saved groups, departments and a settle-run procedure from payments. The concept map gives payroll v4 Agreements only. Which does the target build?
- **Chosen:** Agreements and their terms only, as concepts.payroll and the landings assign; runs, items, groups and terms dropped; departments are budgets'.
- **Why:** The brief itself says 'as the concept map assigns', and the map is decided: no frozen operation makes, lists or shows a run or a group (P2), departments are budgets (concepts.budgets), and payments never depends on payroll (dependencies.payments-payroll), so a settle-run procedure could not be invoked. An agreement's terms live in its terms document and its row, so old terms go too. A run or a group comes back only with a frozen operation that names it.

### payroll.static-links: Board's and documents' doors are plain imports; the process reads proposals

- **Question:** payroll/links.ts loaded `board.propose` and `documents.store` dynamically while both concepts were being designed, and the agreement's process described board's proposal table in words. Both concepts have landed. What changes?
- **Chosen:** Delete links.ts: the payee rpcs import `procedure_board_propose` from ../board/procedures.ts and the offer imports `procedure_documents_store` from ../documents/procedures.ts; the lifecycle reads `board-proposals` for an executed or failed proposal's transaction.
- **Why:** A stand-in was only for a sibling that did not exist yet; payroll depends on both board and documents, so plain imports are the conventional link and a renamed export now fails the typecheck instead of dangling. The process needs the proposal row's `execution_tx_hash` to read the write's facts, so the table belongs in its reads.

### payroll.status-machine: The nine statuses are stored and moved by the agreement's process

- **Question:** Old payroll derived an agreement's status at every read from its row, the chain and the clock. Keep that?
- **Chosen:** Store the status; only the agreement's process moves it, from indexed Agreements events and its own timers.
- **Why:** A3 gives each long-lived object a process that owns its machine, and P7 makes each change one event from a transition, which a status computed at read time can never send (an agreement that lapsed or ended unread would never report it). Lists filter by status, so it must be a column. The names stay the nine `--status` takes (surface, P10). `ending` is a holder's end only, as the old rule had it: a fixed term's own end leaves it active until it ends.

### payroll.two-tables: Two tables: agreements, and the writes on them

- **Question:** Old payroll kept eight tables, three of them for agreements. Which survive, and in what shape?
- **Chosen:** `agreements` (what the chain cannot name, plus the status) and `agreement-writes` (every holder and payee write until the chain decides it).
- **Why:** A holder's write and a payee's write are the same thing to the process: one Agreements call carried by an approval or a proposal, decided by chain proof. One table keeps every collection with its window and PaymentSent id, and lets two holders' writes be in flight at once (a pause beaten by another holder's pause is a frozen claim). An embedded last write lost history and needed a 20-entry side list. Earned, collectable, the current rate and budget figures are read from the chain when asked, never copied, so nothing drifts. The notice-claim table goes with the emails (payroll.notices-elsewhere).

### payroll.withdraw-carriers: An ended offer takes its unsigned writes' approvals with it

- **Question:** When an offer lapses, is withdrawn or is abandoned, what becomes of a write still waiting on it (an acceptance)?
- **Chosen:** The process withdraws each such write's unsigned approval (approvals.withdraw) and abandons the write.
- **Why:** approvals.withdraw's money fact is exactly this: an intent that ended takes its unsigned carrier with it, so nobody signs a call whose intent Capxul no longer records. A signed approval is left to the chain, which fails it. A collection is never withdrawn this way: earned pay stays collectable after an agreement ends.

### payroll.worlds: The seven worlds payroll's refusals are reached from

- **Question:** Which named devnet snapshots do payroll's refusals need?
- **Chosen:** org-with-department; org-with-agreements; org-with-agreements-off; org-with-closed-department; org-with-short-safe; org-with-frozen-ceiling; org-with-writes-awaiting.
- **Why:** org-with-department: a funded USDC department (a top budget) with one office holder, another member and no agreements. org-with-agreements: it plus an open offer to a person and one to an Organization's handle, an active agreement with released pay, a paused, an ending and an ended one, a lapsed and a withdrawn offer, and the department's room nearly used. The rest build on it: the payment-agreements gate off; the budget closed; the company Safe short of the released pay; the payout ceiling frozen; and the payee's collection and a holder's pause each opened and unsigned.

### payroll.write-machine: A write is awaiting until chain proof lands or fails it

- **Question:** Old writes had awaiting_approval, sent, landed, failed and abandoned. What does a target write keep?
- **Chosen:** awaiting, landed, failed, abandoned: sending and its retries are the approval's, in approvals.
- **Why:** Approvals owns sponsoring, the one send and the bundler's refusals (concepts.approvals), so `sent` and the old record-submission and clear-submission rpcs would copy its state. The write needs only what approvals cannot know: whether the transaction carried this write's own Agreements event (K6: only chain proof ends an attempt). A bundler refusal before the send leaves the write awaiting while its approval tries again.

### payroll.write-outcomes: A write's outcome: its approval's or proposal's end, checked on chain

- **Question:** How does the agreement's process learn that a write's approval or Board proposal ended, and how it ended?
- **Chosen:** approval_transitioned (opener payroll, the write as subject) or proposal_transitioned reaches the waiting instance through the outbox; the process takes the transaction from the approval's row (or the executed proposal's) and reads its indexed facts to land or fail the write.
- **Why:** P4 has the reacting concept follow the other's transition, and approvals' event names its opener and subject for exactly this. Landing on the approval alone is not enough: a transaction can run and not change the agreement (a pause beaten by another holder's), so `factsOf` must show the write's own event. A declined approval ends at once, which a deadline timer would only notice minutes later; one workflow per terminal transition would be seven workflows for one wait.

## requests (20)

### requests.acceptance-rewrites: Eleven acceptance lines rewritten as outcomes

- **Question:** Which of requests' frozen acceptance lines name a mechanism a black box cannot see, and what do they claim instead?
- **Chosen:** request.get 1, request.list 1 to 3, inbox.list 1 and inbox.get 1 (the read-time chain check: 'after its Invoice is checked', 'before any chain check', 'a verification is written'); request.cancel 1 ('from the committed row'); inbox.pay 2 ('lineage'); service.create 4 ('before restoration'); service.revise 4 ('is forwarded'); bank.match 1 ('schedules one event': the target sends the request's and the deposit match's events, one each).
- **Why:** P1: a claim names an outcome one of the five observers can check. Each rewrite keeps the old outcome and passes against the old system: the issuer still gets its request with the status the chain shows; a refused list still shows nothing; one cancel still reports one cancel. The `--after` cursor of inbox.list 2 is the printed `nextCursor`, a surface the CLI shows, so that line is kept. The old line and its test are in each rewritten line's `was`.

### requests.auto-match: Keep matching a credited deposit by its reference, as a workflow

- **Question:** The old deposit credit auto-matched a request whose reference the deposit carried. Keep it?
- **Chosen:** A workflow triggered by banking's `credit` matches the one open request its reference names, through `banking.match-deposit`.
- **Why:** It has a caller (the credit), it is the common case of a bank-paid Invoice, and banking's door already names 'its own match by reference'. As an outbox consumer it never holds up the credit, and an ambiguous reference is left to `bank match`.

### requests.budget-overrides: A preview then a write gets 2.5 s at p95

- **Question:** The flows that show a terminal preview before writing take two round trips. Do they keep the write class's 1.5 s?
- **Chosen:** p95 2,500 ms for those flows and their operations, with the person's answer to the prompt excluded.
- **Why:** The frozen claims require the preview ('At a terminal it previews the exact issued request'). A read (p95 1.2 s) and a write (1.5 s) in sequence cannot fit 1.5 s; 2.5 s is their sum less the shared session cost. Everything else takes its class default: reads read, writes write, `inbox pay` chain-read on the server (it reads funding from the chain) and chain-settle end to end, the processes background.

### requests.escrow-process: One process per request replaces the minute escrow sweep

- **Question:** The old cron read the 25 least recently read escrows every minute and relayed payouts from the faucet Safe. What watches an escrow now?
- **Chosen:** The request's own process: woken by each indexed Transfer into its escrow, it reads the balance, moves the request through `fund` once it holds the amount, and deploys and pays out with the escrow relayer's key through the service signer, recording the payout before awaiting it. A cancelled or expired request gets one last read.
- **Why:** P3 keeps only six crons and makes everything the old system swept for a process's own wait. A request is on the durable-process list (rules.durable-processes). The faucet Safe relay worked on Base Sepolia only; the service signer's escrow relayer key does one job and sends once (K4). The old part-received notice is dropped: a partial funding is not a state change, so nothing could follow it (P7).

### requests.inbox-pay-approval: An agent payer signs inbox pay at once; a person on the approval page

- **Question:** A4 gives every signature to the flow, and 'An AI with its own key signs at once'. Requests births the approval through payments. How does `inbox pay` get signed?
- **Chosen:** The flow follows the approval until it is sponsored (`approvals.signing.get`), an agent signs its digest with its own key and gives it to approvals (`approvals.signing.sign`); a person signs on the approval page.
- **Why:** Revised at assembly. This record first waited for the approval page, because requests did not depend on approvals; the review added that edge (dependencies.payers-sign-and-name-budgets), so the flow takes the same signing steps as `payment send`. Approvals still sends the operation once and refuses an unknown or another signer's approval INVALID_INPUT. The flow also passes `--on`/`--at` as the door's `pay_at`, `--resume` as its `resume` (one new approval for a lapsed Payment under the same payment id, decision payments.door-inputs), and prints the deadline the door answers as `expiresAt`.

### requests.inbox-pay-funding: Inbox pay reads funding before its transaction; `--from` names the budget by key

- **Question:** `payments.prepare-command` needs the funding the invoker read from the chain. How does `inbox pay` read it, and how does it find the budget `--from` names?
- **Chosen:** An action: `--from operations` is found by its Capxul key in budgets' table, which gives its chain id (a chain id is taken as it is); indexing's chain budgets give its office hat, which the member's Safe must wear; then the person's Safe balance or the budget's `Budgets.leftOf`, and the birth in one transaction.
- **Why:** Revised at assembly. The designed examples name the budget by its key (`--from operations`) and print its name and what it has left. This record first chose the chain id because requests did not depend on budgets; the review added that edge (dependencies.payers-sign-and-name-budgets: no cycle, budgets sits below requests), so the key is read where budgets keeps it. A chain id still works, for a budget made outside Capxul that has no key. The read is chain-read class because it calls the chain; nothing is signed or sent here.

### requests.invoicing-hat: An Organization's request writes need its Invoicing hat, bank match included

- **Question:** The old guard needed a Seat holding issue invoices for an Organization's issue, cancel, decline and services, and banking's 'management authority' for a deposit match. What does the target check?
- **Chosen:** A current Seat (organizations) for reads; for writes, the Seat's Safe wears the Organization's Invoicing hat now (its deployment names it; indexing's wearers show it). `bank match` takes the same hat.
- **Why:** v4 replaces issue invoices with the Invoicing officer hat (the designed refusals of `request issue` and `request cancel`). Matching a deposit marks a receivable paid, which is the Invoicing officer's work; the deposit's own book fact is banking's and already written at credit. Paying from the Organization's inbox is the budget's hat, which `payments.prepare-command` and the chain check, not this guard.

### requests.link-by-request-id: A checkout link names the request or service id; no separate link token

- **Question:** The old request carried a `plink_` bearer token for its link. Keep it?
- **Chosen:** The link is `/pay/<request id>` or `/buy/<service id>`; the ids are ULIDs and the public terms reveal no private data.
- **Why:** The designed example of `request issue` prints `/pay/req_01K3…`. A link shows only public terms (issuer, reference, amount, where to pay), never the note, the payer's mailbox or the document, and every write it allows (naming a payer, buying) is rate limited. One fewer column and index.

### requests.machine: A request has six stored states; births and payer binding are transitions

- **Question:** The old status admitted eight values, three never written (draft, viewed, expired) and two more derived at read time (scheduled, paid for crypto). Which states does the target store?
- **Chosen:** open, paying, paid, declined, cancelled, expired, every one written by a named transition. Birth is `issue` from no state; naming the payer at a link is `bind_payer` (open to open). Cancelled and expired are not terminal: an escrow that already held the amount still pays out and the request reads paid.
- **Why:** P7 makes every status write a transition and the event the funnel's proof; a derived state cannot be followed by notices and was the old drift between the request and its Payment. `paying` covers every 'being paid' the frozen text names (an approval waiting, a dated payment, a funded escrow paying out). Expiry is the process's own timer, so it is a real transition. The frozen designed text of `request cancel` says a funded escrow still pays out, which is why `settle` leaves cancelled and expired. The frozen note naming draft and viewed stays verbatim: those states simply never occur.

### requests.no-door: Requests has no procedures; the old door is not ported

- **Question:** The old requests door had 21 functions other concepts called. Which become procedures?
- **Chosen:** None: payments, banking and documents no longer call into requests; notices reads its tables.
- **Why:** Every old callback went the wrong way (payments and banking calling up into requests). The dependencies now point from requests down (dependencies.payments-requests, concepts.requests), a document's participants are given when it is stored, and P4 allows a procedure only for a money fact a lower concept must write with its invoker.

### requests.no-money-invoice: No fiat-denominated Invoices: a request is always a token amount

- **Question:** Keep Money Invoices (gross fiat bound to a bank receiving account, envelope-v5)?
- **Chosen:** Drop them: every request is a token amount; a credited bank deposit in the request's token can pay any request (`bank match`).
- **Why:** No frozen operation issues one: `request issue` takes an amount in an asset ('The asset defaults to USDC'). A bank deposit arrives as tokens in the owner's Safe (banking credits its net tokens), so matching it to a token request covers the frozen `bank match`. The fiat path needed a second amount shape, a second document format and banking's receiving-account read in issuing.

### requests.one-checkout-path: Link checkout is one path: name your email, then the escrow or your inbox

- **Question:** The old checkout had three paths: an outside wallet signing the V3 router's send, a registered payer's sponsored Payment from the link, and the escrow. Which survive?
- **Chosen:** The payer names their email at the link: on Capxul, the request goes to their inbox and they pay it like any inbox request; outside, they fund the invoice escrow from any wallet. A service purchase is the same, with a new request born at the link.
- **Why:** The router checkout is pre-v4 (P9) and the registered link checkout duplicated `inbox pay` with its own rpcs into payments' engine. On v4 every outside payment goes through an escrow whose payment id is fixed by its terms, so a direct payment and the payout can never both settle one request. The checkout page is the React SDK's surface (`client.checkouts.pay`), not a frozen operation; its rpcs are reached by that flow.

### requests.one-table: One requests table for requests, Invoices and service purchases

- **Question:** The old concept kept five tables: paymentRequests, invoiceCheckoutPayments, offerCheckouts, fixedOffers and fixedOfferRevisions. Which does the target keep?
- **Chosen:** `requests` (requests, Invoices and service purchases), `services` and `service-revisions`. A purchase at a service's link is a request of kind `purchase`; the Payments that tried to pay a request are payments' rows.
- **Why:** A purchase and a request are the same thing to be paid: an amount owed to a Safe, a document, a payer and one way to pay it. Two shapes meant two checkout paths, two receipt verifiers and two sets of rate limits. invoiceCheckoutPayments was written by payments into requests' table (a write-boundary finding) and only listed attempts; payments now holds every Payment with its v4 payment id, and the request keeps the one that pays it. The receipt, consumption, verification, envelope-v3/v4 and settlement-id columns served the V3 router's checkout and the read-time chain check, both gone (P9, requests.reads-write-nothing). Money Invoices are dropped by requests.no-money-invoice.

### requests.owner-tenancy: A request is its issuer's row; its payer reads it across tenants by Safe

- **Question:** A request is shared by two tenants, its issuer and its payer. Whose row is it, and how does the payer's inbox read it under row-level security?
- **Chosen:** Tenancy owner (`owner_kind`, `owner_id`: the issuer). The inbox reads by `payer_safe` through a crossTenant index, and the generated policy admits the tenant that owns the payer Safe (parties' directory) for reads.
- **Why:** Two rows would copy the status, the payment and the escrow, and could disagree: the old system's derived states were that kind of drift. A global table fails open. The issuer owns the receivable and is the only one who changes its terms; the payer's rights are to read, pay and decline, all through rpcs that check the payer Safe is theirs. The child tables carry their own tenant columns (no tenancy by join), and service revisions carry the owner's too.

### requests.paid-follows-payments: Paid follows payments' settle, unsettle and cancel, escrow payouts included

- **Question:** Which of payments' transitions does a request follow, and how does an escrow payout make it paid?
- **Chosen:** Three workflows triggered through the outbox: `settle` makes the request paid (an inbox payment, or the escrow payout payments births from the indexed PaymentSent), `unsettle` puts it back to paying, `cancel` reopens it. A lapsed Payment keeps it paying; `inbox pay --resume` retries it.
- **Why:** One settlement path (concepts.payments): the escrow's payout ends in a PaymentSent that payments settles with every term checked and books, so following it keeps the request, the Payment and the books agreeing, reorgs included. Following EscrowPaid too would be a second interpretation of the same money. A lapsed Payment kept its payment id and can still be retried, so reopening on a lapse would let a second attempt race it; a cancelled Payment never moves, so a new attempt is safe. The workflows find the request by the Payment's v4 payment id.

### requests.payer-by-safe: The payer is a Safe: on Capxul by an active Party, outside by its email Safe

- **Question:** How does a request name its payer, and when is a payer 'on Capxul'?
- **Chosen:** Resolve the payer through parties' directory: an `@handle`, or a mailbox whose person Party is active, is on Capxul; any other mailbox is outside and named by its email Safe (`safeAddressFor`), which owns the escrow. The inbox is keyed by the payer Safe. Requests creates no Party, and a bare address is refused as a payer.
- **Why:** A person's Safe is a pure function of their email, so the escrow's owner is the very Safe the payer gets when they join: their inbox then shows the request with nothing to migrate, and the escrow's refund goes to them. Naming a Party would need a parties procedure for a fact that is not a money fact for parties (the request already holds the Safe). A bare address can own no email Safe and has no inbox; the frozen text refuses it for an Invoice, and the V3 wallet checkout that paid an address-addressed basic request is pre-v4 (P9). The escrow address and its payment id are computed in the backend as `predictAddress` and `predictPaymentId` answer, so issuing calls nothing.

### requests.reads-write-nothing: Reads check nothing on chain and write nothing; they refuse when Ponder lags

- **Question:** The old current reads checked each link's receipt on chain and recorded what they found. What do the target's reads do, and when do they refuse WRONG_STATE?
- **Chosen:** A read returns the request as its machine stands; the request's process and the payment followers record what the chain proved. A read whose requests follow the chain asks `indexer.head` and refuses WRONG_STATE `verification_unavailable` when Ponder is reindexing or behind by more than the finality depth.
- **Why:** A read that writes is an rpc with two jobs, and the old checks made every list a chain read and a race with the sweep. In the target the paid state follows payments' one settlement path, so there is nothing for a read to verify. The frozen refusal stays meaningful: a status that may be stale because Ponder is behind is refused, never shown, and an unavailable inbox is a failure, never empty. The frozen `changes` lines ('It can record a checkout payment that the chain already proved') stay verbatim as frozen text; in the target the request's process records it.

### requests.service-machine: A service's status is a machine; each revision is a transition

- **Question:** The old `active` boolean had one transition and revisions sent their own event. How does the target book it?
- **Chosen:** `status` active or inactive; `create` from no state, `revise` active to active, `deactivate`; one event.
- **Why:** P7: one event per machine, from every transition. A revision changes the row (its revision) though not its status, so `revise` is a self-transition and 'each revision is reported once, in order' is the event's `revision` prop.

### requests.telemetry: The emit actions and the notify workflow are not ported

- **Question:** The old concept had three telemetry actions (emit-issued, emit-transitioned, emit-changed) and an Invoice notify workflow. Keep them?
- **Chosen:** Drop them: each machine's event leaves through the outbox (P7), and notices sends every email from `request_transitioned`.
- **Why:** Telemetry is never an operation (P7, review emit-only), and no concept but notices sends mail (dependencies.notices-top).

### requests.worlds: The devnet worlds requests' refusals are reached from

- **Question:** Which named devnet snapshots do requests' refusals need?
- **Chosen:** signed-out (no session); person-with-requests (an issuer with an open request to a person on Capxul, an open escrow Invoice to an outside email, a link request, and a paid, a declined and a cancelled one; the payer signed in too); org-with-requests (an Organization that issued and received requests, a member wearing the Invoicing hat and one wearing none, a budget whose office hat a member wears, spent to less than a received request); requests-indexer-behind (person-with-requests with Ponder held more than the finality depth behind the chain); person-with-underfunded-safe (a payer whose Safe holds less than the request to them); person-with-services (an active service at revision 2 and a deactivated one); org-with-services (an Organization's service and a member without the Invoicing hat); person-with-credited-deposit (a credited unmatched deposit, an arriving one, one matched, and an open USDC request it can pay); org-with-credited-deposit (the same for an Organization, with a member without the Invoicing hat).
- **Why:** Each world holds what its refusals need and is named after it, so an eval restores the smallest snapshot; the money worlds (a credited deposit, an underfunded Safe, a lagging indexer) stay separate because building them costs chain and Bridge-sandbox time.
